Maximal subgroups of free projection- and idempotent-generated semigroups with applications to partition monoids


Abstract
This paper investigates the maximal subgroups of a free projection-generated regular ∗ * -semigroup PG ⁡ ( P ) \PG(P) over a projection algebra P P , and their relationship to the maximal subgroups of the free idempotent-generated semigroup IG ⁡ ( E ) \IG(E) over the corresponding biordered set E = E ⁡ ( P ) E=\sfE(P) . In the first part of the paper we obtain a number of general presentations by generators and defining relations, in each case reflecting salient combinatorial/topological properties of the groups. In the second part we apply these to explicitly compute the groups when P = P ⁡ ( 𝒫 n ) P=\sfP(\mathcal{P}_{n}) and E = E ⁡ ( 𝒫 n ) E=\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n}) arise from the partition monoid 𝒫 n \mathcal{P}_{n} . Specifically, we show that the maximal subgroup of PG ⁡ ( P ⁡ ( 𝒫 n ) ) \PG(\sfP(\mathcal{P}_{n})) corresponding to a projection of rank r ≤ n − 2 r\leq n-2 is (isomorphic to) the symmetric group 𝒮 r \mathcal{S}_{r} . In IG ⁡ ( E ⁡ ( 𝒫 n ) ) \IG(\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n})) , the corresponding subgroup is the direct product ℤ × 𝒮 r \mathbb{Z}\times\mathcal{S}_{r} . The appearance of the infinite cyclic group ℤ \mathbb{Z} is explained by a connection to a certain twisted partition monoid 𝒫 n Φ \mathcal{P}^{\Phi}_{n} , which has the same biordered set as 𝒫 n \mathcal{P}_{n} . Keywords : Regular ∗ * -semigroup, projection algebra, free projection-generated regular ∗ * -semigroup, biordered set, free idempotent-generated semigroup, singular square, maximal subgroup, presentation, partition monoid, twisted partition monoid. MSC: 20M05, 20M10, 20M17, 20M20.

1
Introduction

Among the properties of elements vis-à-vis algebraic operations, idempotency is certainly one of the most ubiquitous ones.
As it refers to a single operation, semigroup theory is a natural context in which to study this property in full generality.
The most general theoretical concept for capturing the idempotent structure of a semigroup is that of a biordered set.
To every biordered set
E
one can associate a free semigroup object
\IG(E)
, which is the free-est semigroup with the biordered set
E
– intuitively,
\IG(E)
possesses only the properties that are necessarily implied by
E
.
Since it is well known that idempotents in any semigroup are in one-one correspondence with maximal subgroups, the problem of studying maximal subgroups of
\IG(E)
naturally comes to the fore.

Regular
*
-semigroups are a class of semigroups with involution, which prominently include various diagram monoids.
In such a semigroup one can identify certain special idempotents, called projections, which typically have simpler structure than generic idempotents, but are still sufficient to determine the entire biordered set.
The framework for describing the structure formed by these elements is that of a projection algebra. To every such algebra
P
one can again associate a free object
\PG(P)
, the free-est regular
*
-semigroup with projections
P
.
And again, analogous to the case of
\IG(E)
, the question of their maximal subgroups arises naturally.

The purpose of this paper is to study maximal subgroups of
\PG(P)
and compare them with those of
\IG(E)
.
This will first be done on a general level, by establishing a number of presentations (by generators and defining relations) for the maximal subgroups of
\PG(P)
, and comparing them to the known presentations for the maximal subgroups of
\IG(E)
. In particular, presentations will be obtained that reflect the fact that the former are homomorphic images of the latter. Then we will apply this theory to compute the maximal subgroups in the case where the biordered set
E
and projection algebra
P
arise from the partition monoid.
The determination of the maximal subgroups of
\IG(E)
in this case has been an important open problem for some time.

There are many examples of interactions between semigroup theory and other areas of mathematics revolving around idempotents.
Some examples worth mentioning are: the work on the idempotents of Stone–Čech compactifications
[
88
,
89
]
, idempotent-generated Banach/C*-algebras
[
44
,
43
,
82
,
5
,
60
,
59
,
83
]
,
idempotent tropical matrices
[
54
]
, the fundamental role played by idempotents in Putcha–Renner theory of reductive algebraic monoids

[
76
,
77
,
78
,
79
]
,
as well as the intrinsic importance of idempotents in the study of rings and representation theory.

Of course, the idempotents of a particular semigroup
S
need not form a subsemigroup, and, in many cases, may generate the entire
S
.
These idempotent-generated semigroups are of particular importance in our context, and possess
many features that confirm their significance.
For example, they have the universal property that every semigroup embeds into an idempotent generated semigroup
[
48
]
, and if the semigroup is countable, it can be embedded in a semigroup generated by three idempotents
[
8
]
. Also, many naturally occurring semigroups have the property that they are idempotent-generated. Important examples include semigroups of transformations
[
51
,
49
,
48
,
3
]
, matrix semigroups
[
29
,
61
]
, endomorphism monoids of independence algebras
[
32
,
36
]
, diagram semigroups
[
24
,
22
,
20
,
64
,
15
,
4
]
, and certain reductive linear algebraic monoids

[
76
,
78
]
.

The concept of the free idempotent-generated semigroup
\IG(E)
over a biordered set
E
goes
back to Clifford
[
9
]
and the seminal work of Nambooripad on the structure theory of regular semigroups
[
68
]
; see also
[
73
]
.
Biordered sets are somewhat complicated algebraic/order-theoretic structures whose axioms were given in
[
68
]
.
It was later proved in
[
19
]
that every (abstract) biordered set is in fact the biordered set of a semigroup
S
, i.e. the set

E=\sfE(S):=\{e\in S\colon e^{2}=e\}

of idempotents of
S
, with a partial multiplication that is defined by restricting the multiplication from
S
to the set of so-called basic pairs:

\mathfrak{B}=\mathfrak{B}(S):=\bigl\{(e,f)\in E\times E\colon\{ef,fe\}\cap\{e,f\}\neq\varnothing\bigr\}.

Adopting this viewpoint, the free idempotent-generated semigroup
\IG(E)
is defined by the presentation

\bigl\langle X_{E}\mid x_{e}x_{f}=x_{ef}\ ((e,f)\in\mathfrak{B})\bigr\rangle,

where
X_{E}:=\bigl\{x_{e}\colon e\in E\}
is an alphabet in one-one correspondence with
E
. If we identify
e\in E
with the equivalence class of
x_{e}
modulo the relations, then
\IG(E)
is the free-est semigroup generated by
E
, and having
E
as its biordered set
[
19
]
. In particular, there is a natural homomorphism
\IG(E)\rightarrow S
, which restricts to the identity on
E
, and whose image is the subsemigroup of
S
generated by its idempotents.

Following their discovery, it turned out that the free idempotent-generated semigroups are a rich source of interesting questions in combinatorial semigroup theory, as well as an intriguing interface to many other areas, notably combinatorial/geometric group theory and algebraic topology.
The above-mentioned link between subgroups and idempotents immediately points to the study of maximal subgroups of
\IG(E)
, which has turned out to be a key direction.
On the basis of early results
[
66
,
69
,
72
]
, it seemed plausible for a while that the maximal subgroups may turn out to always be free. This was refuted in
[
7
]
, where a concrete example was exhibited having the free abelian group of rank
2
among its maximal subgroups. The methods of
[
7
]
were topological in nature, and involved realising subgroups of
\IG(E)
as fundamental groups of natural
2
-complexes built from
E
.
Using Reidemeister–Schreier rewriting techniques, a general presentation for the maximal subgroups of
\IG(E)
was established in
[
40
]
, and was deployed in the same paper to show that in fact
any
group is the maximal subgroup of some
\IG(E)
. A number of variations and refinements followed, e.g.
[
18
,
17
,
10
,
12
,
13
,
37
]
.

In the course of this development it became clear that actually identifying the maximal subgroups of
\IG(E)
for a specific given
E
can be a tricky business.
In
[
41
]
it was shown that when
E=\sfE(\mathcal{T}_{n})
is the biordered set of the full transformation semigroup
\mathcal{T}_{n}
, the maximal subgroup of
\IG(E)
containing an idempotent of rank
r\leq n-2
is isomorphic to the symmetric group
\mathcal{S}_{r}
, coinciding exactly with the corresponding maximal subgroup of
\mathcal{T}_{n}
itself.
Analogous results were proved for the partial transformation monoid
[
11
]
and for endomorphism monoids of free
G
-acts (
G
a group)
[
37
]
.
Furthermore, it was shown in
[
16
]
that if one starts with the general linear monoid of dimension
n
over a division ring
Q
, the resulting maximal subgroup of
\IG(E)
is the general linear group of dimension
r
over
Q
,
but only for
r<n/3
.
The nature of the maximal subgroups for higher ranks is an intriguing open problem.

One important class of semigroups that have completely eluded computations of this kind are the so-called
diagram monoids
, such as the partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, Brauer monoid
\mathcal{B}_{n}
and Temperley–Lieb monoid
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
.
Diagram monoids play an important role in many fields, including algebra, topology, category theory, physics and biology
[
45
,
6
,
84
,
65
,
56
,
57
,
33
]
. Beyond their intrinsic interest, diagram monoids have important structural parallels with transformation and linear monoids. Of particular relevance to the topic of the current paper is the fact that
\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\mathcal{B}_{n}
and
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
are ‘almost idempotent-generated’ in the sense that every non-unit is a product of idempotents
[
20
,
64
,
24
]
. Thus, it is natural to ask whether maximal subgroups of these monoids
are isomorphic to those of the associated free idempotent-generated semigroups
\IG(E)
, as is the case for (linear) transformation monoids. In fact, it follows from one of our main results, Theorem
1.2
, that this is
not
the case for
\mathcal{P}_{n}
. Specifically, maximal subgroups corresponding to an idempotent of rank
r\leq n-2
are (isomorphic to) symmetric groups
\mathcal{S}_{r}
in
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, but are direct products
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathcal{S}_{r}
in
\IG(E)
.

However, diagram monoids come equipped with some additional structure, in the form of an involution, making them into regular
*
-semigroups. These are defined as semigroups with a unary operation
a\mapsto a^{*}
that satisfies the following axioms:

(a^{*})^{*}=a=aa^{*}a\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt(ab)^{*}=b^{*}a^{*}.

They were introduced in
[
71
]
, and investigated further over time, e.g. in
[
70
,
1
,
52
,
74
,
55
,
75
,
86
]
,
coming into sharper focus in recent years; see
[
21
,
26
]
.

By way of motivation, let us dwell a bit more on the findings of
[
26
]
.
Given a regular
*
-semigroup
S
, an idempotent
p\in\sfE(S)
satisfying
p=p^{*}
is called a
projection
.
The set
P=\sfP(S)
of all projections can be given the (unary) structure of a
projection algebra
; these were introduced first in

[
52
]
, and subsequently by several different authors in a variety of equivalent forms (under varying names),
a development that is explained in detail in
[
21
, Section 4]
.
So every regular
*
-semigroup gives rise to its projection algebra
\sfP(S)
. One of the main findings of
[
52
]
is that, conversely, for every projection algebra
P
there exists a regular
*
-semigroup
S
such that
\sfP(S)=P
.
An alternative proof of this is given in
[
26
]
, where it was shown that every projection algebra
P
gives rise to a free projection-generated regular
*
-semigroup

\PG(P)
. This can be viewed as the analogue of
\IG(E)
for the category of regular
*
-semigroups, as reflected in the following presentation for
\PG(P)
, established in
[
26
, Theorem 7.2]
:

\bigl\langle X_{P}\mid x_{p}^{2}=x_{p},\ (x_{p}x_{q})^{2}=x_{p}x_{q},\ x_{p}x_{q}x_{p}=x_{pqp}\ (p,q\in P)\bigr\rangle,

where
X_{P}=\{x_{p}\colon p\in P\}
.
This semigroup has projection algebra
P
, and for any other regular
*
-semigroup with
P=\sfP(S)
, the identity map on
P
extends to a homomorphism
{\PG(P)\to S}
.

So, paralleling the development for
\IG(E)
, the following natural general question arises:

•

Given a (projection-generated) regular
*
-semigroup
S
, with
P=\sfP(S)
, how is
\PG(P)
related to
S
?

Certainly,
S
is a natural homomorphic image of
\PG(P)
by construction.
Furthermore, since the projection algebras of
S
and
\PG(P)
coincide, it follows from the basic theory of regular
*
-semigroups that there are natural bijections between their
\mathscr{R}
-,
\mathscr{L}
-,
\mathscr{D}
- and
\mathscr{H}
-classes (see Subsections
2.6
–
2.8
below for more detail).
Informally speaking, this means that the only potential difference between these two semigroups is in their maximal subgroups.
The paper
[
26
]
gives examples of situations where these are different from each other, and some where they are the same. A striking example of the latter situation is provided by the Temperley–Lieb monoids
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
, which turn out to be isomorphic to their own free semigroups
\PG(\sfP(\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}))
; see
[
26
, Theorem 9.1]
.

There is a strong connection between the projection structure of a regular
*
-semigroup and its idempotent structure, which we briefly outline;
for details and references see
[
21
, Subsection 3.1]
and
[
26
, Section 6]
.
Let
S
be a regular
*
-semigroup, let
P:=\sfP(S)
be its projection algebra, and let
E:=\sfE(S)
be
its biordered set of idempotents.
Every
e\in E
is a product of two projections.
More precisely, letting

\mathscr{F}:=\big\{(p,q)\in P\times P\colon pqp=p,\ qpq=q\big\}

be the
friendliness relation
, every idempotent
e\in E
is
uniquely
expressed as a product
e=pq
, with
(p,q)\in\mathscr{F}
.
In
[
26
, Section 6]
it is proved that the projection algebra
P
uniquely determines the biordered set
E
, in the sense that for every regular
*
-semigroup with projection algebra isomorphic to
P
its biordered set is isomorphic to
E
.
Even more strongly, it is proved that there is a categorical equivalence between projection algebras and so-called regular
*
-biordered sets, as defined in
[
70
]
.
One consequence of all this is that the semigroup
\PG(P)
is a natural homomorphic image of
\IG(E)
.

So, in this paper we develop the theory of maximal subgroups of free projection-generated regular
*
-semigroups
\PG(P)
, and apply it to compare these with the corresponding subgroups of free idempotent-generated semigroups
\IG(E)
. We do this on both a general level, and also concretely for the free semigroups arising from the partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}
.
The paper thus has two distinctive parts. In the first, we obtain a number of general presentations (by generators and defining relations) for the maximal subgroups of free projection-generated regular
*
-semigroups. See Theorem
6.28
and Corollaries
6.34
and
6.40
, and also Theorem
7.18
and Corollary
7.22
; the latter two results exhibit maximal subgroups of
\PG(P)
as explicit quotients of corresponding maximal subgroups of
\IG(E)
.
Presentations such as these are important, because they provide concrete descriptions of the maximal subgroups, which can then be analysed with the tools of combinatorial group theory; they also provide a link to the topological viewpoint of
[
26
, Section 10]
.
Then, in the second part of the paper, we apply our theory to determine the maximal subgroups of the free projection- and idempotent-generated semigroups arising from the partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, our main results being the following:

Theorem 1.1
.

The maximal subgroup of
\PG(\sfP(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
corresponding to any idempotent of rank
{0\leq r\leq n-2}
is isomorphic to the symmetric group
\mathcal{S}_{r}
.

Theorem 1.2
.

The maximal subgroup of
\IG(\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
corresponding to any idempotent of rank
{0\leq r\leq n-2}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathcal{S}_{r}
.

The extra direct factor of
\mathbb{Z}
in Theorem
1.2
may appear mysterious at this stage, but we will see that it arises from a tight connection with a certain
twisted
monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\Phi}
, which has the same biordered set as
\mathcal{P}_{n}
.
The subgroups corresponding to idempotents with
r\in\{n-1,n\}
in both theorems are actually easy to determine:
when
r=n-1
they are free with ranks
that can be explicitly computed (see Remark
4.15
and Corollary
6.45
), and when
r=n
they are trivial. Thus, we know precisely all maximal subgroups in both

\PG(\sfP(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
and
\IG(\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
.

The knowledge of the maximal subgroups of
\PG(\mathcal{P}_{n})
translates into a complete description of the semigroup
\PG(\sfP(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
.
It is ‘almost equal’ to
\mathcal{P}_{n}
itself: the only differences are that the
\mathscr{D}
-class consisting of partitions of rank
n-1
is ‘inflated’ so that the maximal subgroups are copies of the appropriate free group, and that non-identity bijections are absent.
This may be interesting to compare to the case of the free projection-generated semigroup arising from the Temperley–Lieb monoid
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
, which is proved to be
exactly

\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
itself in
[
26
, Theorem 9.1]
.

The situation is different for
\IG(\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
: even though we know all its maximal subgroups, and hence its regular
\mathscr{D}
-classes, this monoid contains many non-regular elements (see Subsection
2.9
), and hence has a more complicated structure.
However, for every free idempotent-generated semigroup
\IG(E)
, there exists a distinguished (maximal) regular homomorphic image
\RIG(E)
, whose structure is again completely determined by its maximal subgroups.
Thus, Theorem
1.2
and the above discussion do entail a complete structural description of
\RIG(\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
. However, this semigroup is no longer ‘almost equal’ to
\mathcal{P}_{n}
but to its twisted counterpart
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\Phi}
.
The authors find this quite remarkable: starting from the well-known semigroup
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, and applying the well-known construction
\IG
to it, leads to another well-known semigroup
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\Phi}
, which originally arose via a very different, combinatorial-geometric, route.

Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
can be viewed as analogues of the main result from
[
40
]
, which asserts that every maximal subgroup of
\IG(\sfE(\mathcal{T}_{n}))
corresponding to an idempotent of rank
{1\leq r\leq n-2}
is the symmetric group
\mathcal{S}_{r}
.
In fact, the key idea behind the proofs of both our theorems is to establish links with maximal subgroups of

\IG(\sfE(\mathcal{T}_{n}))
.
However, several conceptual and technical difficulties present themselves in this reduction, especially in the case of
\IG(\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
.
The most notable is the fact that the ‘target group’ is no longer
\mathcal{S}_{r}
, but
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathcal{S}_{r}
. Another is that while in
\mathcal{T}_{n}
every
2\times 2
rectangular band is a singular square, this is not true in
\mathcal{P}_{n}
; see Subsection
8.3
.

The paper is organised as follows.
Section
2
covers the general preliminaries concerning semigroups, including regular
*
-semigroups, presentations, projection algebras, biordered sets, and partition monoids.
In Section
3
we set up our main tool in establishing our general presentations, a Reidemeister–Schreier type rewriting process for maximal subgroups originally developed in
[
81
]
.
In Section
4
we review the application of this process to the maximal subgroups of free idempotent-generated semigroups
\IG(E)
from
[
40
]
, and then introduce a small modification to the final presentation to better suit our needs.
In Section
5
we briefly review the set-up and notation from the computation of maximal subgroups of the free idempotent-generated semigroups
\IG(\sfE(\mathcal{T}_{n}))
arising from the full transformation monoid
\mathcal{T}_{n}

[
41
]
,
which, as mentioned above, are a key ingredient in our overall proof strategy for Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
.

Section
6
contains our first two general presentation results – Theorem
6.28
and Corollary
6.40
– and some related presentations including Corollary
6.34
.
It also contains a number of applications, among them
the proof of Theorem
1.1
in the case
r=0
.
Section
7
gives the remaining general presentations, Theorem
7.18
and Corollary
7.22
; these reflect the fact that the maximal subgroups of
\PG(P)
are natural homomorphic images of the maximal subgroups of
\IG(E)
. After proving these results, we compare the presentations of Sections
6
and
7
to each other, which involves analysing the relationships between two kinds of squares of idempotents, singular squares and linked squares.

This concludes the general, theory-building part of the paper, and the rest is devoted to
an analysis of the maximal subgroups of
\PG(\sfP(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
and
\IG(\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
.
Before the arguments for
\PG(\sfP(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
and
\IG(\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
diverge,
in Section
8
we introduce some further preliminaries
and results that are shared by the two.
After these preparations we are already able to complete the proof of Theorem
1.1
for
r\geq 1
in Section
9
.
The proof of Theorem
1.2
for
r\geq 1
is the longest and most intricate argument of the paper.
It is given in Section
10
, which contains at the beginning an outline and summary of the main ideas.
The proofs of our main results are rounded off in Section
11
, where we prove Theorem
1.2
for
r=0
.

The paper concludes in Section
12
with some remarks and open questions for future research.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
General notation

We will use
\mathbb{Z}
to denote both the set of integers and the corresponding additive group. We will also use standard notation to denote intervals of integers, e.g.
{[m,n]:=\{m,m+1,\ldots,n\}}
and
{(m,n]:=[m+1,n]}
. For an integer
n\geq 0
we will write
[n]
to denote the interval
[1,n]
.

2.2
Presentations

We will use two kinds of presentations in this paper: semigroup and group.
Let
A
be an alphabet, and denote by
A^{+}
the
free semigroup
over
A
, consisting of all non-empty (finite) words over
A
, under concatenation.
Denote by
1
the empty word, and
let
A^{\ast}:=A^{+}\cup\{1\}
denote the
free monoid
over
A
.

A
semigroup presentation
is a pair
\langle A\mid\mathcal{R}\rangle
, where
\mathcal{R}\subseteq A^{+}\times A^{+}
.
The elements of
A
are called
generators
and
the elements of
\mathcal{R}
(
defining
)
relations
; a relation
(u,v)\in\mathcal{R}
is typically displayed as an equality,
{u=v}
.
The presentation
\langle A\mid\mathcal{R}\rangle
defines the semigroup
S:=A^{+}/\rho
, where
\rho
is the congruence generated by
\mathcal{R}
.
The mapping
\pi\colon A^{+}\rightarrow S
,
w\mapsto w/\rho
is a surmorphism.
The semigroup defined by
\langle A\mid\mathcal{R}\rangle
is the free-est semigroup generated by an image of
A
, and satisfying the relations in
\mathcal{R}
.
More formally, if
T
is a semigroup, and
\phi\colon A^{+}\rightarrow T
a homomorphism such that
u\phi=v\phi
for all
(u,v)\in\mathcal{R}
, then there exists a unique homomorphism
\psi\colon S\rightarrow T
such that
\phi=\pi\psi
.

We will typically identify a word
u\in A^{+}
and the element
u/\rho
of
S=A^{+}/\rho
it represents.
Thus we will often write
u=v
to mean that
u/\rho=v/\rho
in
S
.
When there is a danger of confusion, we will emphasise that the equality holds in
S
, or we will say that
u=v
is a consequence of
\mathcal{R}
.

A
group presentation
is a pair
\langle A\mid\mathcal{R}\rangle
, where
\mathcal{R}\subseteq(A\cup A^{-1})^{\ast}\times(A\cup A^{-1})^{\ast}
, with
{A^{-1}:=\{a^{-1}\colon a\in A\}}
a new alphabet consisting of formal inverses of letters from
A
.
It defines the group
(A\cup A^{-1})^{*}/\rho
where
\rho
is the congruence generated by
\mathcal{R}\cup\big\{(aa^{-1}=a^{-1}a=1)\colon a\in A\big\}
.
It is the free-est group generated by an image of
A
that satisfies the defining relations from
\mathcal{R}
.

2.3
Green’s relations, idempotents and regularity

Green’s relations (pre-orders and equivalences) are the standard tool for working with the ideal structure of a semigroup.
Here we give the basic definitions and facts, and refer the reader to any textbook on semigroup theory, such as

[
50
]
, for a more systematic exposition.

Let
S
be a semigroup, and denote by
S^{1}
the monoid obtained by adjoining an identity element to
S
if necessary.
For
a,b\in S
, define

a\leq_{\mathscr{L}}b\ \Leftrightarrow\ S^{1}a\subseteq S^{1}b,\hskip 10.00002pta\leq_{\mathscr{R}}b\ \Leftrightarrow\ aS^{1}\subseteq bS^{1}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pta\leq_{\mathscr{J}}b\ \Leftrightarrow\ S^{1}aS^{1}\subseteq S^{1}bS^{1}.

If
\leq_{\mathscr{K}}
is any of these three pre-orders, it induces an equivalence relation
\mathscr{K}
via

a\mathrel{\mathscr{K}}b\ \Leftrightarrow\ a\leq_{\mathscr{K}}b\text{ and }b\leq_{\mathscr{K}}a.

There are two further Green’s equivalences:

\mathscr{H}:=\mathscr{L}\cap\mathscr{R}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathscr{D}:=\mathscr{L}\vee\mathscr{R},

where
\vee
denotes the join of equivalence relations.
It is well known that
\mathscr{D}=\mathscr{L}\circ\mathscr{R}=\mathscr{R}\circ\mathscr{L}
, where
\circ
denotes relational composition.
For
a\in S
, denote by
L_{a}
,
R_{a}
,
J_{a}
,
H_{a}
and
D_{a}
its
\mathscr{L}
-,
\mathscr{R}
-,
\mathscr{J}
-,
\mathscr{H}
- and
\mathscr{D}
-classes, respectively.
If
\mathscr{K}
is any of
\mathscr{L}
,
\mathscr{R}
or
\mathscr{J}
, the set
S/\mathscr{K}=\{K_{a}\colon a\in S\}
of all
\mathscr{K}
-classes of
S
has an induced partial order
\leq
, given by

K_{a}\leq K_{b}\ \Leftrightarrow\ a\leq_{\mathscr{K}}b\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $a,b\in S$.}

One of the most important basic results concerning Green’s relations is the following; for a proof, see for example
[
50
, Lemma 2.2.1]
.

Lemma 2.1

(Green’s Lemma)
.

Let
a
and
b
be
\mathscr{R}
-related elements of a semigroup
S
, so that
b=as
and
a=bt
for some
s,t\in S^{1}
. Then the maps

L_{a}\to L_{b},\ u\mapsto us\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptL_{b}\to L_{a},\ v\mapsto vt

are mutually inverse bijections. Moreover, these maps restrict to mutually inverse bijections
H_{a}\to H_{b}
and
H_{b}\to H_{a}
.

There is a left-right dual of the previous result, whose statement we omit, but throughout the article we will frequently refer to both formulations as Green’s Lemma. We note that Green’s Lemma, and many of the other forthcoming results and arguments concerning Green’s relations, can be conveniently visualised through the use of so-called
egg-box diagrams
. In such a diagram we place the elements of a
\mathscr{D}
-class in a rectangular array, with
\mathscr{R}
-related elements in the same row,
\mathscr{L}
-related elements in the same column, and
\mathscr{H}
-related elements in the same cell (intersection of a row and column). For more details, see for example
[
50
, Chapter 2]
.

An
idempotent
of a semigroup
S
is an element
e\in S
such that
e=e^{2}
. The set of all idempotents of
S
is denoted

\sfE(S):=\{e\in S\colon e^{2}=e\}.

If
e,f\in\sfE(S)
, then it is easy to check that

(2.2)

e\leq_{\mathscr{L}}f\ \Leftrightarrow\ e=ef\and e\leq_{\mathscr{R}}f\ \Leftrightarrow\ e=fe.

An element
a
of a semigroup
S
is
regular
if
a=axa
for some
x\in S
. Idempotents are obviously regular. If
a=axa
, then
a\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}ax
and
a\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}xa
, with the elements
ax
and
xa
being idempotents. Moreover, the element
y:=xax
satisfies
a=aya
and
y=yay
; the elements
a
and
y
are said to be
(
semigroup
)
inverses
of each other.
We say that
S
is a regular semigroup if every element of
S
is regular. Some basic facts include the following:

•

When
a
is regular, every element of the
\mathscr{D}
-class
D_{a}
is regular, and this is equivalent to the
\mathscr{D}
-class containing an idempotent, and in fact to every
\mathscr{R}
- and
\mathscr{L}
-class in
D_{a}
containing an idempotent.

•

If
e
is an idempotent, then the
\mathscr{H}
-class
H_{e}
is a group with identity
e
, and is a maximal subgroup of
S
.

•

If
e
and
f
are two
\mathscr{D}
-related idempotents, then the groups
H_{e}
and
H_{f}
are isomorphic.

The following well-known result is typically attributed to FitzGerald
[
30
]
or Hall
[
42
]
, although this exact formulation is not found in either paper. We give a simple (standard) proof for completeness and convenience, and since the result is of such fundamental importance to us.

Proposition 2.3

(cf.
[
30
, Lemma 1]
or
[
42
, Lemma 1]
)
.

If
a
is a regular element of a semigroup
S
, and if
a\in\langle\sfE(S)\rangle
, then there exist idempotents
e_{1},\ldots,e_{k}\in\sfE(S)
such that

a=e_{1}\cdots e_{k}\and a\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}e_{1}\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}e_{2}\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}e_{3}\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}e_{4}\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}\cdots\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}e_{k-1}\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}e_{k}\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}a.

Proof.

By assumption we have
a=f_{1}\cdots f_{n}
for some
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\in\sfE(S)
, and also
a=axa
and
x=xax
for some
x\in S
. We then define the elements

g_{i}=f_{i}\cdots f_{n}xf_{1}\cdots f_{i}\text{ for $1\leq i\leq n$}\and h_{i}=f_{i}\cdots f_{n}xf_{1}\cdots f_{i-1}\text{ for $1\leq i\leq n+1$.}

Using
xf_{1}\cdots f_{n}x=xax=x
, one can easily check that each
g_{i},h_{i}\in\sfE(S)
, and that

g_{i}h_{i}=h_{i},\hskip 20.00003pth_{i}g_{i}=g_{i},\hskip 20.00003ptg_{i}h_{i+1}=g_{i}\and h_{i+1}g_{i}=h_{i+1}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $1\leq i\leq n$,}

which says that
h_{i}\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}g_{i}\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}h_{i+1}
for all such
i
. We also have

a\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}ax=h_{1}\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}g_{1}\and a\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}xa=h_{n+1}\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}g_{n}.

A simple induction shows that
g_{1}\cdots g_{i}=(ax)^{i}f_{1}\cdots f_{i}
for each
1\leq i\leq n
, so that in particular

g_{1}\cdots g_{n}=(ax)^{n}f_{1}\cdots f_{n}=(ax)^{n}a=a.

Combining this with
g_{i}=h_{i}g_{i}
, it follows that

a=g_{1}\cdots g_{n}=g_{1}h_{2}g_{2}h_{3}g_{3}\cdots h_{n}g_{n},

which is a factorisation of the desired form.
∎

2.4
Graham–Houghton graphs

The idempotents of a regular
\mathscr{D}
-class of a semigroup lead to the following important combinatorial structure:

Definition 2.4

(
Graham–Houghton graph
)
.

Let
D
be a
\mathscr{D}
-class in a semigroup
S
, and let
R_{i}
(
{i\in I}
) and
L_{j}
(
j\in J
) be the
\mathscr{R}
-
and
\mathscr{L}
-classes of
S
contained in
D
, respectively.
The
Graham–Houghton graph

\GH(D)
of
D
is
the bipartite graph with vertices
I\sqcup J
, the disjoint union of
I
and
J
, and with
an edge connecting
i\in I
and
j\in J
if and only if the
\mathscr{H}
-class
H_{i,j}:=R_{i}\cap L_{j}
is a group.
We identify such an edge
i-j
with the unique idempotent
e_{i,j}
contained in
H_{i,j}
, which is the identity of this group.

The following observation will play a crucial role in our study. It goes back at least to
[
38
]
in the finite case; the argument of
[
38
]
works essentially unchanged in the case that the principal factor of the
\mathscr{D}
-class is completely 0-simple; see also
[
80
, Theorem 2.1]
for a slightly different formulation.

Proposition 2.5
.

If
S
is idempotent-generated, then
\GH(D)
is connected for every regular
\mathscr{D}
-class
D
of
S
.

Proof.

Keeping the notation of Definition
2.4
, it suffices to show that arbitrary vertices
i\in I
and
j\in J
are connected by a path in
\GH(D)
. To do so, fix some such
i\in I
and
j\in J
, and also fix an element
{a\in H_{i,j}=R_{i}\cap L_{j}}
.
Now write
a=e_{1}\cdots e_{k}
as in
Proposition
2.3
.
Identifying each idempotent with an edge of
\GH(D)
as explained in Definition
2.4
,
the sequence
e_{1},\ldots,e_{k}
is the desired path from
i
to
j
.
∎

2.5
Biordered sets and singular squares

Let
S
be a semigroup, and
E=\sfE(S)
its set of idempotents. A pair
(e,f)\in E\times E
is said to be
basic
if
\{ef,fe\}\cap\{e,f\}\not=\varnothing
, i.e. if at least one of the products
ef
or
fe
is equal to one of
e
or
f
. In this case
ef
and
fe
are both idempotents, but they are not necessarily both equal to one of
e
or
f
. We write

\mathfrak{B}=\mathfrak{B}(S):=\big\{(e,f)\in E\times E\colon\{ef,fe\}\cap\{e,f\}\not=\varnothing\big\}

for the set of all such basic pairs. Note that
\mathfrak{B}
consists of all pairs of idempotents that are comparable in one or both of the
\leq_{\mathscr{L}}
or
\leq_{\mathscr{R}}
orders; cf. (
2.2
). The
biordered set of
S
is the algebra with:

•

underlying set
E=\sfE(S)
, and

•

a partial binary operation, which is the restriction to
\mathfrak{B}
of the product in
S
.

Biordered sets were also defined axiomatically by Nambooripad in
[
68
]
, without reference to any containing semigroup. We do not need to give this axiomatisation, however, as it was later shown by Easdown
[
19
]
that every (abstract) biordered set arises from a semigroup in the above way.

Given a biordered set
E=\sfE(S)
, and idempotents
e,f\in E
, we define the
sandwich set

(2.6)

\operatorname{\textup{{S}}}(e,f):=\{h\in E\colon ehf=ef,\ fhe=h\}.

Note that here the products
ehf
and
ef
are taken in the semigroup
S
rather than in
E
, and these need not be idempotents.
Nonetheless,
\operatorname{\textup{{S}}}(e,f)
can be defined from the biordered set
E
alone
[
68
, p2]
.
The biordered set
E
is said to be
regular
if each sandwich set is non-empty. Equivalently,
E
is regular if and only if it is the biordered set of idempotents of some regular semigroup. But note that a regular biordered set can also be the biordered set of a non-regular semigroup.

Fix a biordered set
E=\sfE(S)
, where
S
is a semigroup. A
square of idempotents
in
E
is an array
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
, where
e,f,g,h\in E
, and where

e\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}f,\hskip 20.00003ptg\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}h,\hskip 20.00003pte\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}g\and f\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}h.

Note that
\{e,f,g,h\}
need not be a subsemigroup of
S
; specifically the ‘diagonal products’, such as
eh
, need not be idempotents. The elements
e,f,g,h
are not required to be distinct; if there are equalities among them, the square is said to be
degenerate
. Because of the above
\mathscr{R}
- and
\mathscr{L}
-relationships, we have

e=f\ \Leftrightarrow\ g=h\and e=g\ \Leftrightarrow\ f=h.

We say that the square
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
is a
left-right
(
LR
)
singular square
if there exists
u\in E
such that

(2.7)

ue=e,\hskip 20.00003ptug=g,\hskip 20.00003pteu=f\and gu=h.

The idempotent
u
is said to
(
LR-
)
singularise
the square. In this case we will also say that
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}f&e\\
h&g\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
is a
right-left
(
RL
)
singular square,
(
RL-
)
singularised by
u
. We refer to both
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
and
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}f&e\\
h&g\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
as
horizontal singular squares
, and we visually represent them as shown in Figure
1
(a) and (b).
The following facts are straightforward to check, and we will use them throughout without further reference:

•

If
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
is a square satisfying
(
2.7
) then
uf=f=fu
and
uh=h=hu
, which is to say that
f,h\leq u
in the natural partial order on
E
.

•

If
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
is a square satisfying the first two equalities of (
2.7
), then the third and fourth equalities are equivalent to each other; thus in any specific situation it is sufficient to verify only one of them.

•

If
e,g,u\in E
satisfy
e\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}g
, and
ue=e
and
ug=g
, then
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&eu\\
g&gu\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
is an LR singular square of idempotents (singularised by
u
).

Dually,
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
is an
up-down
(
UD
)
singular square
, with
(
UD
)
-singularising idempotent

u
, if

(2.8)

eu=e,\hskip 20.00003ptfu=f,\hskip 20.00003ptue=g\and uf=h.

In this case we will also say that
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}g&h\\
e&f\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
is a
down-up
(
DU
)
singular square,
(
DU-
)
singularised by
u
. These two squares are referred to as
vertical
, and are visualised in Figure
1
(c) and (d).
Statements analogous to the above three bullet points hold for these squares.

\mathrlap{e}{\phantom{f}}
\mathrlap{f}{\phantom{f}}
\mathrlap{g}{\phantom{f}}
\mathrlap{h}{\phantom{f}}
u

\mathrlap{f}{\phantom{f}}
\mathrlap{e}{\phantom{f}}
\mathrlap{h}{\phantom{f}}
\mathrlap{g}{\phantom{f}}
u

\mathrlap{e}{\phantom{f}}
\mathrlap{f}{\phantom{f}}
\mathrlap{g}{\phantom{f}}
\mathrlap{h}{\phantom{f}}
u

\mathrlap{g}{\phantom{f}}
\mathrlap{h}{\phantom{f}}
\mathrlap{e}{\phantom{f}}
\mathrlap{f}{\phantom{f}}
u

(a)

(b)

(c)

(d)

Figure 1:
Visual representations of singular squares:
(a) and (b) respectively represent the LR and RL squares
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
and
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}f&e\\
h&g\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
satisfying (
2.7
);
(c) and (d) respectively represent the UD and DU squares
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
and
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}g&h\\
e&f\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
satisfying (
2.8
). In all cases the singularising element is
u
.

2.6
Free (regular) idempotent-generated semigroups
\IG(E)
and
\RIG(E)

Fix a semigroup
S
, and its biordered set
E=\sfE(S)
. Recall from the Introduction that the
free idempotent-generated semigroup

\IG(E)
is defined by the presentation

\bigl\langle X_{E}\mid x_{e}x_{f}=x_{ef}\ ((e,f)\in\mathfrak{B})\bigr\rangle.

Here
X_{E}:=\{x_{e}\colon e\in E\}
is an abstract generating set in one-one correspondence with
E
, and

\mathfrak{B}:=\bigl\{(e,f)\in E\times E\colon\{ef,fe\}\cap\{e,f\}\neq\varnothing\bigr\}
is the set of basic pairs.

Let
\pi\colon\IG(E)\rightarrow S
be the natural homomorphism induced by
x_{e}\mapsto e
, for
e\in E
. (Here we recall that the one-letter word
x_{e}
is identified with its equivalence class modulo the relations.) The image of
\pi
is
\langle E\rangle
, the idempotent-generated subsemigroup of
S
.
The following are some basic facts that will be used extensively throughout.

(IG1)

\sfE(\IG(E))=X_{E}
, and
\pi
induces an isomorphism of biordered sets of
\IG(E)
and
S
.

(IG2)

For
e\in E
, the
\mathscr{D}
-class
D_{x_{e}}
of
\IG(E)
is mapped onto the
\mathscr{D}
-class
D_{e}
of
\langle E\rangle
under
\pi
.
Furthermore,
\pi
induces bijections between the sets of
\mathscr{R}
-,
\mathscr{L}
- and
\mathscr{H}
-classes in
D_{x_{e}}
and
the corresponding sets in
D_{e}
.

Intuitively,
(IG2)
is saying that the egg-box diagrams of the
\mathscr{D}
-classes
D_{x_{e}}
(in
\IG(E)
) and
D_{e}
(in
\langle E\rangle
) look the same, except that the maximal subgroups (and hence the
\mathscr{H}
-class sizes) may be larger in
D_{x_{e}}
. As noted in Subsection
2.3
, the
\mathscr{D}
-classes
D_{x_{e}}
and
D_{e}
(
e\in E
) are precisely the regular
\mathscr{D}
-classes of
\IG(E)
and
\langle E\rangle
, respectively.
The statement
(IG1)
follows from the main result of
[
19
]
, while
(IG2)
follows from
(IG1)
and
[
30
]
, as discussed in
[
40
, p148]
.

The semigroup
\IG(E)
need not be regular, even when
E
is a regular biordered set. But
\IG(E)
in this case does have a free-est
regular
homomorphic image still with the same biordered set of idempotents.
This is the
free regular idempotent-generated semigroup

\RIG(E)
, which is defined by the presentation

\bigl\langle X_{E}\mid x_{e}x_{f}=x_{ef}\ ((e,f)\in\mathfrak{B}),\ x_{e}x_{h}x_{f}=x_{e}x_{f}\ (e,f\in E,\ h\in\operatorname{\textup{{S}}}(e,f))\bigr\rangle.

The second family of relations involve the sandwich set
\operatorname{\textup{{S}}}(e,f)
, defined in (
2.6
).
This semigroup is regular by
[
73
, Proposition 4.9 and Corollary 5.10]
; an alternative construction using categorical machinery can be found in
[
68
]
. It is clear that there are homomorphisms
\pi_{\textsf{R}}\colon\RIG(E)\rightarrow S
,
x_{e}\mapsto e
and
\theta\colon\IG(E)\rightarrow\RIG(E)
,
x_{e}\mapsto x_{e}
, and that
\theta\pi_{\textsf{R}}=\pi
. In particular, it follows that:

(RIG1)

\sfE(\RIG(E))=X_{E}
, and
\pi_{\textsf{R}}
induces an isomorphism of biordered sets of
\RIG(E)
and
S
.

(RIG2)

For
e\in E
, the
\mathscr{D}
-class
D_{x_{e}}
of
\RIG(E)
is mapped onto the
\mathscr{D}
-class
D_{e}
in
\langle E\rangle
under
\pi_{\textsf{R}}
.
Furthermore,
\pi_{\textsf{R}}
induces bijections between the sets of
\mathscr{R}
-,
\mathscr{L}
- and
\mathscr{H}
-classes in
D_{x_{e}}
and
the corresponding sets in
D_{e}
.

By
[
7
, Theorem 3.6]
we also have:

(RIG3)

For any
e\in E
the homomorphism
\theta
induces an isomorphism between the maximal subgroups containing
x_{e}
in
\IG(E)
and
\RIG(E)
.

This time, the entire
\RIG(E)
has exactly the same Green’s structure as
\langle E\rangle
, except that the maximal subgroups of
\RIG(E)
are homomorphic preimages of those in
\langle E\rangle
.

2.7
Regular
*
-semigroups

Now we gather some basic definitions and properties concerning a regular
*
-semigroup
S
. For ease of reference we include some already encountered in the Introduction.
First recall that by definition,
S
has a unary operation
a\mapsto a^{*}
such that the following hold:

(2.9)

(a^{*})^{*}=a=aa^{*}a\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt(ab)^{*}=b^{*}a^{*}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all }a,b\in S.

Unless otherwise stated, the proofs of the following can be found in
[
26
, Subsection 2.1]
or in earlier references cited therein.

(RS1)

A
projection
is an idempotent
p
satisfying
p^{*}=p
; the set of all projections of
S
is denoted by
P=\sfP(S)
.

(RS2)

For
a\in S
the element
aa^{*}
is the unique projection
\mathscr{R}
-related to
a
, and
a^{*}a
is the unique projection
\mathscr{L}
-related to
a
. Consequently,
a\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}b\ \Leftrightarrow\ aa^{*}=bb^{*}
and
a\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}b\ \Leftrightarrow\ a^{*}a=b^{*}b
.

(RS3)

The product of two projections is an idempotent, but need not be a projection.

(RS4)

The
friendliness relation
on
P
is given by

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}

\displaystyle:=\big\{(p,q)\in P\times P\colon pqp=p,\ qpq=q\big\}

\displaystyle\phantom{:}=\big\{(p,q)\in P\times P\colon\text{$R_{p}\cap L_{q}$ is a group (with identity\penalty\ $pq$)}\big\}.

(RS5)

For any idempotent
e\in\sfE(S)
, the pair
(ee^{*},e^{*}e)
is the unique pair
(p,q)\in\mathscr{F}
such that
{e=pq}
.

(RS6)

The product of two idempotents need not be an idempotent.

(RS7)

For
a\in S
and
p\in P
we have
a^{*}pa\in P
;
in particular,
qpq\in P
for all
p,q\in P
.

(RS8)

The set
P=\sfP(S)
together with the unary operations
\theta_{p}\colon q\mapsto pqp
(one operation for each
p\in P
) is called the
projection algebra
of
S
.

(RS9)

Projection algebras can be abstractly axiomatised, and every abstract projection algebra is the projection algebra of a regular
*
-semigroup
[
52
,
55
,
26
]
.

(RS10)

Associated to
\mathscr{F}
, there is a reflexive relation
\leq_{\mathscr{F}}
on
P
defined by
p\leq_{\mathscr{F}}q\ \Leftrightarrow\ pqp=p
, and we have
{\mathscr{F}}={\leq_{\mathscr{F}}}\cap{\geq_{\mathscr{F}}}
.
In fact,
p\leq_{\mathscr{F}}q\ \Leftrightarrow\ p\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}qpq
.

(RS11)

For
p,q\in P
we have
p\leq_{\mathscr{F}}q
if and only if
pq\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}p
(see below), and dually
p\leq_{\mathscr{F}}q
if and only if
qp\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}p
. Consequently,
p\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q\ \Leftrightarrow\ p\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}pq\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}q
.

(RS12)

Every projection algebra
P
uniquely determines an associated biordered set
E=\sfE(P)
in the sense that for every regular
*
-semigroup
S
with
\sfP(S)\cong P
we have
\sfE(S)\cong E
; see
[
26
, Theorem 6.19]
.

As justification for
(RS11)
, note that
p\leq_{\mathscr{F}}q\ \Rightarrow\ p=pqp\ \Rightarrow\ p\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}pq
. Conversely, by
(RS2)
we have
p\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}pq\ \Rightarrow\ p=pq(pq)^{*}=pqp\ \Rightarrow\ p\leq_{\mathscr{F}}q
.

2.8
Free projection-generated semigroups
\PG(P)

Let
P
be a projection algebra, and let
S
be a regular
*
-semigroup such that
P=\sfP(S)
.
The
free projection-generated semigroup

\PG(P)
was constructed in
[
26
]
using categorical machinery. By
[
26
, Theorem 7.2]
, we can take
\PG(P)
to be the semigroup defined by the presentation

\bigl\langle X_{P}\mid x_{p}^{2}=x_{p},\ (x_{p}x_{q})^{2}=x_{p}x_{q},\ x_{p}x_{q}x_{p}=x_{pqp}\ (p,q\in P)\bigr\rangle,

where
X_{P}:=\{x_{p}\colon p\in P\}
, and where the product
pqp
is taken in
S
. Note that
pq
itself need not belong to
P
, but
pqp
always does; see
(RS3)
and
(RS7)
.
The following facts include some that are proved in
[
26
]
, and some simple consequences:

(PG1)

The mapping
x_{p}\mapsto p
extends to a homomorphism
\pi\colon\PG(P)\rightarrow S
.
The image of
\pi
is the (regular
*
-)subsemigroup
\langle P\rangle
of
S
generated by the projections, which coincides with the subsemigroup generated by the idempotents.
See
[
26
, Theorem 5.8]
.

(PG2)

\sfP(\PG(P))=X_{P}
, and the restriction of
\pi
to
X_{P}
is an isomorphism of projection algebras.
Again see
[
26
, Theorem 5.8]
.

(PG3)

Consequently, the homomorphism
\pi
induces bijections between the sets of
\mathscr{D}
-,
\mathscr{R}
-,
\mathscr{L}
- and
\mathscr{H}
-classes of
\PG(P)
and
\langle P\rangle
.
It also induces an isomorphism between the biordered sets of idempotents of
\PG(P)
and
\langle P\rangle
.

(PG4)

It also follows that for every projection
p\in\sfP(S)
, the homomorphism
\pi
induces a surmorphism from the maximal subgroup of
\PG(P)
containing
x_{p}
onto the maximal subgroup of
\langle P\rangle
containing
p
.

(PG5)

Letting
E=\sfE(P)
be the biordered set associated with
P
as per
(RS12)
,
the mapping
{x_{e}\mapsto x_{ee^{*}}x_{e^{*}e}}
(
e\in E
) induces a surmorphism
\theta\colon\IG(E)\rightarrow\PG(P)
.
See
[
26
, p45 and the proof of Theorem 7.10]
.

(PG6)

Again, the surmorphism
\theta
induces bijections between the sets of regular
\mathscr{D}
-,
\mathscr{R}
- and
\mathscr{L}
-classes of
\IG(E)
and (all) the
\mathscr{D}
-,
\mathscr{R}
- and
\mathscr{L}
-classes of
\PG(P)
, an isomorphism between their biordered sets of idempotents, and surmorphisms from maximal subgroups of
\IG(E)
onto the corresponding maximal subgroups of
\PG(P)
.
See
(IG2)
,
(RS12)
and
(PG2)
.

(PG7)

Analogous statements hold about the relationship between
\RIG(E)
and
\PG(P)
.
See
(RIG2)
,
(RS12)
and
(PG2)
.

2.9
Some remarks about the relationships between
S
,
\PG(P)
,
\IG(E)
and
\RIG(E)

The observations that follow are technically not needed for the subsequent development, but the reader may find them useful because they reflect the viewpoint and motivation of this paper; unless otherwise stated, each assertion follows from various combinations of items in the preceeding subsections.
Let
S
be a projection-generated regular
*
-semigroup, and let
P:=\sfP(S)
and
E:=\sfE(S)
.
Then we have a chain of natural surmorphisms

\IG(E)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\theta_{\sfI}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\RIG(E)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\theta_{\sfR}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\PG(P)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\theta_{\sfP}}}{{\longrightarrow}}S.

Results of the current paper can be used to show that none of these mappings are isomorphisms when
S=\langle\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n})\rangle
is the idempotent-generated submonoid of the partition monoid of degree
n\geq 3
(see the next subsection for definitions):

•

\theta_{\sfI}
is not an isomorphism because
\RIG(E)
is regular but
\IG(E)
is not. Indeed, it follows quickly from
[
17
, Theorem 3.6]
that any word of the form
x_{e}x_{f}
with
(ef,e)\not\in\mathscr{R}
and
(ef,f)\not\in\mathscr{L}
represents a non-regular element of
\IG(E)
. Such elements
e
and
f
are easy to find in
\mathcal{P}_{n}
.

•

\theta_{\sfR}
is not an isomorphism because maximal subgroups corresponding to projections of rank
0\leq r\leq n-2
are finite in
\PG(P)
, but infinite in
\RIG(E)
. See Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
, and recall that
\RIG(E)
and
\IG(E)
have the same subgroups up to isomorphism.

•

Finally,
\theta_{\sfP}
is not an isomorphism because
S
is finite, but
\PG(P)
is infinite. Indeed, it follows from Corollary
6.45
that in the second-top
\mathscr{D}
-class of
\PG(P)
, maximal subgroups are free of rank
\binom{n-1}{2}\geq 1
.

However, the following strong links are present (for any
S
):

•

Each of
\theta_{\sfI}
,
\theta_{\sfR}
and
\theta_{\sfP}
restricts to an isomorphism of biordered sets;
furthermore,
\theta_{\sfP}
restricts to an isomorphism of projection algebras.

•

Let
\theta
be any of
\theta_{\sfI}
,
\theta_{\sfR}
or
\theta_{\sfP}
, and let
D
be a regular
\mathscr{D}
-class
in the domain of
\theta
(which means
any

\mathscr{D}
-class in the case of
\theta_{\sfR}
or
\theta_{\sfP}
).
Then
D\theta
is a (regular)
\mathscr{D}
-class in the image of
\theta
, and
\theta
induces
bijections between the
\mathscr{R}
-,
\mathscr{L}
- and
\mathscr{H}
-classes of
D
and
D\theta
.

•

Restricted to any group
\mathscr{H}
-class,
\theta_{\sfI}
is a group isomorphism,
while
\theta_{\sfR}
and
\theta_{\sfP}
are group surmorphisms.
The latter two are not isomorphisms in general (for example when
{S=\langle\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n})\rangle}
, as above).

•

As a consequence,
\IG(E)
and
\RIG(E)
do not in general admit a regular
*
-structure that would be natural in the sense of being compatible with the above scheme of surmorphisms.

The topic of this paper is to analyse the maximal subgroups of
\IG(E)
(equivalently
\RIG(E)
),
\PG(P)
and
S
in relation to each other.

2.10
The partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}
and the full transformation monoid
\mathcal{T}_{n}

Let
X
be a set, and
X^{\prime}=\{x^{\prime}\colon x\in X\}
a disjoint copy. The
partition monoid

\mathcal{P}_{X}
consists of all set partitions of
X\cup X^{\prime}
. Such a partition
a\in\mathcal{P}_{X}
is identified with any graph on vertex set
X\cup X^{\prime}
whose connected components are the blocks of
a
; such a graph is typically drawn with vertices from
X
and
X^{\prime}
on a top and bottom row, respectively. Partitions
a,b\in\mathcal{P}_{X}
are multiplied as follows. First, let
X^{\prime\prime}=\{x^{\prime\prime}\colon x\in X\}
be another copy of
X
, and define three further graphs:

•

a^{\vee}
, the graph on vertex set
X\cup X^{\prime\prime}
obtained by changing each lower vertex
x^{\prime}
of
a
to
x^{\prime\prime}
,

•

b^{\wedge}
, the graph on vertex set
X^{\prime\prime}\cup X^{\prime}
obtained by changing each upper vertex
x
of
b
to
x^{\prime\prime}
,

•

\Gamma(a,b)
, the graph on vertex set
X\cup X^{\prime\prime}\cup X^{\prime}
whose edge set is the union of the edge sets of
a^{\vee}
and
b^{\wedge}
.

We call
\Gamma(a,b)
the
product graph
of
a
and
b
. The product
ab
is the unique element of
\mathcal{P}_{X}
such that
x,y\in X\cup X^{\prime}
belong to the same block of
ab
if and only if they are in the same connected component of
\Gamma(a,b)
. When displaying product graphs, the vertices from
X^{\prime\prime}
are shown on a middle row.
When
X=[n]=\{1,\ldots,n\}
for some integer
n\geq 0
, we write
\mathcal{P}_{n}
for
\mathcal{P}_{X}
. Unless otherwise specified, vertices are shown in the natural order,
1<\cdots<n
. An example product is given in Figure
2
, for

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=\big\{\{1,4\},\{2,3,4^{\prime},5^{\prime}\},\{5,6\},\{1^{\prime},2^{\prime},6^{\prime}\},\{3^{\prime}\}\big\},

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle=\big\{\{1,2\},\{3,4,1^{\prime}\},\{5,5^{\prime},6^{\prime}\},\{6\},\{2^{\prime},3^{\prime}\},\{4^{\prime}\}\big\},

\displaystyle\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptab

\displaystyle=\big\{\{1,4\},\{2,3,1^{\prime},5^{\prime},6^{\prime}\},\{5,6\},\{2^{\prime},3^{\prime}\},\{4^{\prime}\}\big\}.

The partition monoids
\mathcal{P}_{n}
contain many important diagram monoids as submonoids, including the Brauer, Temperley–Lieb and Motzkin. For example, the Brauer monoid
\mathcal{B}_{n}
consists of all partitions whose blocks have size
2
.
The partition monoids
\mathcal{P}_{n}
also contain (isomorphic copies of) the full transformation monoids
\mathcal{T}_{n}
, as we will soon see.

a=
b=
=ab

Figure 2:
Multiplication of partitions
a,b\in\mathcal{P}_{6}
, with the product graph
\Gamma(a,b)
shown in the middle.

For a set
X
, we call a non-empty subset
A
of
X\cup X^{\prime}
:

•

an
upper non-transversal
(or
upper block
) if
A\subseteq X
,

•

a
lower non-transversal
(or
lower block
) if
A\subseteq X^{\prime}
,

•

a
transversal
otherwise.

If
a\in\mathcal{P}_{X}
for finite
X
, we write

a=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&D_{1}&\cdots&D_{t}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)

to indicate that
a
has transversals
A_{i}\cup B_{i}^{\prime}
(for
1\leq i\leq r
), upper non-transversals
C_{i}
(for
1\leq i\leq s
), and lower non-transversals
D_{i}^{\prime}
(for
1\leq i\leq t
). For example, with
a\in\mathcal{P}_{6}
from Figure
2
, we have
a=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 2,3&1,4&5,6\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 4,5&1,2,6&3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
.
We sometimes adopt the convention of not listing singleton blocks, so for example,
b=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 3,4&5&1,2\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&5,6&2,3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
.

The partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{X}
has a natural involution
a\mapsto a^{*}
, obtained by swapping dashed and undashed vertices. For finite
X
, if
a=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&D_{1}&\cdots&D_{t}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
, then
a^{*}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&D_{1}&\cdots&D_{t}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
. Graphically, this corresponds to a reflection in the horizontal axis. This involution gives
\mathcal{P}_{X}
the structure of a regular
*
-semigroup, as it is easy to check that the identities (
2.9
) hold.
The above-mentioned submonoids (Brauer, Temperley–Lieb, etc) are closed under
∗
, and are hence themselves regular
*
-semigroups.

The
domain
of a partition
a=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&D_{1}&\cdots&D_{t}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
is the set

{\dom(a):=A_{1}\cup\cdots\cup A_{r}}
.
The
kernel
of
a
is the equivalence relation
\ker(a)
on
[n]
with classes

A_{1},\ldots,A_{r},C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}
.
The
codomain

\codom(a)
and
cokernel

\coker(a)
are defined dually, using the sets
B_{1},\ldots,B_{r},D_{1},\ldots,D_{t}
.
The
rank
of
a
is the number of transversals,
\rank(a)=r
.
Green’s equivalences on
\mathcal{P}_{n}
can be described using these parameters as follows; for proofs see
[
85
,
31
]
.

Lemma 2.10
.

For
a,b\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, we have

(i)

a\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}b\ \Leftrightarrow\ \dom(a)=\dom(b)
and
\ker(a)=\ker(b)
;

(ii)

a\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}b\ \Leftrightarrow\ \codom(a)=\codom(b)
and
\coker(a)=\coker(b)
;

(iii)

a\mathrel{\mathscr{D}}b\ \Leftrightarrow\ \rank(a)=\rank(b)
;

(iv)

every group
\mathscr{H}
-class of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
consisting of partitions of rank
r
is isomorphic to the symmetric group
\mathcal{S}_{r}
.

The full transformation monoid
\mathcal{T}_{n}
can be naturally viewed as the submonoid of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
consisting of all partitions with full domain and trivial cokernel.
For such a partition

a=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil b_{1}&\cdots&b_{r}&c_{1}&\cdots&c_{n-r}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
we will often use the more familiar transformation notation

a=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}\\
b_{1}&\cdots&b_{r}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
It is well known that
\mathcal{T}_{n}
is a regular semigroup, and so it inherits Green’s
\mathscr{L}
and
\mathscr{R}
equivalences from
\mathcal{P}_{n}
by
[
50
, Proposition 2.4.2]
. The same happens to be true of the
\mathscr{D}
relation, and one obtains the familiar description:

Lemma 2.11
.

For
a,b\in\mathcal{T}_{n}
, we have

(i)

a\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}b\ \Leftrightarrow\ \ker(a)=\ker(b)
;

(ii)

a\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}b\ \Leftrightarrow\ \codom(a)=\codom(b)
;

(iii)

a\mathrel{\mathscr{D}}b\ \Leftrightarrow\ \rank(a)=\rank(b)
;

(iv)

every group
\mathscr{H}
-class of
\mathcal{T}_{n}
consisting of transformations of rank
r
is isomorphic to the symmetric group
\mathcal{S}_{r}
.

Note that
\mathcal{T}_{n}
is not closed under the involution
∗
. Rather,
\mathcal{T}_{n}^{*}=\{a^{*}\colon a\in\mathcal{T}_{n}\}
is an anti-isomorphic copy of
\mathcal{T}_{n}
.

3
Reidemeister–Schreier rewriting for maximal subgroups

In this section we outline a general method for finding presentations for maximal subgroups of semigroups given by presentations, which was introduced in
[
81
]
, and has many similarities with the Reidemeister–Schreier rewriting theory for groups
[
63
, Section II.4]
.
This method was used in
[
40
]
to establish a presentation for maximal subgroups of free idempotent-generated semigroups
\IG(E)
, of which we will present a brief review in Section
4
.
We will deploy it twice more,
in Sections
6
and
7
, to find two different presentations for maximal subgroups of free projection-generated semigroups
\PG(P)
.

Let
S
be the semigroup defined by a (semigroup) presentation
\langle X\mid\mathcal{R}\rangle
, and let

e_{0}\in X^{+}
be a word representing an idempotent in
S
.
We want to exhibit a presentation for the maximal subgroup
G
of
S
containing
e_{0}
, i.e. for the group
\mathscr{H}
-class
H_{e_{0}}
.

Let
D=D_{e_{0}}
be the
\mathscr{D}
-class of
e_{0}
. Index the
\mathscr{R}
- and
\mathscr{L}
-classes in
D
as
R_{i}
(
i\in I
), and
L_{j}
(
j\in J
). For
i\in I
and
j\in J
, let
H_{i,j}:=R_{i}\cap L_{j}
– these are the
\mathscr{H}
-classes in
D
. Let
i_{0}\in I
and
{j_{0}\in J}
be such that
e_{0}\in H_{i_{0},j_{0}}
; thus
G=H_{i_{0},j_{0}}
. Let

(3.1)

K:=\big\{(i,j)\in I\times J\colon H_{i,j}\text{ is a group}\big\}.

For
(i,j)\in K
, let
e_{i,j}\in X^{+}
be any word that represents the identity of
H_{i,j}
, with
e_{i_{0},j_{0}}=e_{0}
.

By Green’s lemma there is an action of
S
on the set
(R_{i_{0}}/\mathscr{H})\cup\{0\}=\{H_{i_{0},j}\colon j\in J\}\cup\{0\}
, consisting of the
\mathscr{H}
-classes contained in
R_{i_{0}}
plus an extra symbol
0
, given by right multiplication.
This is equivalent to an action of
S
on the set
J\cup\{0\}
, determined by

(3.2)

j\cdot s=\begin{cases}k&\text{if }H_{i_{0},j}s=H_{i_{0},k}\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

A set of
Schreier representatives
for this action is a prefix-closed set
\{r_{j}\colon j\in J\}
of words from
X^{*}
, such that

j_{0}\cdot r_{j}=j
for every
j\in J
.
It is routine to see that such a set must exist. One way is to use the action to define a directed graph on vertex set
J
, with edges labelled by the generators (from
X
), observe that the graph must be strongly connected (i.e. there is a directed path from any vertex to any other vertex) by the definition of the
\mathscr{R}
equivalence, and then take a directed spanning tree rooted at
j_{0}
. By this we mean a subgraph whose underlying undirected graph is a tree, such that there exists a (unique) directed path from
j_{0}
to every other vertex. The edge-labels on these paths determine the representatives.

Also, by the definition of
\mathscr{R}
, and Green’s Lemma, there exist words
\{r_{j}^{\prime}\colon j\in J\}
from
X^{*}
such that

ar_{j}r_{j}^{\prime}=a
for all
a\in H_{i_{0},j_{0}}
and
br_{j}^{\prime}r_{j}=b
for all
b\in H_{i_{0},j}
.
We note that nothing further is required from the words
r_{j}^{\prime}
; e.g. they need not be prefix-closed.

By
[
81
, Theorem 2.7]
, the maximal subgroup
G

is generated by the set

\{e_{0}r_{j}xr_{j\cdot x}^{\prime}\colon j\in J,\ x\in X,\ j\cdot x\neq 0\}.

So we introduce a new alphabet

B:=\bigl\{[j,x]\colon j\in J,\ x\in X,\ j\cdot x\neq 0\bigr\},

where
[j,x]
is a letter/symbol, which we think of as representing the generator
e_{0}r_{j}xr_{j\cdot x}^{\prime}
. The presentation for
G
will be written in terms of the alphabet
B
.

The defining relations for
G
are obtained by
rewriting
in some sense the defining relations
\mathcal{R}
of
S
. To make this formal, we need the

rewriting mapping
:

\phi\colon\{(j,w)\in J\times X^{\ast}\colon j\cdot w\neq 0\}\rightarrow B^{\ast},

defined inductively by

(3.3)

\phi(j,1)=1\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\phi(j,xw)=[j,x]\phi(j\cdot x,w)\text{ for }w\in X^{*}\text{ with }j\cdot xw\not=0.

Theorem 3.4

(see
[
81
, Theorem 2.9]
)
.

With the notation as above, the maximal subgroup
G
is defined by the group presentation

(3.5)

\displaystyle\bigl\langle B\mid\>

\displaystyle[j,x]=1

\displaystyle(j\in J,\ x\in X,\ r_{j}x=r_{j\cdot x}),

(3.6)

\displaystyle\phi(j,u)=\phi(j,v)

\displaystyle(j\in J,\ (u=v)\in\mathcal{R},\ j\cdot u\neq 0)\bigr\rangle.

4
Maximal subgroups of
\IG(E)

4.1
Applying the general rewriting

The above theory was applied to the case of maximal subgroups of
\IG(E)
in
[
40
]
.
Here we briefly review the steps, with a two-fold aim: 1) we want to make a small modification to the way the final presentation is written out; and 2) we will want to re-run this process in the special case where
E
comes from a regular
*
-semigroup in Section
7
, which will yield a comparison between maximal subgroups of
\IG(E)
and
\PG(P)
.

Let
E
be a biordered set. As previously discussed, we may take
E
to be embedded in a semigroup
S
so that
E=\sfE(S)
. There are two main reasons for adopting this viewpoint,
as opposed to working with
E
as an abstract biordered set, as specified by the axioms. Firstly, while this axiomatics is natural and reflects the fundamental properties of idempotent structure, it is certainly more cumbersome than working within a single semigroup. And, secondly, our main results in the second half of the paper will follow this pattern: we will take a concrete semigroup, specifically the partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, and investigate the maximal subgroups of the free idempotent- and projection-generated semigroups arising from its idempotents or projections.

In what follows, we will further assume that
S
is idempotent-generated. If it were not, we would replace it with the subsemigroup
\langle E\rangle
generated by the idempotents.

Recall the presentational definition of
\IG(E)
given in Subsection
2.6
:

(4.1)

\IG(E):=\bigl\langle X_{E}\mid x_{e}x_{f}=x_{ef}\ ((e,f)\in\mathfrak{B})\bigr\rangle,

where
X_{E}:=\{x_{e}\colon e\in E\}
, and

\mathfrak{B}:=\bigl\{(e,f)\in E\times E\colon\{ef,fe\}\cap\{e,f\}\neq\varnothing\bigr\}
.
Since we assumed that
S
is idempotent-generated, it follows that the natural homomorphism
\pi\colon\IG(E)\rightarrow S
,
x_{e}\mapsto e
, is surjective.
Suppose now that we are given a fixed idempotent
e_{0}\in E
, and we want to obtain a presentation for the maximal subgroup
G^{\sfE}=H_{x_{e_{0}}}
of
\IG(E)
containing
x_{e_{0}}
.
We apply the method from the previous section, so we carry the notation over, including the sets

D=D_{e_{0}},\hskip 20.00003ptR_{i}\ (i\in I),\hskip 20.00003ptL_{j}\ (j\in J),\hskip 20.00003ptH_{i,j}=R_{i}\cap L_{j}\and K.

We will also write
E_{D}=E\cap D=\{e_{i,j}\colon(i,j)\in K\}
for the set of idempotents in
D
.

However, there are two important technical subtleties that the reader will need to keep in mind, as we now record:

Remark 4.2
.

A key aspect in Section
3

is the action of the semigroup under consideration on the
\mathscr{H}
-classes within a fixed
\mathscr{R}
-class.
So, for us here, that would be the action of
\IG(E)
on the
\mathscr{H}
-classes in the
\mathscr{R}
-class of
x_{e_{0}}
in
\IG(E)
.
However, the natural surmorphism
\pi
induces a bijection between the
\mathscr{L}
-classes in
the
\mathscr{D}
-class of
x_{e_{0}}
in
\IG(E)
and those in the
\mathscr{D}
-class of
e_{0}
in
S
by
(IG2)
.
In turn, this induces a bijection between the
\mathscr{H}
-classes in the
\mathscr{R}
-class of
x_{e_{0}}
in
\IG(E)
and the
\mathscr{H}
-classes in the
\mathscr{R}
-class of
e_{0}
in
S
.
Furthermore,
each
x_{e}
acts on the former in the same way as
e
acts on the latter.
A complete formal proof that these two actions are the same is given in
[
17
, Lemma 2.8]
.
Since it is
S
that is
a priori
given to us, we will throughout use its actions in place of those of
\IG(E)
.
This point of view was also adopted and explained in
[
40
]
.

Remark 4.3
.

A priori
, a set of Schreier representatives would be a set of words over the generating set
X_{E}
of
\IG(E)
.
However, recalling that we are assuming that
S
is idempotent-generated, it follows by Proposition
2.3
that

D\subseteq\langle E_{D}\rangle
. Repeating the reasoning for the existence of Schreier representatives from Section
3
, but using only edges labelled by elements of
E_{D}
, yields a set of Schreier representatives over the alphabet
\{x_{e}\colon e\in E_{D}\}
.

The presentation for
G^{\sfE}
given in Theorem
3.4
has generators

B:=\bigl\{[j,x_{e}]\colon j\in J,\ e\in E,\ j\cdot e\neq 0\bigr\}

and defining relations

(4.4)

\displaystyle[j,x_{e}]=1

\displaystyle(j\in J,\ e\in E,\ r_{j}x_{e}=r_{j\cdot e}),

(4.5)

\displaystyle[j,x_{e}][j\cdot e,x_{f}]=[j,x_{ef}]

\displaystyle(j\in J,\ (e,f)\in\mathfrak{B},\ j\cdot ef\neq 0).

This presentation is transformed in
[
40
]
in the following way.
For every
i\in I
pick any
\textup{{j}}(i)\in J
such that
(i,\textup{{j}}(i))\in K
; this exists because
D
is regular.
Introduce new generating symbols via

(4.6)

\llbracket i,j\rrbracket:=[\textup{{j}}(i),x_{e_{i,j}}]\text{ for }(i,j)\in K,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and set}\hskip 10.00002ptA:=\bigl\{\llbracket i,j\rrbracket\colon(i,j)\in K\bigr\}.

The original generators can be eliminated as follows.
Consider an arbitrary
[t,x_{e}]\in B
.
Let
j:=t\cdot e
.
By
[
40
, Lemma 3.3]
, there exists
i\in I
such that
(i,t),(i,j)\in K
and
es=s
for all
s\in R_{i}
,
and then
[
40
, Lemma 3.1]
yields

(4.7)

[t,x_{e}]=\llbracket i,t\rrbracket^{-1}\llbracket i,j\rrbracket.

Performing this elimination
yields the following presentation for the maximal subgroup
G^{\sfE}
of
\IG(E)
containing
x_{e_{0}}
(see
[
40
, Theorem 5]
):

(4.8)

\displaystyle\bigl\langle A\mid\

\displaystyle\llbracket i,j\rrbracket=\llbracket i,l\rrbracket

\displaystyle((i,j),(i,l)\in K,\ r_{j}x_{e_{i,l}}=r_{l}),

(4.9)

\displaystyle\llbracket i,\textup{{j}}(i)\rrbracket=1

\displaystyle(i\in I),

(4.10)

\displaystyle\llbracket i,j\rrbracket^{-1}\llbracket i,l\rrbracket=\llbracket k,j\rrbracket^{-1}\llbracket k,l\rrbracket

\displaystyle(\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e_{i,j}&e_{i,l}\\
e_{k,j}&e_{k,l}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\in\mathfrak{S})\bigr\rangle,

where
\mathfrak{S}
stands for the set of all singular squares of idempotents of
D
, as defined in Subsection
2.5
. Recall that if
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e_{i,j}&e_{i,l}\\
e_{k,j}&e_{k,l}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
is an LR singular square, then
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e_{i,l}&e_{i,j}\\
e_{k,l}&e_{k,j}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
is an RL-square; clearly these squares give equivalent relations of the form (
4.10
). Similarly, matching pairs of UD- and DU-squares give equivalent relations. A relation (
4.10
) coming from a degenerate square is trivially true; i.e. it holds in the free group over
A
.

Before we move on, we make a brief historical remark about the presentation (
4.8
)–(
4.10
).
A topological version of the above presentation, in the case of
\RIG(E)
(
E
regular), is given in
[
7
]
and attributed to Nambooripad.
In the same paper it is shown that the maximal subgroups of
\RIG(E)
and
\IG(E)
corresponding to the same idempotent are isomorphic, with the consequence that (
4.8
)–(
4.10
) is indeed a presentation for
G^{\sfE}
when
E
is regular. The general case, where
E
need not be regular, was established in
[
40
]
.

4.2
Spanning trees

For our purposes here, we want to recast the presentation (
4.8
)–(
4.10
) in a slightly different way, using the language of spanning trees. We keep the notation of Subsection
4.1
.

Since the
\mathscr{D}
-class
D
is regular, its Graham–Houghton graph
\GH(D)
is connected by Proposition
2.5
. We can therefore fix a spanning tree
T
of
\GH(D)
. We will identify
T
with its set of edges,
i.e. we consider it as a subset of
E_{D}=E\cap D
.
We claim that
T
yields a natural set of Schreier representatives
r_{j}
and elements
\textup{{j}}(i)
.
Indeed, for
j\in J
, consider the unique path from
j_{0}
to
j
using edges from
T
:

(4.11)

j_{0}-i_{1}-j_{1}-\cdots-i_{k-1}-j_{k-1}-i_{k}-j_{k}=j.

This means that
(i_{1},j_{0}),(i_{1},j_{1}),\ldots,(i_{k},j_{k-1}),(i_{k},j_{k})\in K
. The path (
4.11
) is empty if
j=j_{0}
.
We now define

r_{j}:=x_{e_{i_{1},j_{1}}}x_{e_{i_{2},j_{2}}}\cdots x_{e_{i_{k},j_{k}}},

i.e. the product of generators corresponding to every other edge in the path, starting with
i_{1}-j_{1}
. Note that
r_{j_{0}}
is necessarily the empty word.
We claim that
\{r_{j}\colon j\in J\}
is a set of Schreier representatives.
Note that we have
H_{i_{0},j_{t-1}}e_{i_{t},j_{t}}=H_{i_{0},j_{t}}
because
H_{i_{t},j_{t-1}}
is a group.
Therefore
j_{t-1}\cdot x_{e_{i_{t},j_{t}}}=j_{t}
, and so

j_{0}\cdot r_{j}=j_{0}\cdot x_{e_{i_{1},j_{1}}}x_{e_{i_{2},j_{2}}}\cdots x_{e_{i_{k},j_{k}}}=j_{1}x_{e_{i_{2},j_{2}}}\cdots x_{e_{i_{k},j_{k}}}=\cdots=j_{k}=j.

To show that the
r_{j}
are prefix closed consider an arbitrary prefix
x_{e_{i_{1},j_{1}}}\cdots x_{e_{i_{t},j_{t}}}
of
r_{j}
.
The sequence
j_{0}-i_{1}-j_{1}-\cdots-i_{t}-j_{t}
is a subpath of (
4.11
), and hence the unique path from
j_{0}
to
j_{t}
using edges from
T
.
Hence
x_{e_{i_{1},j_{1}}}\cdots x_{e_{i_{t},j_{t}}}=r_{j_{t}}
, completing the proof that
\{r_{j}\colon j\in J\}
is a system of Schreier representatives.
Furthermore, in reference to Remark
4.3
, we note that, by construction, each
r_{j}
is a word over the alphabet
\{x_{e}\colon e\in E_{D}\}
.

Moving on to selecting the elements
\textup{{j}}(i)
, suppose
i\in I
is arbitrary, and let

j_{0}-i_{1}-j_{1}-\cdots-i_{k-1}-j_{k-1}-i_{k}=i

be the unique path from
j_{0}
to
i
using edges from
T
.
Then define
\textup{{j}}(i):=j_{k-1}
. It is clear that
(i,\textup{{j}}(i))\in K
.

Consider the presentation (
4.8
)–(
4.10
) arising from this choice of the
r_{j}
and
\textup{{j}}(i)
.
In particular, consider an arbitrary relation (
4.8
).
From the condition
r_{j}x_{e_{i,l}}=r_{l}
(and keeping in mind (
4.11
)), it follows that the final two edges in the unique
T
-path from
j_{0}
to
l
are
e_{i,j}
and
e_{i,l}
. Therefore
j=\textup{{j}}(i)
, and so, in the presence of relations (
4.9
), relations (
4.8
) can be simplified to

(4.12)

\llbracket i,l\rrbracket=1\hskip 10.00002pt\text{whenever }x_{e_{i,l}}\text{ is the last letter of }r_{l}.

Now notice that for every
e_{i,j}
in
T
one of the following two conditions holds:

•

j
immediately precedes
i
in the unique
T
-path from
j_{0}
to
i
, and so
j=\textup{{j}}(i)
; or

•

i
immediately precedes
j
in the unique
T
-path from
j_{0}
to
j
, in which case
e_{i,j}
is the final letter of
r_{j}
.

Therefore the relations (
4.9
) and (
4.12
) can together be written as

\llbracket i,j\rrbracket=1\hskip 10.00002pt(e_{i,j}\in T).

Renaming the letters
\llbracket i,j\rrbracket
into
a_{e_{i,j}}
, so that
A=\{a_{e}\colon e\in E_{D}\}
, we have proved the following:

Theorem 4.13

(cf.
[
40
, Theorem 5]
)
.

With the notation as above, and
T
a spanning tree for the Graham–Houghton graph
\GH(D)
, the maximal subgroup of
\IG(E)
containing
x_{e_{0}}
is defined by the presentation

\displaystyle\bigl\langle A\mid\>

\displaystyle a_{e}=1

\displaystyle(e\in T),

∎

\displaystyle a_{e}^{-1}a_{f}=a_{g}^{-1}a_{h}

\displaystyle(\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\in\mathfrak{S})\bigr\rangle.

Motivated by this, we introduce some notation for future use:

Definition 4.14
.

For a subset
F\subseteq E_{D}
, let:

•

\mathcal{R}_{1}(F):=\big\{(a_{e}=1)\colon e\in F\big\}
;

•

\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}:=\big\{(a_{e}^{-1}a_{f}=a_{g}^{-1}a_{h})\colon\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\in\mathfrak{S}\big\}
;

•

\presn(F):=\big\langle A\mid\mathcal{R}_{1}(F),\ \mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}\big\rangle
.

With this notation, Theorem
4.13
asserts that
G^{\sfE}
, the maximal subgroup of
\IG(E)
containing
x_{e_{0}}
, is defined by
\presn(T)
,
where
T
is any spanning tree of the Graham–Houghton graph
\GH(D)
.
One can arrive at the same result by using the fact that
G^{\sfE}
is the fundamental group of the

Graham–Houghton complex
of
D

[
7
, Section 3]
. However, in what follows we will deploy
variants of the above argument in situations where such a topological interpretation is not so readily available.

Remark 4.15
.

When there are no singular squares,
the Graham–Houghton complex is simply a graph, and its fundamental group is free of rank

|E_{D}\setminus T|
, which is equal to
|E_{D}|-|I|-|J|+1
when
E_{D}
is finite.
This is also explained in
[
7
]
after Theorem 4.1, and
can be seen as an immediate consequence of Theorem
4.13
.
Furthermore, if
S
is a regular
*
-semigroup, we have
|I|=|J|=|P_{D}|
, the number of projections in
D
, and hence the rank is
|E_{D}|-2|P_{D}|+1
.
When
E=\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n})
and
D
is the
\mathscr{D}
-class of partitions of rank
n-1
, there are no singular squares
in
D
because the identity is the only idempotent
\mathscr{J}
-above
D
.
In this case we have
|P_{D}|=n+\binom{n}{2}
, and
|E_{D}|=n+5\binom{n}{2}
; see
[
24
]
.
Hence, the maximal subgroups in
\IG(E)
corresponding to idempotents of rank
n-1
are free
of rank
|E_{D}|-2|P_{D}|+1=\frac{(n-1)(3n-2)}{2}
.

5
Maximal subgroups in
\IG(\sfE(\mathcal{T}_{n}))

When we come to determining the maximal subgroups of the free projection-generated regular
*
-semigroup

\PG(\sfP(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
and the free idempotent-generated semigroup
\IG(\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
arising from the partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
the key idea will be to make a series of reductions towards the maximal subgroups of the free idempotent-generated semigroup
\IG(E(\mathcal{T}_{n}))
arising from the full transformation monoid
\mathcal{T}_{n}
.
These latter maximal subgroups have been determined in
[
41
]
. Specifically, the maximal subgroup containing an idempotent of rank
r\leq n-2
is isomorphic to the symmetric group
\mathcal{S}_{r}
.
The starting presentation in
[
41
]
arises from a specific choice of Schreier representatives and elements
\textup{{j}}(i)
. For our purposes here, we need a presentation that arises from a spanning tree. It is important, however, that the two presentations are
equivalent
, in the sense that they have the same generators, and the two sets of defining relations imply each other; this means that any consequence of the relations from
[
41
]
are also consequences of ours.

Now, in the previous subsection we have seen that every spanning tree gives rise to a natural choice of Schreier representatives and elements
\textup{{j}}(i)
. Unfortunately, the Schreier representatives and the
\textup{{j}}(i)
from
[
41
]
do not arise in such a way. Nonetheless, we are able to identify a spanning tree that works, with a little bit of extra justification.

Let us briefly review the set-up from
[
41
]
; for full details see Section 2 in that paper.
Let
1\leq r\leq n-2
, and take
e_{0}:=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}1&\cdots&r&r+1&\cdots&n\\
1&\cdots&r&r&\cdots&r\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
, a fixed idempotent of rank
r
in
\mathcal{T}_{n}
.
Denote by
D(n,r)
the
\mathscr{D}
-class of
e_{0}
in
\mathcal{T}_{n}
; it consists of all mappings of rank
r
.
Based on Lemma
2.11
, we can
index the
\mathscr{R}
-classes in
D(n,r)
by the set
I
of all partitions of
[n]=\{1,\ldots,n\}
into
r
blocks,
and the
\mathscr{L}
-classes by the set
J
of all subsets of
[n]
of size
r
.
For
C\in J
and
V\in I
we write
C\perp V
if
C
is a cross-section of
V
.
This is precisely the necessary and sufficient condition for the existence of an idempotent transformation with kernel
V
and image
C
, which we denote by
e_{V,C}
; in other words,
K=\bigl\{(V,C)\in I\times J\colon C\perp V\bigr\}
. If
(V,C)\in K
, and if
V=\{V_{1},\ldots,V_{r}\}
and
C=\{c_{1},\ldots,c_{r}\}
with each
c_{i}\in V_{i}
, then
e_{V,C}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}V_{1}&\cdots&V_{r}\\
c_{1}&\cdots&c_{r}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

For every partition
V=\{V_{1},\ldots,V_{r}\}
in
I
, define

\textup{{C}}(V):=\{\min(V_{1}),\ldots,\min(V_{r})\}\in J.

This is the lexicographically smallest
C\in J
with
C\perp V
.
The sets
\textup{{C}}(V)
will serve as the
\mathscr{R}
-class representatives, which were denoted by
\textup{{j}}(i)
in the previous two sections.

Analogously, for every set
C=\{c_{1}<\cdots<c_{r}\}
in
J
, define

\textup{{V}}(C):=\bigl\{[1,c_{1}],(c_{1},c_{2}],\ldots,(c_{r-2},c_{r-1}],(c_{r-1},n]\bigr\}\in I.

This is the lexicographically smallest
V\in I
with
C\perp V
. Note that partitions from
I
are ordered lexicographically as follows. For
{V=\{V_{1}<_{\textup{lex}}\cdots<_{\textup{lex}}V_{r}\}}
and
W=\{W_{1}<_{\textup{lex}}\cdots<_{\textup{lex}}W_{r}\}
, we have
V<_{\textup{lex}}W
if there exists
1\leq k\leq r
such that
V_{i}=W_{i}
for
1\leq i<k
and
V_{k}<_{\textup{lex}}W_{k}
.

In the presentation established in Subsection
4.1
, relations (
4.9
) are precisely
\llbracket V,\textup{{C}}(V)\rrbracket=1

for
V\in I
, while the relations (
4.8
) take the form
\llbracket V,C\rrbracket=\llbracket V,D\rrbracket
for some
V\in I
and some
{C,D\in J}
.
Which combinations are taken depends on the actual choice of Schreier representatives, but we do not need this specific information. Key for us is to note that, by the construction in
[
41
]
,
V
is a
convex
partition in each of those relations, i.e. a partition in which all the blocks are intervals.
In other words, there exist convex partitions
V^{(1)},\ldots,V^{(t)}
in
I
and sets
C_{1},\ldots,C_{t},D_{1},\ldots,D_{t}
in
J
so that the maximal subgroup
G^{\sfE}
is defined by the presentation

(5.1)

\displaystyle\bigl\langle A\mid\>

\displaystyle\llbracket V^{(i)},C_{i}\rrbracket=\llbracket V^{(i)},D_{i}\rrbracket

\displaystyle(i=1,\ldots,t),

(5.2)

\displaystyle\llbracket V,\textup{{C}}(V)\rrbracket=1

\displaystyle(V\in I),

(5.3)

\displaystyle\llbracket V,C\rrbracket^{-1}\llbracket V,D\rrbracket=\llbracket W,C\rrbracket^{-1}\llbracket W,D\rrbracket

\displaystyle(\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e_{V,C}&e_{V,D}\\
e_{W,C}&e_{W,D}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\in\mathfrak{S})\bigr\rangle.

Proposition 5.4

(cf.
[
41
, Main Theorem]
)
.

The set

T_{\textup{lex}}=T_{\textup{lex}}(n,r):=\big\{e_{V,\textup{{C}}(V)}\colon V\in I\big\}\cup\big\{e_{\textup{{V}}(C),C}\colon C\in J\big\}.

is a spanning tree for the Graham–Houghton graph of
D(n,r)
in
\mathcal{T}_{n}
.
The resulting presentation
\presn(T_{\textup{lex}})=\langle A\mid\mathcal{R}_{1}(T_{\textup{lex}}),\ \mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}\rangle
is equivalent to the presentation (
5.1
)–(
5.3
), and defines the symmetric group
\mathcal{S}_{r}
.

Proof.

Observe that
e_{0}
belongs to both sets forming
T_{\textup{lex}}
, so that
|T_{\textup{lex}}|\leq|I|+|J|-1
. Hence to show that
T_{\textup{lex}}
is a spanning tree, it is sufficient to show that it is connected.
