Note that for an arbitrary
C=\{c_{1}<\cdots<c_{r}\}\in J
we have

\textup{{C}}(\textup{{V}}(C))=\{1,c_{1}+1,c_{2}+1,\ldots,c_{r-1}+1\}.

Thus
\textup{{C}}(\textup{{V}}(C))\leq_{\textup{lex}}C
, with equality holding if and only if
C=[r]
.
In
T_{\textup{lex}}
we have a path
C-\textup{{V}}(C)-\textup{{C}}(\textup{{V}}(C))
.
An inductive argument now shows that every
C\in J
is connected to
[r]
by a path in
T_{\textup{lex}}
.
Also, any
V\in I
is connected to
\textup{{C}}(V)\in J
by an edge in
T_{\textup{lex}}
, and so again there is a path from
V
to
[r]
. Hence
T_{\textup{lex}}
is indeed connected, and so a tree.

Now consider the presentation (
5.1
)–(
5.3
).
By
[
41
, Lemma 4.2]
, the relations (
5.1
)–(
5.3
) imply
all the relations
\llbracket V,C\rrbracket=1
where
V
is a convex partition and
C\perp V
.
In particular, they imply
\llbracket\textup{{V}}(C),C\rrbracket=1
for all
C\in J
, and we add these relations
to the presentation. Noting that these relations together with (
5.2
) are precisely the relations
\mathcal{R}_{1}(T_{\textup{lex}})
, we now have the presentation

\langle A\mid\eqref{eq:GRpres1},\ \mathcal{R}_{1}(T_{\textup{lex}}),\ \mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}\rangle
for
G^{\sfE}
.

The proof of the proposition will be complete if we can show that
(
5.1
) is a consequence of
\mathcal{R}_{1}(T_{\textup{lex}})\cup\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}
. In fact we prove the following stronger statement, which is the analogue of
[
41
, Lemma 4.2]
for the new presentation
\presn(T_{\textup{lex}})
:
for every convex partition
V\in I
and every
C\in J
with
C\perp V
, the relation
\llbracket V,C\rrbracket=1
is a consequence of the relations

\mathcal{R}_{1}(T_{\textup{lex}})\cup\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}
.

The proof is inductive with respect to the lexicographic ordering on the set of pairs under consideration, where both the set of all convex partitions, and the set
J
are taken to be themselves ordered lexicographically.
First note that if
V=\bigl\{\{1\},\ldots,\{r-1\},[r,n]\bigr\}
then we must have
C=\{1,\ldots,r-1,q\}
for some
r\leq q\leq n
; in this case,
V=\textup{{V}}(C)
, and the relation
\llbracket V,C\rrbracket=1
is included in
\mathcal{R}_{1}(T_{\textup{lex}})
.
This includes the case where
C=[r]
.
Now consider any pair
(V,C)
where
C\perp V
,
V\neq\bigl\{\{1\},\ldots,\{r-1\},[r,n]\bigr\}
and
C\neq[r]
.

We know that
\textup{{C}}(V)\leq_{\textup{lex}}C
and
\textup{{V}}(C)\leq_{\textup{lex}}V
.
If either of these is an equality, the relation
\llbracket C,V\rrbracket=1
is in
\mathcal{R}_{1}(T_{\textup{lex}})
, and we are done.
So we may suppose that
D:=\textup{{C}}(V)<_{\textup{lex}}C
and
W:=\textup{{V}}(C)<_{\textup{lex}}V
.

We claim that
D\perp W
.
If
C=\{c_{1}<\cdots<c_{r}\}
and
V=\bigl\{[v_{1},w_{1}]<\cdots<[v_{r},w_{r}]\bigr\}
then

D=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{r}\}
and
W=\bigl\{[1,c_{1}],(c_{1},c_{2}],\ldots,(c_{r-2},c_{r-1}],(c_{r-1},n]\bigr\}
.
From
C\perp V
we have
v_{i}\leq c_{i}\leq w_{i}
for all
i
.
Therefore
c_{i-1}\leq w_{i-1}<v_{i}\leq c_{i}
for all
i\geq 2
. Additionally,

1=v_{1}\leq c_{1}
, and so indeed
D\perp W
, as claimed.

We therefore have a square of idempotents
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e_{V,C}&e_{V,D}\\
e_{W,C}&e_{W,D}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
.
One can verify that this is in fact a rectangular band, by computing the diagonal products,
e.g.
e_{V,C}e_{W,D}=e_{V,D}
. By
[
41
, Remark 2.8]
every rectangular band in
\mathcal{T}_{n}
is a singular square, so
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e_{V,C}&e_{V,D}\\
e_{W,C}&e_{W,D}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\in\mathfrak{S}
.
Thus,

\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}
contains the relation
\llbracket V,C\rrbracket^{-1}\llbracket V,D\rrbracket=\llbracket W,C\rrbracket^{-1}\llbracket W,D\rrbracket
.
From
D<_{\textup{lex}}C
and
W<_{\textup{lex}}V
it follows by induction that
\llbracket V,D\rrbracket=\llbracket W,C\rrbracket=\llbracket W,D\rrbracket=1
are consequences
of
\mathcal{R}_{1}(T_{\textup{lex}})\cup\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}
. Therefore
\llbracket V,C\rrbracket=1
is also a consequence
of
\mathcal{R}_{1}(T_{\textup{lex}})\cup\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}
, completing the proof.
∎

Remark 5.5
.

In Figure
3
we picture the Graham–Houghton graph
\GH(D(4,2))
, and the spanning tree
T_{\textup{lex}}(4,2)
.
The vertices from
I
and
J
are shown on the upper and lower rows, respectively, and are ordered lexicographically, from left to right. Partitions are indicated by an obvious notation, so for example
(1|234)
represents
\big\{\{1\},\{2,3,4\}\big\}
.
The idempotents
e_{V,\textup{{C}}(V)}

(V\in I
) and
e_{\textup{{V}}(C),C}
(
C\in J
) are indicated by red and blue edges, respectively. As noted at the start of the above proof, the idempotent
e_{0}
is the unique edge coloured both red and blue.

(1|234)
(1|234)
(12|34)
(123|4)
(124|3)
(13|24)
(134|2)
(14|23)

Figure 3:
The Graham–Houghton graph
\GH(D(4,2))
, with the edges of the spanning tree
T_{\textup{lex}}(4,2)
shown in colour. See Remark
5.5
for more details.

6
First family of presentations for maximal subgroups of
\PG(P)

In this section and the next we obtain several general presentations for maximal subgroups of the free projection-generated semigroup
\PG(P)
associated to a projection algebra
P
. The presentations in each section have their own ‘flavour’, and are derived from two different presentations for
\PG(P)
itself, by applying the Reidemeister–Schreier rewriting method described in Section
3
. Here we start with the presentation in terms of the generating set
P
, and in the next section we use the generating set
E=\sfE(P)
.

6.1
Presentations via linked diamonds

Throughout this section,
P
denotes a fixed projection algebra, and without loss of generality we take
P=\sfP(S)
for some projection-generated regular
*
-semigroup
S
.
We start by recalling the standard presentation for
\PG(P)
stated in Subsection
2.8
. It has generators
X_{P}=\{x_{p}\colon p\in P\}
, and defining relations

(6.1)

\displaystyle x_{p}^{2}=x_{p}

\displaystyle(p\in P),

(6.2)

\displaystyle(x_{p}x_{q})^{2}=x_{p}x_{q}

\displaystyle(p,q\in P),

(6.3)

\displaystyle x_{p}x_{q}x_{p}=x_{pqp}

\displaystyle(p,q\in P).

By
(RS2)
, every
\mathscr{D}
-class of a regular
*
-semigroup contains projections. Since maximal subgroups contained in a single
\mathscr{D}
-class are all isomorphic, every maximal subgroup is isomorphic to a maximal subgroup containing a projection.
By
(PG2)
, the projections of
\PG(P)
are precisely (the equivalence-classes of) the generators
x_{p}
, for
p\in P
. Thus, we will fix an arbitrary projection
p_{0}\in P
, and seek a presentation for the maximal subgroup
G^{\textsf{P}}
containing
x_{p_{0}}

(which is of course precisely the
\mathscr{H}
-class of
x_{p_{0}}
in
\PG(P)
).

Following the methodology of Section
3
, we need to index
the
\mathscr{R}
- and
\mathscr{L}
-classes of the
\mathscr{D}
-class of
p_{0}
and find a system of Schreier representatives.
Analogous to the discussion in Subsection
4.1
, but this time based on
(PG3)
, we can make these choices in
S
instead of
\PG(P)
.

So, given
p_{0}\in P
, let
D=D_{p_{0}}
be
its
\mathscr{D}
-class.
Let
P_{D}:=P\cap D
.
Then
\{R_{p}\colon p\in P_{D}\}
and
\{L_{p}\colon p\in P_{D}\}
are precisely the
\mathscr{R}
- and
\mathscr{L}
-classes contained in
\mathscr{D}
by
(RS2)
; note here that
R_{p}
and
L_{p}
simply denote the
\mathscr{R}
- and
\mathscr{L}
-classes of the projection
p\in P_{D}
. Thus, we will be using
P_{D}
as a natural and convenient index set for both
\mathscr{R}
- and
\mathscr{L}
-classes.
Thus, in the notation of Section
3
, we are taking
I=J=P_{D}
.
For
p,q\in P_{D}
, let
H_{p,q}:=R_{p}\cap L_{q}
.
Note that, by
(RS4)
, we have

K=\big\{(i,j)\in I\times J\colon H_{i,j}\text{ is a group}\big\}=\big\{(p,q)\in P_{D}\times P_{D}\colon pq\in E_{D}\big\}=\mathscr{F}_{D},

the restriction of the friendliness relation
\mathscr{F}
to
D
.

From the above, the
\mathscr{H}
-classes in
R_{p_{0}}
are
\{H_{p_{0},p}\colon p\in P_{D}\}
.
By
(RS2)
and
(RS7)
, the action (
3.2
) of
S
on
P_{D}\cup\{0\}
induced by the action of
S
on these
\mathscr{H}
-classes is given by

(6.4)

p\cdot s=\begin{cases}s^{*}ps&\text{if }ps\in R_{p}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

When
s=q\in P
is a projection, using
(RS11)
, this action becomes

(6.5)

p\cdot q=\begin{cases}qpq&\text{if }p\leq_{\mathscr{F}}q\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

We are now going to establish a set of Schreier representatives, which we additionally require to consist of words over the alphabet
\{x_{p}\colon p\in P_{D}\}
.
A routine modification of the argument in Remark
4.3
shows that such a set exists.
However, we are going to be a bit more specific, and adapt the construction from Subsection
4.2
.
We begin by noting that there is a close connection between the Graham–Houghton graph
\GH(D)
, and the relation
\mathscr{F}_{D}
considered as a (symmetric) digraph; these were called ‘projection graphs’ in
[
24
]
.
Specifically, it follows from
(RS11)
that
(p,q)\in\mathscr{F}_{D}
if and only if the idempotent
pq
(and also
qp
) is an edge of
\GH(D)
.
Since
S
is projection-generated, Proposition
2.5
tells us that
\GH(D)
is connected, and hence it follows that
\mathscr{F}_{D}
is strongly connected.
Let
T
be a directed spanning tree of
\mathscr{F}_{D}
, rooted at
p_{0}
.

Consider an arbitrary
q\in P_{D}
. Let
p_{0}\rightarrow p_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow p_{k-1}\rightarrow p_{k}=q
be the unique path from
p_{0}
to
q
via edges from
T
.
Define
r_{q}:=x_{p_{1}}\cdots x_{p_{k}}
. It is clear that
r_{q}
is a word over the alphabet
\{x_{p}\colon p\in P_{D}\}
, and that the set
\{r_{p}\colon p\in P_{D}\}
is prefix-closed.
Since
(p_{i},p_{i+1})\in\mathscr{F}
for each
0\leq i<k
, it follows that
p_{i+1}p_{i}p_{i+1}=p_{i+1}
for each
i
. Thus, using (
6.5
), we have

p_{0}\cdot r_{q}=p_{0}\cdot x_{p_{1}}\cdots x_{p_{k}}=(p_{k}\cdots p_{1})p_{0}(p_{1}\cdots p_{k})=p_{k}=q.

We conclude that
\{r_{p}\colon p\in P_{D}\}
is a set of Schreier representatives as required. For the words
\{r_{p}^{\prime}\colon p\in P_{D}\}
, we can take
r_{p}^{\prime}=r_{p}^{*}x_{p_{0}}
. Indeed, since
x_{p_{0}}r_{p}\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}x_{p_{0}}
, and since
\PG(P)
is a regular
*
-semigroup, it follows from
(RS2)
that
x_{p_{0}}=x_{p_{0}}r_{p}(x_{p_{0}}r_{p})^{*}=x_{p_{0}}r_{p}r_{p}^{*}x_{p_{0}}=x_{p_{0}}r_{p}r_{p}^{\prime}
.

Theorem
3.4
now yields a presentation for
G^{\textsf{P}}
, the maximal subgroup of
\PG(P)
containing
x_{p_{0}}
. The generators are

B=\bigl\{[t,p]\colon t\in P_{D},\ p\in P,\ t\cdot p\neq 0\bigr\}=\bigl\{[t,p]\colon t\in P_{D},\ p\in P,\ t\leq_{\mathscr{F}}p\bigr\},

where (
6.5
) is used for the second equality.
Actually, in the notation of Theorem
3.4
, the generators from
B
were denoted
[t,x_{p}]
, but we have abbreviated these to
[t,p]
in order to avoid subsequent calculations occurring in subscripts.
Keeping in mind the above choice of words
r_{p}^{\prime}
, the generator
[t,p]
stands for the element

(6.6)

[t,p]=x_{p_{0}}r_{t}x_{p}r_{t\cdot p}^{\prime}=x_{p_{0}}r_{t}x_{p}r_{ptp}^{*}x_{p_{0}}.

Recalling the definition (
3.3
) of the rewriting mapping
\phi
,
the general presentation (
3.5
)–(
3.6
) for
G^{\textsf{P}}
, applied to the presentation

(
6.1
)–(
6.3
)
for
\PG(P)
, becomes:

(6.7)

\displaystyle[t,p]=1

\displaystyle(t,p\in P_{D},\ r_{t}x_{p}=r_{t\cdot p}),

(6.8)

\displaystyle[t,p][ptp,p]=[t,p]

\displaystyle(t\in P_{D},\ p\in P,\ t\leq_{\mathscr{F}}p),

(6.9)

\displaystyle[t,p][ptp,q][qptpq,p][pqptpqp,q]=[t,p][ptp,q]

\displaystyle(t\in P_{D},\ p,q\in P,\ t\leq_{\mathscr{F}}p,\ ptp\leq_{\mathscr{F}}q),

(6.10)

\displaystyle[t,p][ptp,q][qptpq,p]=[t,pqp]

\displaystyle(t\in P_{D},\ p,q\in P,\ t\leq_{\mathscr{F}}p,\ ptp\leq_{\mathscr{F}}q).

We are now going to transform this presentation into a form analogous to Theorem
4.13
. First, with regards to (
6.7
), we note that

t\cdot p=ptp
by (
6.5
). Furthermore, it follows from the construction of the Schreier representatives
r_{q}
that

r_{t}x_{p}=r_{ptp}
holds if and only if the unique
T
-path from
p_{0}
to
p(=ptp)
visits
t
just before
p
. In turn, this is the case if and only if
tp
is an edge in
T
.
Keeping this in mind, and performing the obvious cancellations in (
6.8
) and (
6.9
), the relations (
6.7
)–(
6.10
) become:

(6.11)

\displaystyle[t,p]=1

\displaystyle(t,p\in P_{D},\ \ tp\in T),

(6.12)

\displaystyle[ptp,p]=1

\displaystyle(t\in P_{D},\ p\in P,\ t\leq_{\mathscr{F}}p),

(6.13)

\displaystyle[qptpq,p][pqptpqp,q]=1

\displaystyle(t\in P_{D},\ p,q\in P,\ t\leq_{\mathscr{F}}p,\ ptp\leq_{\mathscr{F}}q),

(6.14)

\displaystyle[t,p][ptp,q][qptpq,p]=[t,pqp]

\displaystyle(t\in P_{D},\ p,q\in P,\ t\leq_{\mathscr{F}}p,\ ptp\leq_{\mathscr{F}}q).

We note that relations (
6.12
) contain among them

(6.15)

[p,p]=1\hskip 10.00002pt(p\in P_{D}).

Now consider an arbitrary generator
[t,p]\in B
, so
t\in P_{D}
,
p\in P
and
t\leq_{\mathscr{F}}p
.
Define
{q:=ptp\in P_{D}}
.
Trivially,
ptp\leq_{\mathscr{F}}q
, and hence we have a relation from (
6.14
), which (after some simplification of terms such as
pqp=pptpp=ptp
) reads

(6.16)

[t,p][ptp,ptp][ptp,p]=[t,ptp].

The generators
[ptp,ptp]
and
[ptp,p]
equal
1
as a consequence of
(
6.12
) and (
6.15
), so the relation (
6.16
) becomes

(6.17)

[t,p]=[t,ptp].

Also note that
t\leq_{\mathscr{F}}p
implies
(t,ptp)\in\mathscr{F}_{D}
(by
(RS10)
).
Thus, (
6.17
) enables us to eliminate all the generators from
B
of the form
[t,p]
, except those where

(t,p)\in\mathscr{F}_{D}
. Transforming
(
6.11
)–(
6.14
)
in this way (and remembering that
pq
is an idempotent for
p,q\in P
), yields the following presentation:

(6.18)

\displaystyle[t,p]=1

\displaystyle(t,p\in P_{D},\ tp\in T),

(6.19)

\displaystyle[ptp,ptp]=1

\displaystyle(t\in P_{D},\ p\in P,\ t\leq_{\mathscr{F}}p),

(6.20)

\displaystyle[qptpq,pqptpqp][pqptpqp,qptpq]=1

\displaystyle(t\in P_{D},\ p,q\in P,\ t\leq_{\mathscr{F}}p,\ ptp\leq_{\mathscr{F}}q),

(6.21)

\displaystyle[t,ptp][ptp,qptpq][qptpq,pqptpqp]=[t,pqptpqp]

\displaystyle(t\in P_{D},\ p,q\in P,\ t\leq_{\mathscr{F}}p,\ ptp\leq_{\mathscr{F}}q).

Now note that the relations (
6.19
) coincide precisely with the relations
[p,p]=1
(
p\in P_{D}
).
Also, each relation (
6.20
) has the form
[u,v]=[v,u]^{-1}
with
{(u,v)\in\mathscr{F}_{D}}
.
Conversely, if
{(t,p)\in\mathscr{F}_{D}}
, then letting
q=t
in (
6.20
) we obtain
[t,p]=[p,t]^{-1}
.
Thus we arrive at the following presentation:

(6.22)

\displaystyle[t,p]=1

\displaystyle(t,p\in P_{D},\ tp\in T),

(6.23)

\displaystyle[p,p]=1

\displaystyle(p\in P_{D}),

(6.24)

\displaystyle[p,q]=[q,p]^{-1}

\displaystyle((p,q)\in\mathscr{F}_{D}),

(6.25)

\displaystyle[t,ptp][ptp,qptpq][qptpq,pqptpqp]=[t,pqptpqp]

\displaystyle(t\in P_{D},\ p,q\in P,\ t\leq_{\mathscr{F}}p,\ ptp\leq_{\mathscr{F}}q).

We now turn our attention to the final family of relations (
6.25
).
A straightforward manipulation, including one application of (
6.24
), lets us write it in the following form:

(6.26)

[t,ptp]^{-1}[t,pqptpqp]=[qptpq,ptp]^{-1}[qptpq,pqptpqp]\hskip 10.00002pt(t\in P_{D},\ p,q\in P,\ t\leq_{\mathscr{F}}p,\ ptp\leq_{\mathscr{F}}q).

Let us say that a quadruple
(s,u;v,w)
of elements of
P_{D}
is a
linked diamond
if

•

{(s,v),(s,w),(u,v),(u,w)\in\mathscr{F}_{D}}
, and

•

there exists
p\in P

such
that
psp=v
and
pup=w
.

In this case we say that the diamond is
p
-linked
, and we note that
p
generally does not belong to
P_{D}
, but rather comes from a
\mathscr{D}
-class higher up in the
\leq_{\mathscr{J}}
order.
We now press on towards the main result of this subsection, but following it there will be a brief discussion of the name choice and visualisation for linked diamonds in Remark
6.33
.

Denote by
\mathfrak{L}_{D}
the set of all linked diamonds with entries from
P_{D}
.
Note that in (
6.26
), the quadruple
(t,qptpq;ptp,pqptpqp)
is
p
-linked.
In other words, all relations in (
6.26
) are contained in

(6.27)

[s,v]^{-1}[s,w]=[u,v]^{-1}[u,w]\hskip 10.00002pt((s,u;v,w)\in\mathfrak{L}_{D}).

Now consider an arbitrary
p
-linked diamond
(s,u;v,w)\in\mathfrak{L}_{D}
.
Let
t:=s
and
q:=u
. Since
sp
is an idempotent and
s\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}v
, we have

tpt=sps=s(psp)s=svs=s=t
, i.e.
t\leq_{\mathscr{F}}p
.
Also,
ptp=psp=v\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}u=q
, so certainly
ptp\leq_{\mathscr{F}}q
.
Hence, we have a relation (
6.26
) for these values of
t
,
p
and
q
,
and a straightforward calculation shows that it is precisely the relation (
6.27
) for the original values of
s
,
u
,
v
and
w
.
Therefore, relations (
6.27
) coincide with (
6.26
), and hence are equivalent to
(
6.25
).
If we introduce a new, more ‘letter-like’, notation
a_{p,q}
for the generating symbol
[p,q]
, and re-index mildly, we obtain the following:

Theorem 6.28
.

Let
P
be a projection algebra, let
S
be any projection-generated regular
*
-semigroup with
P=\sfP(S)
, and let
p_{0}\in P
be arbitrary.
Denote by
D
the
\mathscr{D}
-class of
S
containing
p_{0}
, and let
T
be any
p_{0}
-rooted directed spanning tree for
\mathscr{F}_{D}
.
With the rest of the notation as above, the maximal subgroup of
\PG(P)
containing
x_{p_{0}}
has a presentation
with generators
A=\{a_{p,q}\colon(p,q)\in\mathscr{F}_{D}\}
and relations

(6.29)

\displaystyle a_{p,q}=1

\displaystyle(p,q\in P_{D},\ pq\in T),

(6.30)

\displaystyle a_{p,p}=1

\displaystyle(p\in P_{D}),

(6.31)

\displaystyle a_{p,q}=a_{q,p}^{-1}

\displaystyle((p,q)\in\mathscr{F}_{D}),

(6.32)

\displaystyle a_{s,v}^{-1}a_{s,w}=a_{u,v}^{-1}a_{u,w}

\displaystyle((s,u;v,w)\in\mathfrak{L}_{D}).

Remark 6.33
.

A
p
-linked diamond
(s,u;v,w)
can be visualised as in the left-hand diagram of Figure
4
. In the diagram, the solid lines indicate
\mathscr{F}
-relationships, and the dotted arrows indicate the action of ‘conjugation’ by
p
.
The dashed line between
v
and
w
indicates that we additionally have
v\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}w
. This was not part of the defining properties of the linked diamond, but it quickly follows. Indeed, using the assumed
\mathscr{F}
-relationships and
pwp=w
, we have

vwv=pspwpsp=pswsp=psp=v\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and similarly}\hskip 10.00002ptwvw=w.

On the other hand,
s
and
u
might not be
\mathscr{F}
-related. It is also worth noting that in the presence of (
6.31
), the relation (
6.32
) can be rewritten as

a_{s,v}a_{v,u}a_{u,w}a_{w,s}=1.

Intuitively, one can think of this as equating the path
s\to v\to u\to w\to s
around the boundary of the diamond to the empty path. This in turn links back to the topological interpretation of
\PG(P)
from
[
26
, Section 10]
as the fundamental groupoid of a certain
2
-complex built from
\mathscr{F}
.

v
w
s
u
psp={}
{}=pup
p
p
v
w
v
w
s
u
p
p
p
p

Figure 4:
Left: a
p
-linked diamond
(s,u;v,w)
. Right: the corresponding
p
-linked triangles
(w,s,v)
and
(v,u,w)
. See Remarks
6.33
and
6.41
for more details.

6.2
Linked squares and linked pairs

The generators
a_{p,q}
(
(p,q)\in\mathscr{F}_{D}
) in the presentation we have just established are in one-one correspondence with the idempotents in
D
by
(RS5)
.
Thus, one may view the relations (
6.32
) as having the same form as the singular square relations for
\IG(E)

that we saw in Section
4
: they express equality of quotients of generators corresponding to four idempotents arranged in a ‘square’ (and forming a rectangular band).
Which squares are taken, however, is different in the two situations, although some strong links will be established in Section
7
.

Let us be more specific:
consider an arbitrary
p
-linked diamond
(s,u;v,w)\in\mathfrak{L}_{D}
. Then

\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr):=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}sv&sw\\
uv&uw\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)

is a square of idempotents, and it is easy to see that they form a rectangular band.
Additionally,
pee^{*}p=psp=v=e^{*}e
and
phh^{*}p=pup=w=h^{*}h
. If we call such a square
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
a (
p
-)
linked square
(
of idempotents
), and if we let
a_{e}:=a_{ee^{*},e^{*}e}
for
e\in E_{D}(=E\cap D)
, then the relations (
6.32
) become:

a_{e}^{-1}a_{f}=a_{g}^{-1}a_{h}\hskip 10.00002pt(\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\text{ is a linked square of idempotents in $D$}).

With the above identification
a_{e}=a_{ee^{*},e^{*}e}
, note that
a_{p,q}=a_{pq}
for
(p,q)\in\mathscr{F}_{D}
. Transforming the remaining relations (
6.29
)–(
6.31
) in the presentation from Theorem
6.28
we then obtain:

Corollary 6.34
.

Let
P
be a projection algebra, let
S
be any projection-generated regular
*
-semigroup with
P=\sfP(S)
, and let
p_{0}\in P
be arbitrary.
Denote by
D
the
\mathscr{D}
-class of
S
containing
p_{0}
, and let
T
be any
p_{0}
-rooted directed spanning tree for
\mathscr{F}_{D}
.
With the rest of the notation as above, the maximal subgroup of
\PG(P)
containing
x_{p_{0}}
has a presentation
with generators
A=\{a_{e}\colon e\in E_{D}\}
and relations

(6.35)

\displaystyle a_{e}=1

\displaystyle(e\in T\cup P_{D}),

(6.36)

\displaystyle a_{e}=a_{e^{*}}^{-1}

\displaystyle(e\in E_{D}),

(6.37)

\displaystyle a_{e}^{-1}a_{f}=a_{g}^{-1}a_{h}

\displaystyle(\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\text{ is a linked square of idempotents in $D$}).

Remark 6.38
.

So a
2\times 2
array of idempotents
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
is
p
-linked if

(6.39)

pee^{*}p=e^{*}e\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptphh^{*}p=h^{*}h.

Note that we have similar conditions concerning the other idempotents in the square, since
e\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}f\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}h\ \Rightarrow\ ff^{*}=ee^{*}
and
f^{*}f=h^{*}h
, and similarly
g^{*}g=e^{*}e
and
gg^{*}=hh^{*}
. Thus, if we define the projections

s=ee^{*}=ff^{*},\hskip 10.00002ptu=gg^{*}=hh^{*},\hskip 10.00002ptv=e^{*}e=g^{*}g\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptw=f^{*}f=h^{*}h,

then condition (
6.39
) says that
psp=v
and
pup=w
, which is one of the conditions for a linked diamond of projections. The other condition involving
\mathscr{F}
-relationships comes from the very fact that the idempotents
e
,
f
,
g
and
h
exist in the first place; see
(RS4)
. These properties also imply that the square array
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}sv&sw\\
uv&uw\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
is a rectangular band, as follows from checking a diagonal product, e.g.
eh=sv\;\!\;\!uw=spsp\;\!\;\!upup=sp\;\!\;\!up=s\;\!\;\!pup=sw=f
.

We can visualise the
p
-linked square
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
in its egg-box as in Figure
5
. In the diagram,
\mathscr{R}
- and
\mathscr{L}
-classes are indexed by projections, so for example
e,f\in R_{s}
and
e,g\in L_{v}
, and we have
e=sv
. As in Figure
4
, dotted arrows indicate the action of conjugation by
p
.

v
w
s
u
psp
pup

=

=

\mathrlap{e}{\phantom{f}}
\mathrlap{f}{\phantom{f}}
\mathrlap{g}{\phantom{f}}
\mathrlap{h}{\phantom{f}}
p
p

Figure 5:
A
p
-linked square
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}sv&sw\\
uv&uw\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
, corresponding to the
p
-linked diamond
(s,u;v,w)
. See Remark
6.38
for more details.

Another equivalent form of the relation (
6.32
) is obtained by recalling from
[
26
]
the notion of a
p
-linked pair of projections.
It is easy to see that if
(s,u;v,w)
is a
p
-linked diamond, then
(s,u)
is a
p
-linked pair, meaning that
s=spups
and
u=upspu
. And conversely a
p
-linked pair
(s,u)
leads to the
p
-linked diamond
(s,u;psp,pup)
. Hence we can write
(
6.32
) as:

a_{s,psp}^{-1}a_{s,pup}=a_{u,psp}^{-1}a_{u,pup}\hskip 10.00002pt(s,u\in P_{D},\ (s,u)\text{ is a $p$-linked pair}).

6.3
Linked triangles

Our final variation identifies a subset of (
6.32
) that implies the rest.
Let us a call a triple
(s,u,w)
of projections from
P_{D}
a
p
-linked triangle
if
(s,u;s,w)
is a
p
-linked diamond, i.e. if
(s,w),(u,s),(u,w)\in\mathscr{F}_{D}
,
psp=s
and
pup=w
.
Let
\mathfrak{T}_{D}
denote the set of all linked triangles in
P_{D}
.
Now consider an arbitrary
p
-linked diamond
(s,u;v,w)
. It is easy to check that the defining properties of this diamond (and the consequence
v\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}w
from Remark
6.33
) imply that
(w,s,v),(v,u,w)\in\mathfrak{T}_{D}
(both are
p
-linked).
The relations (
6.32
) arising from the diamonds
(w,s;w,v)
and
(v,u;v,w)
are

a_{w,w}^{-1}a_{w,v}=a_{s,w}^{-1}a_{s,v}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pta_{v,v}^{-1}a_{v,w}=a_{u,v}^{-1}a_{u,w}.

These two relations, together with (
6.30
) and (
6.31
), imply

a_{s,v}^{-1}a_{s,w}=a_{w,v}^{-1}=a_{v,w}=a_{u,v}^{-1}a_{u,w},

which is the relation (
6.32
) arising from the original diamond
(s,u;v,w)
. Thus, we can replace the entire set of relations (
6.32
) by the subset corresponding to triangles
(s,u,w)\in\mathfrak{T}_{D}
. The relation (
6.32
) for the corresponding diamond
(s,u;s,w)\in\mathfrak{L}_{D}
is
a_{s,s}^{-1}a_{s,w}=a_{u,s}^{-1}a_{u,w}
. Using (
6.30
), this simplifies to
a_{u,s}a_{s,w}=a_{u,w}
.
Hence we obtain the following:

Corollary 6.40
.

With the notation as above, the maximal subgroup of
\PG(P)
containing the projection
x_{p_{0}}
has a presentation
with generators
A=\{a_{p,q}\colon(p,q)\in\mathscr{F}_{D}\}
and relations

\displaystyle a_{p,q}=1

\displaystyle(p,q\in P_{D},\ pq\in T),

\displaystyle a_{p,p}=1

\displaystyle(p\in P_{D}),

\displaystyle a_{p,q}=a_{q,p}^{-1}

\displaystyle((p,q)\in\mathscr{F}_{D}),

∎

\displaystyle a_{u,s}a_{s,w}=a_{u,w}

\displaystyle((s,u,w)\in\mathfrak{T}_{D}).

Remark 6.41
.

As in Remark
6.33
, one can visualise a linked triangle
(s,u,w)\equiv(s,u;s,w)
as:

w
s
u
psp={}
{}=pup
p
p

We saw above that a linked diamond
(s,u;v,w)
induces two linked triangles
(w,s,v)
and
(v,u,w)
. These triangles can be seen in Figure
4
(right), and this can again be interpreted topologically; see
[
26
, Section 10]
. One could also formulate Corollary
6.40
in terms of the alphabet
\{a_{e}\colon e\in E_{D}\}
.

6.4
First application: degenerate diamonds and free groups

We conclude this section by giving some applications of Theorem
6.28
. The first family of applications is based on the observation that in the absence of linked diamonds, the presentation (
6.29
)–(
6.32
) defines a free group. We can actually do a bit better than that. Let us call a linked diamond
(s,u;v,w)

degenerate
if at least one of the following conditions is satisfied:

(D1)

s=u
,

(D2)

v=w
,

(D3)

s=v
and
u=w
.

We note in passing that in fact
(D1)
implies
(D2)
.
In each of these three situations, the relation (
6.32
) reads as follows:

(D1)

a_{s,v}^{-1}a_{s,w}=a_{s,v}^{-1}a_{s,w}
,

(D2)

a_{s,v}^{-1}a_{s,v}=a_{u,v}^{-1}a_{u,v}
,

(D3)

a_{s,s}^{-1}a_{s,u}=a_{u,s}^{-1}a_{u,u}
.

Thus in cases
(D1)
and
(D2)
the relation is trivially true, while in case
(D3)
it is a consequence of
(
6.30
) and (
6.31
). It is also worth noting that when
(s,u;v,w)
is degenerate, the diamond pictured in Figure
4
collapses to a line; the specific type of collapse depends on the type of degeneracy
(D1)
–
(D3)
.

In the next statement recall that
P_{D}=P\cap D
and
E_{D}=E\cap D
, where
D
is the
\mathscr{D}
-class of
p_{0}
. We also fix a subset
E_{D}^{\prime}
of
E_{D}\setminus P_{D}
such that:

•

E_{D}^{\prime}
contains exactly one of
e
or
e^{*}
for each
e\in E_{D}\setminus P_{D}
,

•

E_{D}^{\prime}
contains the spanning tree
T
from Theorem
6.28
.

Proposition 6.42
.

With the notation of Theorem
6.28
, if there are no non-degenerate linked diamonds in
D
, then the maximal subgroup of
\PG(P)
containing
x_{p_{0}}
is free of rank
|E_{D}^{\prime}\setminus T|
. In particular, when
P_{D}
is finite, this rank is equal to

\frac{|E_{D}|-3|P_{D}|}{2}+1.

Proof.

In the absence of non-degenerate linked diamonds,
the presentation from Theorem
6.28
consists of generators
A=\{a_{p,q}\colon(p,q)\in\mathscr{F}_{D}\}
and relations (
6.29
)–(
6.31
), and we recall that there is a bijection
\mathscr{F}_{D}\to E_{D}
given by
(p,q)\mapsto pq
.
The generators of the form
a_{p,p}
are equal to
1
by (
6.30
), and the remaining generators
are equal in pairs according to (
6.31
). We can therefore eliminate all generators but those corresponding to
E_{D}^{\prime}
. Finally, the generators corresponding to
T
can be eliminated, being equal to
1
by (
6.29
). When this elimination has been carried out, no relations remain. This gives the first claim.

For the second claim, we note that when
P_{D}
is finite, so too is
E_{D}
and
T
, and so we have
|E_{D}^{\prime}\setminus T|=|E_{D}^{\prime}|-|T|
. The formula then follows from
|E_{D}^{\prime}|=\frac{|E_{D}|-|P_{D}|}{2}
and
|T|=|P_{D}|-1
.
∎

Remark 6.43
.

Proposition
6.42
can also be interpreted and proved topologically. By
[
26
, Theorem 10.7]
, and since there are no non-degenerate linked diamonds in
D
, the maximal subgroup in question is (isomorphic to) the fundamental group of the graph with vertex set
P_{D}
, and an edge
\{p,q\}
for distinct
p,q\in P_{D}
with
p\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q
. It is well known (see
[
46
, Example 1.22]
) that the fundamental group of a connected graph with edge set
E
is free of rank
|E\setminus T|
for any spanning tree
T
.

We now deduce a number of corollaries of this result. The first concerns the
adjacency semigroups
of Jackson and Volkov
[
53
]
, which were treated as a key example in
[
26
]
. Let
\Gamma
be a connected, symmetric, reflexive digraph with vertex set
P
and edge set
E
. The
adjacency semigroup

\sfA(\Gamma)
has underlying set
(P\times P)\cup\{0\}
, and product

0^{2}=0=0(p,q)=(p,q)0\and(p,q)(r,s)=\begin{cases}(p,s)&\text{if $(q,r)\in E$}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}.

The involution
0^{*}=0
and
(p,q)^{*}=(q,p)
gives it the structure of a regular
*
-semigroup. We have
\sfE(\sfA(\Gamma))=E\cup\{0\}
, and if we identify
p\in P
with
(p,p)\in\sfA(\Gamma)
, then
\sfP(\sfA(\Gamma))=P\cup\{0\}
. It follows quickly from connectivity of
\Gamma
that
\sfA(\Gamma)
is projection-generated. Indeed, for any
p,q\in P
we have
(p,q)=p_{1}\cdots p_{k}
for any path
p=p_{1}\to\cdots\to p_{k}=q
in
\Gamma
. It follows that
D=P\times P
is the unique non-zero
\mathscr{D}
-class in
\sfA(\Gamma)
, and that there are no non-degenerate linked diamonds; specifically, it is easy to check that if
(s,u;v,w)
is
p
-linked, then
s=u=v=w=p
. Proposition
6.42
therefore applies, and we obtain the following:

Corollary 6.44
.

Let
S=\sfA(\Gamma)
be the adjacency semigroup of a finite, connected, symmetric, reflexive digraph
\Gamma=(P,E)
. Suppose also that
\Gamma
has
|P|=n
vertices, and that the simple (undirected, loop-free) reduct of
\Gamma
has
k
edges. Then the maximal subgroup of
\PG(\sfP(S))
containing any non-zero projection is free of rank
k-n+1
.

Proof.

Writing
D:=P\times P
, we have
|P_{D}|=n
and
|E_{D}|=2k+n
. Proposition
6.42
and a simple calculation gives the stated rank.
∎

We can also give an
r=n-1
version of Theorem
1.1
:

Corollary 6.45
.

Let
n\geq 1
, and let
p_{0}
be a projection of rank
n-1
in the partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}
.
Then the maximal subgroup of the free projection-generated semigroup
\PG(\sfP(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
containing
x_{p_{0}}
is free of rank
\binom{n-1}{2}
.

Proof.

Let
D:=D_{p_{0}}
. As in Remark
4.15
we have
|P_{D}|=n+\binom{n}{2}
, and
|E_{D}|=n+5\binom{n}{2}
. Since the only idempotent in the
\mathscr{D}
-class above
D
is the identity element, there are no non-degenerate linked diamonds in
D
. The proof again concludes with an application of Proposition
6.42
, and a simple calculation.
∎

As a final non-obvious application of Proposition
6.42
, we can also determine the maximal subgroup in the bottom
\mathscr{D}
-class of
\PG(\sfP(\mathcal{B}_{4}))
, where
\mathcal{B}_{4}
is the Brauer monoid of degree
4
, consisting of partitions from
\mathcal{P}_{4}
into blocks all of size
2
.

Corollary 6.46
.

Let
p_{0}
be a projection of rank
0
in the Brauer monoid
\mathcal{B}_{4}
.
Then the maximal subgroup of the free projection-generated semigroup
\PG(\sfP(\mathcal{B}_{4}))
containing
x_{p_{0}}
is free cyclic.

Proof.

Note that
D:=D_{p_{0}}
is a
3\times 3
square band, because there are three equivalence relations on the set
[4]
with classes of size
2
.
We claim that there are no non-degenerate linked diamonds in
D
, and therefore Proposition
6.42
applies. Since
|P_{D}|=3
and
|E_{D}|=9
, the proposition will tell us the group is free of rank
1
.

To prove the claim, consider a
p
-linked diamond
(s,u;v,w)
in
D
. If
p=1
, then
v=psp=s
and
w=pup=u
, so the diamond is degenerate of type
(D3)
. Now suppose
p\not=1
, so that
p
has an upper block
A
, and hence also the lower block
A^{\prime}
. We see then that
v=psp
and
w=pup
also contain the blocks
A
and
A^{\prime}
. Since
v
and
w
have rank
0
, they also contain the blocks
[4]\setminus A
and
[4]^{\prime}\setminus A^{\prime}
, and hence
v=w
. Thus, the diamond is degenerate of type
(D2)
.
∎

6.5
Second application: maximal subgroups in
\PG(\sfP(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
, the case of rank
0

Our second application of Theorem
6.28
is to determine the maximal subgroups of the free projection-generated semigroups over partition monoids corresponding to idempotents in the bottom
\mathscr{D}
-class, thereby establishing the
{r=0}
case of Theorem
1.1
. This time there are
many
non-degenerate linked diamonds, and these play an important role in the proof.

Proposition 6.47
.

Let
n\geq 1
, and let
p_{0}
be a projection of rank
0
in the partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}
.
Then the maximal subgroup of the free projection-generated semigroup
\PG(\sfP(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
containing
x_{p_{0}}
is trivial.

Proof.

The projections in
D:=D_{p_{0}}
are naturally indexed by the set
\textup{{Eq}}[n]
of equivalence relations on
[n]
.
For
\sigma\in\textup{{Eq}}[n]
, let us write
\overline{\sigma}
and
\widetilde{\sigma}
respectively for the projection in
D

and the full-domain projection determined by
\sigma
. More precisely, if
A_{1},\ldots,A_{k}
are the
\sigma
-classes, then

\overline{\sigma}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{k}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{k}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\and\widetilde{\sigma}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{k}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{k}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr).

The projections of
D
are then precisely
P_{D}=\bigl\{\overline{\sigma}\colon\sigma\in\textup{{Eq}}[n]\bigr\}
.

Let us denote by
\Delta=\Delta_{[n]}
and
\nabla=\nabla_{[n]}
the equality and full relations on
[n]
, respectively. Without loss of generality we can assume that
p_{0}=\overline{\nabla}
. For the abstract generators

a_{\overline{\sigma},\overline{\tau}}
appearing in Theorem
6.28
, we omit the overlines, and also temporarily revert to the square bracket notation to enhance readability:
[\sigma,\tau]:=a_{\overline{\sigma},\overline{\tau}}
. Since
D
is a rectangular band,
\mathscr{F}_{D}=\nabla_{P_{D}}
is the universal relation on
P_{D}
, so for the spanning tree
T
we can pick the star graph with edges
\overline{\nabla}\to\overline{\sigma}
for
\sigma\in\textup{{Eq}}[n]\setminus\{\nabla\}
. The presentation
(
6.29
)–(
6.32
)
becomes:

(6.48)

\displaystyle[\nabla,\sigma]=1

\displaystyle(\sigma\in\textup{{Eq}}[n]\setminus\{\nabla\}),

(6.49)

\displaystyle[\sigma,\sigma]=1

\displaystyle(\sigma\in\textup{{Eq}}[n]),

(6.50)

\displaystyle[\sigma,\tau]=[\tau,\sigma]^{-1}

\displaystyle(\sigma,\tau\in\textup{{Eq}}[n]),

(6.51)

\displaystyle[\sigma,\mu]^{-1}[\sigma,\nu]=[\tau,\mu]^{-1}[\tau,\nu]

\displaystyle((\overline{\sigma},\overline{\tau};\overline{\mu},\overline{\nu})\in\mathfrak{L}_{D}).

We aim to show that
[\sigma,\tau]=1
for all
\sigma,\tau\in\textup{{Eq}}[n]
as a consequence of
(
6.48
)–(
6.51
). We first prove a special instance:

Claim 1
.

If
\sigma,\tau\in\textup{{Eq}}[n]
satisfy
\sigma\subseteq\tau
, then
[\sigma,\tau]=1
as a consequence of (
6.48
)–(
6.51
).

Proof.

Writing
\cdot
for the action in (
6.4
), notice that

\overline{\nabla}\cdot\widetilde{\tau}=\overline{\nabla}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\overline{\sigma}\cdot\widetilde{\tau}=\overline{\tau},

the second equality being a consequence of the assumption
\sigma\subseteq\tau
.
Hence
(\overline{\nabla},\overline{\sigma};\overline{\nabla},\overline{\tau})
is a
\widetilde{\tau}
-linked diamond, and (
6.51
) contains the relation

[\nabla,\nabla]^{-1}\>[\nabla,\tau]=[\sigma,\nabla]^{-1}\>[\sigma,\tau].

But
[\nabla,\nabla]=[\nabla,\tau]=[\sigma,\nabla]=1
from (
6.48
)– (
6.50
), so indeed
[\sigma,\tau]=1
.
∎

Now consider arbitrary
\sigma,\tau\in\textup{{Eq}}[n]
.
Denote by
\sigma\wedge\tau
and
\sigma\vee\tau
their meet (i.e. intersection) and join in the lattice
\textup{{Eq}}[n]
.
This time we have

\overline{\sigma}\cdot\widetilde{\tau}=\overline{\sigma\vee\tau}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\overline{\sigma\wedge\tau}\cdot\widetilde{\tau}=\overline{\tau},

so that
(\overline{\sigma},\overline{\sigma\wedge\tau};\overline{\sigma\vee\tau},\overline{\tau})
is
\widetilde{\tau}
-linked, and so (
6.51
) contains the relation

[\sigma,\sigma\vee\tau]^{-1}\>[\sigma,\tau]=[\sigma\wedge\tau,\sigma\vee\tau]^{-1}\>[\sigma\wedge\tau,\tau].

By Claim
1
we have
[\sigma,\sigma\vee\tau]=[\sigma\wedge\tau,\sigma\vee\tau]=[\sigma\wedge\tau,\tau]=1
, and it again follows that
[\sigma,\tau]=1
,
completing the proof of the proposition.
∎

Remark 6.52
.

The previous result again has a topological interpretation, saying that a natural simplicial
2
-complex corresponding to the projections of rank
0
is simply connected; cf.
[
26
, Section 10]
. The presence of the linked diamonds means that this complex has many (non-degenerate)
2
-cells, and hence Proposition
6.42
does not apply.
One may wonder whether it is possible to gain a more intrinsic understanding of this complex, so as to naturally deduce its simple connectedness via a purely topological route.

7
Second family of presentations for maximal subgroups of
\PG(P)

We now aim to obtain a presentation for a maximal subgroup of
\PG(P)
that would reflect the fact that such a group is a homomorphic image of the corresponding maximal subgroup of
\IG(E)
.
Here
P
is a projection algebra, and
E=\sfE(P)
is the associated biordered set, and, as
in Section
6
, we can assume that
P=\sfP(S)
and
E=\sfE(S)
for some projection-generated regular
*
-semigroup
S
. We fix such an
S
for the rest of this section. We also fix a projection
{p_{0}\in P(\subseteq E)}
, and we continue to write
G^{\sfE}
and
G^{\textsf{P}}
for the maximal subgroups of
\IG(E)
and
\PG(P)
containing
x_{p_{0}}
, respectively.

7.1
Presentations via singular squares

Our starting point is
[
26
, Theorem 7.9]
, which gives the following presentation for
\PG(P)
as a homomorphic image of
\IG(E)
:

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
P
be a projection algebra, let
E=\sfE(P)
be the associated biordered set, and let
X_{E}=\{x_{e}\colon e\in E\}
be an alphabet in one-one correspondence with
E
.
Then the free projection-generated semigroup
\PG(P)
is defined by the presentation

(7.2)

\displaystyle\langle X_{E}\ |\

\displaystyle x_{e}x_{f}=x_{ef}\hskip 10.00002pt((e,f)\in\mathfrak{B}),

(7.3)

\displaystyle x_{p}x_{q}=x_{pq}\hskip 10.00002pt(p,q\in P)\rangle.

We will now apply the Reidemeister–Schreier rewriting method to this presentation.
Roughly speaking, rewriting the defining relations (
7.2
) for
\IG(E)
will give us a presentation for its maximal subgroup
G^{\sfE}
as in Section
4
, while rewriting the additional relations (
7.3
) will yield some further relations, giving us the quotient
G^{\textsf{P}}
.

Once again we need to set up the Reidemeister–Schreier framework.
As explained in Subsections
4.1
and
6.1
, we can index the
\mathscr{R}
- and
\mathscr{L}
-classes in the
\mathscr{D}
-class
D
of
p_{0}
in
S
, and use this indexing for the
\mathscr{D}
-classes of
x_{p_{0}}
in both
\PG(P)
and
\IG(E)
.
And, as in Subsection
6.1
,
we take
I=J=P_{D}=P\cap D
, and
K=\mathscr{F}_{D}
.
Moreover, for
(p,q)\in\mathscr{F}_{D}
, the idempotent
e_{p,q}\in H_{p,q}=R_{p}\cap L_{q}
is
e_{p,q}=pq
.
Following the approach from Subsection
4.2
, we will fix an arbitrary spanning tree
T
of the Graham–Houghton graph
\GH(D)
; this leads to a natural system of Schreier representatives
\{{r_{p}\colon p\in P_{D}\}}
, as explained in Subsection
4.2
.
These representatives are words over the alphabet
{\{x_{e}\colon e\in E_{D}\}}
, where
E_{D}:=E\cap D
.

Applying the Reidemeister–Schreier rewriting to the presentation for
\PG(P)
given in Theorem
7.1
, we obtain a presentation for
G^{\textsf{P}}
over the alphabet

B:=\bigl\{[t,x_{e}]\colon t\in P_{D},\ e\in E,\ t\cdot e\neq 0\bigr\},

corresponding to the generating set

\{x_{p_{0}}r_{t}x_{e}r_{t\cdot e}^{\prime}\colon t\in P_{D},\ e\in E,\ t\cdot e\neq 0\},

and with the following defining relations over
B
:

(7.4)

\displaystyle[t,x_{e}]=1

\displaystyle(t\in P_{D},\ e\in E_{D},\ r_{t}x_{e}=r_{t\cdot e}),

(7.5)

\displaystyle[t,x_{e}][t\cdot e,x_{f}]=[t,x_{ef}]

\displaystyle(t\in P_{D},\ (e,f)\in\mathfrak{B},\ t\cdot ef\neq 0),

(7.6)

\displaystyle[t,x_{p}][t\cdot p,x_{q}]=[t,x_{pq}]

\displaystyle(t\in P_{D},\ p,q\in P,\ t\cdot pq\neq 0).

The first two families of relations constitute the presentation for
G^{\sfE}
we considered in Section
4
, where it was transformed into the presentation (
4.8
)–(
4.10
).
Here we follow this same process, but in our new notation.
The key step is the introduction of new generators (
4.6
), which now becomes:

(7.7)

\llbracket p,q\rrbracket=[\textup{{q}}(p),x_{pq}]\hskip 10.00002pt(p,q\in\mathscr{F}_{D}).

Here,
\textup{{q}}(p)
replaces
\textup{{j}}(i)
, and is obtained in reference to the spanning tree
T
, as explained in Subsection
4.2
; its salient property is that
H_{p,\textup{{q}}(p)}
is a group, i.e.
(p,\textup{{q}}(p))\in\mathscr{F}_{D}(=K)
. Then the original generators were eliminated using (
4.7
), which now becomes

(7.8)

[t,x_{e}]=\llbracket p,t\rrbracket^{-1}\llbracket p,q\rrbracket,\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptp:=ee^{*}tee^{*}\ \text{and}\ q:=e^{*}te.

In this elimination, note that the ‘
j
’ from Subsection
4.1
is
j=t\cdot e=e^{*}te=q
, and the ‘
i
’ is any element of
I
such that
{(i,t),(i,j)\in K}
and
es=s
for all
s\in R_{i}
. One can check that
i=ee^{*}tee^{*}=p
is a valid choice. For this check, note that a generator
[t,x_{e}]
existing means that
t\cdot e\not=0
, i.e.
te\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}t
, which gives
t=te(te)^{*}=tee^{*}t
.
The final presentation (
4.8
)–(
4.10
), written in our current notation, becomes:

(7.9)

\displaystyle\llbracket p,t\rrbracket=\llbracket p,q\rrbracket

\displaystyle((p,t),(p,q)\in\mathscr{F}_{D},\ r_{t}x_{pq}=r_{q}),

(7.10)

\displaystyle\llbracket p,\textup{{q}}(p)\rrbracket=1

\displaystyle(p\in P_{D}),

(7.11)

\displaystyle\llbracket p,s\rrbracket^{-1}\llbracket p,t\rrbracket=\llbracket q,s\rrbracket^{-1}\llbracket q,t\rrbracket

\displaystyle(\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}ps&pt\\
qs&qt\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\in\mathfrak{S}).

To obtain our desired presentation for
G^{\textsf{P}}
we need to perform the substitution (
7.8
) in (
7.6
) as well:

(7.12)

\llbracket ptp,t\rrbracket^{-1}\llbracket ptp,ptp\rrbracket\llbracket qptpq,ptp\rrbracket^{-1}\llbracket qptpq,qptpq\rrbracket=\llbracket pqptpqp,t\rrbracket^{-1}\llbracket pqptpqp,qptpq\rrbracket\\
(t\in P_{D},\ p,q\in P,\ t\cdot pq\neq 0).

A subset of these relations can be obtained by taking
(p,q)\in\mathscr{F}_{D}
and
t=p
:

(7.13)

\llbracket p,p\rrbracket^{-1}\llbracket p,p\rrbracket\llbracket qpq,p\rrbracket^{-1}\llbracket qpq,qpq\rrbracket=\llbracket pqpqp,p\rrbracket^{-1}\llbracket pqpqp,qpq\rrbracket\hskip 10.00002pt((p,q)\in\mathscr{F}_{D}).

Using
(p,q)\in\mathscr{F}
, and swapping the left- and right-hand sides, this simplifies to:

(7.14)

\llbracket p,p\rrbracket^{-1}\llbracket p,q\rrbracket=\llbracket q,p\rrbracket^{-1}\llbracket q,q\rrbracket\hskip 10.00002pt((p,q)\in\mathscr{F}_{D}).

We will now show that in fact relations (
7.14
), together with the
\IG(E)
relations
(
7.9
)–(
7.11
),
imply all relations (
7.12
).
To do so, let
t\in P_{D}
and
p,q\in P
be such that
t\cdot pq\not=0
, as in (
7.12
).
It follows from
t\cdot pq\not=0
(and
pq=pqpq
) that
t\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}tpq\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}tpqp
, which (using the involution) implies
pqpt\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}t
.
But
t\cdot pq\not=0
also implies
t\cdot p\not=0
, which gives
t\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}tp
, and hence
pt\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}t
.
The previous two conclusions give
pt\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}pqpt
. The elements
pt
and
pqpt=pqp\;\!\;\!t
are idempotents (as products of two projections), and since
p
is a left identity for them both, we have an LR singular square

\begin{pmatrix}pt&pt\;\!\;\!p\\
pqpt&pqpt\;\!\;\!p\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}ptp\;\!\;\!t&ptp\;\!\;\!ptp\\
pqptpqp\;\!\;\!t&pqptpqp\;\!\;\!ptp\end{pmatrix}.

The corresponding relation from (
7.11
) is:

(7.15)

\llbracket ptp,t\rrbracket^{-1}\llbracket ptp,ptp\rrbracket=\llbracket pqptpqp,t\rrbracket^{-1}\llbracket pqptpqp,ptp\rrbracket.

We saw above that
tp\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}t\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}tpqp
. Since
\mathscr{R}
is a left congruence, it follows that
qptp\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}qptpqp
. These last two elements are again idempotents (products of two projections), and have
p
as a common right identity, so we have a UD singular square

\begin{pmatrix}qptp&qptpqp\\
p\;\!\;\!qptp&p\;\!\;\!qptpqp\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}qptpq\;\!\;\!ptp&qptpq\;\!\;\!pqptpqp\\
pqptpqp\;\!\;\!ptp&pqptpqp\;\!\;\!pqptpqp\end{pmatrix}.

This gives us the following relation from (
7.11
):

(7.16)

\llbracket qptpq,ptp\rrbracket^{-1}\llbracket qptpq,pqptpqp\rrbracket=\llbracket pqptpqp,ptp\rrbracket^{-1}\llbracket pqptpqp,pqptpqp\rrbracket.

And we observe that (
7.14
) contain the following relation:

(7.17)

\llbracket qptpq,qptpq\rrbracket^{-1}\llbracket qptpq,pqptpqp\rrbracket=\llbracket pqptpqp,qptpq\rrbracket^{-1}\llbracket pqptpqp,pqptpqp\rrbracket.

Now we have

\displaystyle\llbracket ptp,t\rrbracket^{-1}

\displaystyle\llbracket ptp,ptp\rrbracket\llbracket qptpq,ptp\rrbracket^{-1}\llbracket qptpq,qptpq\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket pqptpqp,t\rrbracket^{-1}\llbracket pqptpqp,ptp\rrbracket\llbracket qptpq,ptp\rrbracket^{-1}\llbracket qptpq,qptpq\rrbracket

by (
7.15
)

\displaystyle=\llbracket pqptpqp,t\rrbracket^{-1}\llbracket pqptpqp,pqptpqp\rrbracket\llbracket qptpq,pqptpqp\rrbracket^{-1}\llbracket qptpq,qptpq\rrbracket

by (
7.16
)

\displaystyle=\llbracket pqptpqp,t\rrbracket^{-1}\llbracket pqptpqp,qptpq\rrbracket

\displaystyle\text{by \eqref{eq:pq13}},

which is precisely the relation (
7.12
).
So, a presentation for the maximal subgroup consists of relations (
7.9
)–(
7.11
) and (
7.14
).

As a final transformation, recall
that we derived the Schreier representatives
r_{p}
and the elements
\textup{{q}}(p)
from the spanning tree
T
. Therefore, following the argument from Subsection
4.2
, the relations (
7.9
) and (
7.10
) can be replaced by the spanning tree relations
\mathcal{R}_{1}(T)
.
And once again, we rename the generators
\llbracket p,q\rrbracket
into
a_{e}
where
e=pq\in E_{D}
, noting then that
p=ee^{*}
and
q=e^{*}e
.
Thus we obtain the following:

Theorem 7.18
.

Let
P
be a projection algebra, let
S
be any projection-generated regular
*
-semigroup with
P=\sfP(S)
, and let
p_{0}\in P
be arbitrary. Denote by
D
the
\mathscr{D}
-class of
S
containing
p_{0}
, and let
T
be any spanning tree for the Graham–Houghton graph
\GH(D)
. With the rest of the notation as above, the maximal subgroup of
\PG(P)
containing
x_{p_{0}}
is defined by the presentation with generators
A=\{a_{e}\colon e\in E_{D}\}
and relations

(7.19)

\displaystyle a_{e}=1

\displaystyle(e\in T),

(7.20)

\displaystyle a_{ee^{*}}^{-1}a_{e}=a_{e^{*}}^{-1}a_{e^{*}e}

\displaystyle(e\in E_{D}),

(7.21)

\displaystyle a_{e}^{-1}a_{f}=a_{g}^{-1}a_{h}

\displaystyle(\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\in\mathfrak{S}).

The above presentation simplifies somewhat when one makes
what seems a natural requirement on the spanning tree, namely that it contains all the projections from
D
.
This can always be done, by, for example, taking a spanning tree for the friendship digraph
\mathscr{F}_{D}
, as we did in Subsection
6.1
, and extending it by the set of projections.
Under this assumption,
relations (
7.19
) contain all
a_{p}=1
(
p\in P_{D}
), and
relations (
7.20
) then become
a_{e}=a_{e^{*}}^{-1}
.

Corollary 7.22
.

With the notation as above, and assuming that the spanning tree
T
contains
P_{D}
, the maximal subgroup of
\PG(P)
containing
x_{p_{0}}

is defined by the presentation with generators
A=\{a_{e}\colon e\in E_{D}\}
and relations

(7.23)

\displaystyle a_{e}=1

\displaystyle(e\in T),

(7.24)

\displaystyle a_{e}=a_{e^{*}}^{-1}

\displaystyle(e\in E_{D}),

(7.25)

\displaystyle a_{e}^{-1}a_{f}=a_{g}^{-1}a_{h}

\displaystyle(\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\in\mathfrak{S}).

7.2
The relationship between singular squares and linked squares

The presentations for the group
G^{\textsf{P}}
in Corollaries
6.34
and
7.22
are strikingly similar. The first two sets of relations in each are the same (bearing in mind the connections between the two types of trees, as discussed before Corollary
7.22
, and assuming we take the same tree in both presentations), while the third sets of relations in each presentation are of the form
a_{e}^{-1}a_{f}=a_{g}^{-1}a_{h}
for squares
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
of two different kinds: linked squares or singular squares.
Tracing back the proofs, we claim that the letter
a_{e}
(
e\in E_{D}
) in both presentations corresponds to the same element of
\PG(P)
, as long as one chooses the set-up carefully.
Indeed, keeping in mind that
X_{P}\subseteq X_{E}
, we can make the same choice of words
\{r_{p}\colon p\in P_{D}\}
and
\{r_{p}^{\prime}\colon p\in P_{D}\}

for both presentations, as in Subsection
6.1
, and we choose
\textup{{q}}(p)=p
for all
p\in P_{D}
for the second presentation. Now consider a letter
a_{e}
, where
e\in E_{D}
, and write
t=ee^{*}
and
p=e^{*}e
, so that
(t,p)\in\mathscr{F}
and
e=tp
. Then in the second presentation,
a_{e}
represents

a_{e}=a_{tp}=\llbracket t,p\rrbracket=[\textup{{q}}(t),x_{tp}]=[t,x_{e}]=x_{p_{0}}r_{t}x_{e}r_{t\cdot e}^{\prime}=x_{p_{0}}r_{t}\;\!\;\!x_{t}x_{p}\;\!\;\!r_{e^{*}te}^{*}x_{p_{0}}=x_{p_{0}}r_{t}x_{p}r_{ptp}^{*}x_{p_{0}},

where in the second-last step we used (
7.3
), and in the last step
e^{*}te=pt\;\!\;\!t\;\!\;\!tp=ptp
, and the fact that the last letter of
r_{t}
is
x_{t}
by definition (so that
r_{t}x_{t}=r_{t}
by (
7.3
)). Comparing this to (
6.6
), the claim is proved.

Now that we know the generators in the two presentations for
G^{\textsf{P}}
correspond to the same elements of the group, we know
a priori
that the sets of relations in the two presentations are equivalent. One might then wonder if this can be shown directly, i.e. show explicitly that each set of relations implies the other set. It turns out that this is actually a worthwhile exercise, as it ultimately leads to a deeper understanding of the relationship between (and among) the two kinds of squares. The next three lemmas explore this relationship, showing that any square implies the existence of other squares of various kinds. The squares under consideration in each of them are illustrated in Figure
6
. After we prove these lemmas, we use them to explicitly derive the presentations from Corollaries
6.34
and
7.22
from each other.
As an additional consequence of this derivation, we will see that some further simplification in the relations is possible, in the sense that certain ‘special’ square relations imply the others; see Remark
7.30
.

Lemma 7.26
.

Let
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}sv&sw\\
uv&uw\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
be a
p
-linked square of idempotents, corresponding to the
p
-linked diamond of projections
(s,u;v,w)
. Then the squares

\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
v&vw\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}sv&sw\\
v&vw\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}g&h\\
wv&w\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}uv&uw\\
wv&w\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)

are both UD-singularised by
p
.

Proof.

Because of the
\mathscr{F}
-relationships among the projections
s,u,v,w
(including
v\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}w
as explained in Remark
6.33
), we have the following idempotents:

\begin{pmatrix}s&sv&&sw\\
vs&v&vu&vw\\
&uv&u&uw\\
ws&wv&wu&w\end{pmatrix}.

In the above, entries in the same row or column are
\mathscr{R}
- or
\mathscr{L}
-related, respectively,
but it is possible for some of these
\mathscr{R}
- or
\mathscr{L}
-classes to coincide. We only show that the square
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
v&vw\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}sv&sw\\
v&vw\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
is UD-singularised by
p
, as the other is analogous. Since
v=psp
and
w=pup
(as
(s,u;v,w)
is
p
-linked), it follows that
p
is a right identity for both
sv
and
sw
. We also have
p\;\!\;\!sv=pspsp=psp=v
.
∎

s
v
u
w
s
v
u
w
\mathrlap{e}{\phantom{f}}
\mathrlap{f}{\phantom{f}}
\mathrlap{g}{\phantom{f}}
\mathrlap{h}{\phantom{f}}
\mathrlap{ep}{\phantom{ff}}
\mathrlap{gp}{\phantom{ff}}
s
v
u
w
s
v
u
w
\mathrlap{e}{\phantom{f}}
\mathrlap{f}{\phantom{f}}
\mathrlap{g}{\phantom{f}}
\mathrlap{h}{\phantom{f}}
s
v
u
w
s
v
u
w
\mathrlap{e}{\phantom{f}}
\mathrlap{f}{\phantom{f}}
\mathrlap{g}{\phantom{f}}
\mathrlap{h}{\phantom{f}}

Figure 6:
Left: a
p
-linked square (black), and the two resulting singular squares (red) from Lemma
7.26
.
Middle: a singular square (black), and the two resulting projection-singularised squares (red) from Lemma
7.27
.
Right: a projection-singularised square (black), and the two resulting linked squares (red) from Lemma
7.28
.
In each diagram, blue
\mathscr{H}
-classes contain projections, and grey
\mathscr{H}
-classes contain idempotents.

Of course applying the involution to the vertical squares in Lemma
7.26
yields a pair of horizontal squares. Similarly, the next two results have duals, which we will not state explicitly.

Lemma 7.27
.

Let
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
be a singular square that is LR-singularised by the idempotent
pq
, where
(p,q)\in\mathscr{F}
. Then the squares

\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&ep\\
g&gp\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}f&ep\\
h&gp\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)

are both LR-singularised by
p
.

Proof.

Since
pq
is a left identity for each of
e
,
f
,
g
and
h
, it follows that
p
is a left identity for the same elements. Hence
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&ep\\
g&gp\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
and
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}f&fp\\
h&hp\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
are singularised by
p
.
Since
epq=f
and
p\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q
it follows that

fp=(epq)p=ep
; analogously,
hp=gp
, completing the proof.
∎

Lemma 7.28
.

Let
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}sv&sw\\
uv&uw\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
be a singular square that is LR-singularised by the projection
p
, and where
(s,v),(s,w),(u,v),(u,w)\in\mathscr{F}
. Then
(s,v;s,w)
and
(u,v;u,w)
are
p
-linked diamonds of projections (i.e.
(s,v,w)
and
(u,v,w)
are
p
-linked triangles), yielding the
p
-linked squares of idempotents

\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}s&f\\
e^{*}&vw\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}s&sw\\
vs&vw\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}u&h\\
g^{*}&vw\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}u&uw\\
vu&vw\end{smallmatrix}\bigr).

Proof.

We prove the statement for
(s,v;s,w)
;
(u,v;u,w)
is analogous.
Since, by assumption,
(s,v),(s,w)\in\mathscr{F}
, and
(s,s)\in\mathscr{F}
trivially,
we just need to verify that
(v,w)\in\mathscr{F}
,
psp=s
and
pvp=w
.
Keeping in mind
(RS5)
, we have
s=ee^{*}=ff^{*}
,
v=e^{*}e
and
w=f^{*}f
.
Now we have:

psp=pee^{*}p=(pe)(pe)^{*}=ee^{*}=s\and pvp=pe^{*}ep=(ep)^{*}(ep)=f^{*}f=w.

With the help of the the second equality above, and remembering that the product of two projections is an idempotent, we also have
wvw=pvp\;\!\;\!v\;\!\;\!pvp=pvp=w
, and

\displaystyle vwv

\displaystyle=v\;\!\;\!pvp\;\!\;\!v=vpv=e^{*}eppe^{*}e=e^{*}(ep)(ep)^{*}e=e^{*}ff^{*}e=e^{*}ee^{*}e=e^{*}e=v,

completing the proof.
∎

We now have:

Proposition 7.29
.

The presentations given in Corollaries
6.34
and
7.22
are equivalent.

Proof.

(
\Leftarrow
)
We show that relations
(
7.23
)–(
7.25
)
imply (
6.37
).
Suppose
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
is a linked square of idempotents in
E_{D}
, so that
{\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}sv&sw\\
uv&uw\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)}
for a corresponding linked diamond of projections
(s,u;v,w)
; cf. Remark
6.38
. Using the singular squares
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
v&vw\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
and
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}g&h\\
wv&w\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
from Lemma
7.26
, we calculate

a_{e}^{-1}a_{f}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\eqref{eq:pq21}}}}{{=}}a_{v}^{-1}a_{vw}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\eqref{eq:pq19}}}}{{=}}a_{vw}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\eqref{eq:pq22}}}}{{=}}a_{wv}^{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\eqref{eq:pq19}}}}{{=}}a_{wv}^{-1}a_{w}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\eqref{eq:pq21}}}}{{=}}a_{g}^{-1}a_{h},

which is exactly relation (
6.37
) for our original square
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
.

(
\Rightarrow
)
Now we show that relations (
6.35
)–(
6.37
) imply the singular square relations
arising from
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}sv&sw\\
uv&uw\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
.
First we do this in the case when the square is LR-singularised by a projection
p
. By Lemma
7.28
we have two
p
-linked squares of idempotents

\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}s&f\\
e^{*}&vw\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
and
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}u&h\\
g^{*}&vw\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
.
Using these, we calculate

a_{f}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\eqref{eq:rwp40}}}}{{=}}a_{s}^{-1}a_{f}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\eqref{eq:rwp43}}}}{{=}}a_{e^{*}}^{-1}a_{vw}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\eqref{eq:rwp42}}}}{{=}}a_{e}a_{vw}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pta_{h}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\eqref{eq:rwp40}}}}{{=}}a_{u}^{-1}a_{h}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\eqref{eq:rwp43}}}}{{=}}a_{g^{*}}^{-1}a_{vw}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\eqref{eq:rwp42}}}}{{=}}a_{g}a_{vw}.

These in turn yield
a_{e}^{-1}a_{f}=a_{vw}
and
a_{g}^{-1}a_{h}=a_{vw}
, which combine to give
a_{e}^{-1}a_{f}=a_{g}^{-1}a_{h}
, i.e. the relation (
7.25
) for the square
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
.

Now consider a square
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
that is LR-singularised by an arbitrary idempotent
pq
, where
(p,q)\in\mathscr{F}
. By Lemma
7.27
, the squares
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&ep\\
g&gp\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
and
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}ep&f\\
gp&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
are LR-singularised by the projection
p
. By the previous paragraph, (
6.35
)–(
6.37
) imply

a_{e}^{-1}a_{ep}=a_{g}^{-1}a_{gp}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pta_{ep}^{-1}a_{f}=a_{gp}^{-1}a_{h},

which, in turn, imply

a_{e}^{-1}a_{f}=a_{e}^{-1}a_{ep}\;\!\;\!a_{ep}^{-1}a_{f}=a_{g}^{-1}a_{gp}\;\!\;\!a_{gp}^{-1}a_{h}=a_{g}^{-1}a_{h}
, i.e. relation (
7.25
) for the LR square
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
.

The proof for UD squares is dual, and the proposition follows.
∎

Remark 7.30
.

It is worth drawing out the fact (which is apparent in the proof of Proposition
7.29
) that in the presentation from Corollary
7.22
, we only need to include singular square relations (
7.25
) for squares singularised by projections.
In fact, looking at the form of the squares in Lemma
7.26
(which were used in the proof of Proposition
7.29
), we only need to include such relations for projection-singularised squares in which one of the entries (of the square) is a projection.

It is also worth noting that the exact form of the spanning tree
T
appearing in the presentations from Corollaries
6.34
and
7.22
did not play a part in the above deductions, only that it contains the projections
P_{D}
.

8
Maximal subgroups in
\PG(\sfP(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
and
\IG(\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
:
the common set-up

At this point we turn our attention to our main application of the theory developed in the first half of the paper: computing the maximal subgroups in the free projection-generated regular
*
-semigroup
\PG(P)
and in the free idempotent-generated semigroup
\IG(E)
arising from the partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}
.
Following the first preliminaries on
\mathcal{P}_{n}
from Subsection
2.10
,
here we establish the common notation and framework to be used in the subsequent sections.

8.1
Relations and presentations

Throughout what follows, we will consider the partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}
for some
n\geq 2
, and a projection
p_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
of rank
r\leq n-2
. We let
P:=\sfP(\mathcal{P}_{n})
be the projection algebra of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, and
E:=\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n})
the biordered set of idempotents of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
.
We will again denote by
G^{\textsf{P}}
and
G^{\sfE}
the maximal subgroups of
\PG(P)
and
\IG(E)
containing
x_{p_{0}}
, respectively.
In
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, we will write:

•

D(n,r)
for the
\mathscr{D}
-class of all partitions of rank
r
,

•

P(n,r)
for the set of all projections in
D(n,r)
,

•

E(n,r)
for the set of all idempotents in
D(n,r)
,

•

\mathfrak{S}(n,r)
for the set of all singular squares involving idempotents from
E(n,r)
, and

•

A(n,r):=\big\{a_{e}\colon e\in E(n,r)\big\}
for the alphabet featuring in the presentations from Theorems
4.13
and
7.18
and Corollaries
6.34
and
7.22
.

We also write

A_{F}:=\{a_{e}\colon e\in F\}\subseteq A(n,r)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for any $F\subseteq E(n,r)$.}

The relations featuring in the presentations established in Sections
4
–
7
come in families, and we will use different notations for these. As before, we have the sets

\mathcal{R}_{1}(F):=\big\{(a_{e}=1)\colon e\in F\big\}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for any $F\subseteq E(n,r)$},

and

\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}(n,r):=\big\{(a_{e}^{-1}a_{f}=a_{g}^{-1}a_{h})\colon\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\in\mathfrak{S}(n,r)\big\}.

Finally, we gather the relations that say that
a_{e}
is the inverse of
a_{e^{*}}
, which are present only in Corollary
7.22
:

\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{inv}}(n,r):=\big\{(a_{e}=a_{e^{*}}^{-1})\colon e\in E(n,r)\big\}.

With this notation, Theorem
4.13
and Corollary
7.22
respectively say that:

•

G^{\sfE}
has presentation
\langle A(n,r)\mid\mathcal{R}_{1}(T),\ \mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}(n,r)\rangle
, where
T
is any spanning tree of the Graham–Houghton graph of
D(n,r)
.

•

G^{\textsf{P}}
has presentation
\langle A(n,r)\mid\mathcal{R}_{1}(T),\ \mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}(n,r),\ \mathcal{R}_{\textsf{inv}}(n,r)\rangle
, where
T
is any spanning tree of the Graham–Houghton graph of
D(n,r)
that contains
P(n,r)
.

8.2
Projections and idempotents

In the coming sections we will of course work a lot with projections and idempotents of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, so we need a good understanding of what these look like. Projections are very simple: it is readily checked (and well known) that they are the partitions of the form

\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&B_{1}&\cdots&B_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&B_{1}&\cdots&B_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr).

Arbitrary idempotents of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
are more complicated, and were described in
[
14
]
. To review this, we need some notation. First, given partitions
a\in\mathcal{P}_{X}
and
b\in\mathcal{P}_{Y}
over disjoint sets
X
and
Y
, their (disjoint) union is a partition in
\mathcal{P}_{X\cup Y}
; we will denote it by
a\oplus b
.
Next, we define the
super-kernel
of a partition
a\in\mathcal{P}_{X}
to be the relation
\KER(a):=\ker(a)\vee\coker(a)
. If
\KER(a)=\nabla_{X}
(the universal relation on
X
) we say that
a
is
connected
. This is equivalent to the middle row
X^{\prime\prime}
being contained in a single component of the product graph
\Gamma(a,a)
.

Proposition 8.1

(see
[
14
, Theorem 5]
)
.

The idempotents of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
are precisely the partitions of the form
e=e_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus e_{k}
, where each component
e_{i}
is connected and has rank
\leq 1
.

For example, one can check that the partitions
a,b\in\mathcal{P}_{6}
pictured in Figure
2
are both idempotents. Moreover,
a
is connected, and hence a single component of rank
1
, while
b
is the sum of two such components, the
\KER(b)
-classes being
\{1,2,3,4\}
and
\{5,6\}
. On the other hand,
ab
is not an idempotent; the
\KER(ab)
-classes are
\{1,4,5,6\}
and
\{2,3\}
, but
ab
is not the union of partitions from
\mathcal{P}_{\{1,4,5,6\}}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\{2,3\}}
.

The subsemigroup of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
generated by its idempotents was proved in
[
20
]
to consist of the identity partition, plus all partitions of rank
<n
; in other words,
\langle\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n})\rangle
is
\mathcal{P}_{n}
with the non-identity permutations from
\mathcal{S}_{n}
removed.
In terms of Green’s relations this semigroup is identical to
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, except for the very top
\mathscr{D}
-class, which is trivial. Therefore, in what follows we will work with
\mathcal{P}_{n}
instead of
\langle\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n})\rangle
as we would be required to, strictly-speaking, by the theory from Sections
5
–
7
.

8.3
A note on rectangular bands and singular squares in
\mathcal{P}_{n}

As well as the idempotents of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, we will also have to deal with singular squares formed by them.
We begin with a word of warning concerning the nature of singular squares in
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, in contrast with the situation for
\mathcal{T}_{n}
.

Towards the end of the proof of Proposition
5.4
, we recalled from
[
41
]
that every
2\times 2
rectangular band in
\mathcal{T}_{n}
is a singular square (and hence the
2\times 2
rectangular bands are precisely the non-degenerate singular squares in
\mathcal{T}_{n}
). It was also noted in
[
41
, Remark 2.7]
that every singular square in
\mathcal{T}_{n}
is horizontal (and possibly also vertical). It turns out that both of these special properties of
\mathcal{T}_{n}
fail in the partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}
.

For example, the elements
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e_{1}&f_{1}\\
g_{1}&h_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
of
\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{2})
pictured in Figure
7
(left) form a rectangular band, but this square is not singular. Indeed, it is easy to check that the only common left identity for these elements is
1
(cf. (
2.7
)), and that the only common right identity is also
1
(cf. (
2.8
)). And clearly
1
cannot singularise any non-degenerate square.

In fact, the square
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e_{1}&f_{1}\\
g_{1}&h_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
has the form
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}p&pq\\
qp&q\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
for the friendly pair of projections
{(p,q)=(e_{1},h_{1})}
in
\sfP(\mathcal{P}_{2})
. Such a square
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}p&pq\\
qp&q\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
exists for any friendly pair
(p,q)\in\mathscr{F}
in a regular
*
-semigroup
S
, but can only be singular if it is totally degenerate, meaning that
p=q=pq=qp
. Indeed, suppose this square
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}p&pq\\
qp&q\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
is LR-singularised by
u\in\sfE(S)
, so that:

(i)

up=p
,

(ii)

uqp=qp
,

(iii)

pu=pq
,

(iv)

qpu=q
.

We then have
p\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\ref{prosq1}}}}{{=}}up=uu^{*}up\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\ref{prosq1}}}}{{=}}uu^{*}p=u(pu)^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\ref{prosq3}}}}{{=}}u(pq)^{*}=uqp\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\ref{prosq2}}}}{{=}}qp
. It then follows that
p=p^{*}=(qp)^{*}=pq
, and since
p\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q
that
q=qpq=pq=p
.

It is still possible to find non-singular
2\times 2
rectangular bands in
\mathcal{P}_{n}
involving no projections. For example, this is the case for the elements
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e_{2}&f_{2}\\
g_{2}&h_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
of
\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{3})
pictured in Figure
7
(middle). Again, the only common left or right identity for these elements is
1
.

Regarding the failure in
\mathcal{P}_{n}
of the second special property of
\mathcal{T}_{n}
, i.e. that all singular squares are horizontal, Figure
7
(right) shows a square
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e_{3}&f_{3}\\
g_{3}&h_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
in
\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{3})
, and its UD-singularising element
u\in\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{3})
. This is not a horizontal square, however, as the only common left identity is again
1
. Of course, applying the involution yields a horizontal singular square that is not vertical.

The upshot of all of this is that when we want to establish singularity of a square in
\mathcal{P}_{n}
we must specify the singularising idempotent, in addition to the four idempotents forming the square.

e_{1}

and

. See Subsection

for more details.

8.4
NT-reducing squares and generator elimination

We now establish two types of singular squares, one general (Lemma
8.8
) and the other fairly special (Lemma
8.10
), which then let us eliminate all generators from the presentations for
\PG(P)
and
\IG(E)
, except for those of three particular kinds.
The squares of the first type will be used extensively in the subsequent sections as well.

For a partition
a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, let
\NTu(a)
and
\NTd(a)
respectively denote the numbers of upper and lower non-transversals of
a
, and let
\NT(a):=\NTu(a)+\NTd(a)
be the total number of non-transversals of
a
. If we write
\|\sigma\|
for the number of
\sigma
-classes for an equivalence relation
\sigma
, then

(8.2)

\NTu(a)=\|{\ker(a)}\|-\rank(a)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\NTd(a)=\|{\coker(a)}\|-\rank(a).

Note also that
0\leq\NTu(a)\leq n-r
for
a\in D(n,r)
, and that

\NTu(a)=0\ \Leftrightarrow\ \dom(a)=[n]\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\NTu(a)=n-r\ \Leftrightarrow\ \ker(a)=\Delta_{[n]}.

Analogous statements hold for
\NTd(a)
and codomains/cokernels. Also note that
\NTu(a),\NTd(a)\geq 1
when
r=0
.
Since Green’s
\mathscr{R}
- and
\mathscr{L}
-relations are governed by equality of (co)domains and (co)kernels (see Lemma
2.10
), it follows that

(8.3)

a\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}b\ \Rightarrow\ \NTu(a)=\NTu(b)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pta\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}b\ \Rightarrow\ \NTd(a)=\NTd(b).

If
p
is a projection then
\NTu(p)=\NTd(p)
.
We stratify
P(n,r)
according to the number of upper (equivalently, lower) blocks:

P_{k}=P_{k}(n,r):=\{p\in P(n,r)\colon\NTu(p)=k\}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for }0\leq k\leq n-r.

Remark 8.4
.

We can also use the
\NTu
and
\NTd
parameters to stratify the egg-box diagram of the entire
\mathscr{D}
-class
D(n,r)
, as shown in Figure
8
(left). For each
0\leq k,l\leq n-r
, it follows from (
8.3
) that the sets

D^{k}=D^{k}(n,r):=\{a\in D(n,r)\colon\NTu(a)=k\}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptD_{l}=D_{l}(n,r):=\{a\in D(n,r)\colon\NTd(a)=l\}

are unions of
\mathscr{R}
- and
\mathscr{L}
-classes, respectively. We call the sets
D^{k}
and
D_{l}
horizontal and vertical
strips
, respectively. The intersection of a horizontal and vertical strip,

D^{k}_{l}=D^{k}_{l}(n,r):=D^{k}(n,r)\cap D_{l}(n,r)=\{a\in D(n,r)\colon\NTu(a)=k,\ \NTd(a)=l\},

is a union of
\mathscr{H}
-classes. In Figure
8
, we have coloured certain sets of the above kinds. For example,
D_{0}^{0}
and
D_{1}^{1}
are blue and green, respectively. The rest of
D^{0}
and
D_{0}
are pink. The set
D^{0}_{n-r}=D(n,r)\cap\mathcal{T}_{n}
has been outlined in red. The further significance of these shaded regions will be explained in Remarks
8.12
and
9.3
.

Note that this stratification of
D(n,r)
induces one of
E(n,r)
as well. Moreover,

E^{k}_{l}(n,r):=E\cap D^{k}_{l}(n,r)

is non-empty for each
0\leq k,l\leq n-r
when
r\geq 1
, or for each
1\leq k,l\leq n-r
when
r=0
. For example, if
(n,r,k,l)=(10,5,4,2)
, then we have

\in E^{k}_{l}(n,r)
.

\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\ddots
\NTu
\NTd
0
1
2
n-r
0
1
2
n-r
\NTu
\NTd
l^{\prime}
l
k^{\prime}
k
e_{1}
e_{2}
e_{3}
e

Figure 8:
Left: the
\mathscr{D}
-class
D(n,r)
for
r\geq 1
, stratified by the
\NTu
and
\NTd
parameters; when
r=0
, the indexing starts at
1
; see Remarks
8.4
,
8.12
and
9.3
for more details.
Right: an NT-reducing singular square
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e_{1}&e_{2}\\
e_{3}&e\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
with base
e
; see Remark
8.7
for more details.

Our first goal is to show that our groups
G^{\sfE}
and
G^{\textsf{P}}
are generated by letters
a_{e}
corresponding to idempotents
e\in E(n,r)
with small
\NTu
and
\NTd
parameters; see Lemma
8.14
. The key idea behind this is the following:

Definition 8.5
.

We say that a singular square
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e_{1}&e_{2}\\
e_{3}&e\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\in\mathfrak{S}(n,r)
is
NT-reducing
with
base

e
if

\NTu(e_{2})<\NTu(e)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\NTd(e_{3})<\NTd(e).

Combined with (
8.3
), the above inequalities in fact yield

\NTu(e_{1})=\NTu(e_{2})<\NTu(e_{3})=\NTu(e)\hskip 10.00002pt\textup{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\NTd(e_{1})=\NTd(e_{3})<\NTd(e_{2})=\NTd(e),

which in turn yields

(8.6)

\NT(e_{i})<\NT(e)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for }i=1,2,3.

Consequently, every NT-reducing square is non-degenerate.

Remark 8.7
.

One can visualise an NT-reducing square
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e_{1}&e_{2}\\
e_{3}&e\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
using the stratification of the
\mathscr{D}
-class
D(n,r)
, as explained in Remark
8.4
. In the notation of that remark, we have

e\in E^{k}_{l}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pte_{1}\in E^{k^{\prime}}_{l^{\prime}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for some $0\leq k^{\prime}<k\leq n-r$ and $0\leq l^{\prime}<l\leq n-r$,}

and then we also have
e_{2}\in E^{k^{\prime}}_{l}
and
e_{3}\in E^{k}_{l^{\prime}}
. In particular,
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
and
e_{3}
are closer than
e
to the top-left corner of the
\mathscr{D}
-class
D(n,r)
. This is all shown in Figure
8
(right).

For an equivalence relation
\sigma
on a set
X
, with equivalence classes
A_{1},\ldots,A_{k}
,
let
\id_{\sigma}
denote the full-(co)domain projection
\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{k}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{k}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
in
\mathcal{P}_{X}
.
For any
a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, define the projections
{D(a):=\id_{\ker(a)}}
and
R(a):=\id_{\coker(a)}
.
Observe that

D(a)a=a=aR(a)
.
In fact, the
D
and
R
operations give
\mathcal{P}_{n}
the structure of an
Ehresmann semigroup
; see
[
25
]
.

Lemma 8.8
.

Let
e,f\in E(n,r)
be such that
e\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}f
and
\ker(e)\subseteq\ker(f)
.
Then

\begin{pmatrix}e_{1}&e_{2}\\
e_{3}&e\end{pmatrix}:=\begin{pmatrix}fD(e)&f\\
eD(e)&e\end{pmatrix}

is a singular square. Furthermore, if
\ker(e)\neq\ker(f)
and
\ker(e)\nsubseteq\coker(e)
then
the square is NT-reducing with base
e
.

Proof.

We have already noted that
D(e)e=e
; also,
D(e)f=f
, as
\ker(e)\subseteq\ker(f)
.
It therefore follows that
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e_{1}&e_{2}\\
e_{3}&e\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
is indeed an RL-square singularised by
D(e)
. This square is illustrated in Figure
9
.

When
\ker(e)\neq\ker(e_{2})
, we have
\ker(e)\subsetneq\ker(e_{2})
, so there are more
\ker(e)
-classes than
\ker(e_{2})
-classes. Since
\rank(e)=\rank(e_{2})
, we then deduce
\NTu(e)>\NTu(e_{2})
from (
8.2
).

If
\ker(e)\nsubseteq\coker(e)
, then

\coker(e_{3})=\coker(eD(e))=\coker(e)\vee\ker(e)\supsetneq\coker(e),

so it again follows from (
8.2
) that
\NTd(e)>\NTd(e_{3})
.
∎

The previous lemma has a dual, but we do not need to state it explicitly.

Remark 8.9
.

As a special instance of the square from Lemma
8.8
, we can take
f=R(e)e
. It follows from
eR(e)=e
that
e\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}f
, and we have
\ker(f)=\ker(R(e)e)=\coker(e)\vee\ker(e)\supseteq\ker(e)
. So we indeed have the square

\begin{pmatrix}R(e)eD(e)&R(e)e\\
eD(e)&e\end{pmatrix},

which is in fact RL- and DU-singularised by
D(e)
and
R(e)
, respectively. (This square is present in any Ehresmann semigroup.)
If
\ker(e)\not\subseteq\coker(e)
and
{\coker(e)\not\subseteq\ker(e)}
both hold, then the square is NT-reducing.

An example of the singular square featuring in Lemma
8.8
.

The rest of the results of this section are valid for
r\geq 1
. Analogues for
r=0
will be given when we eventually consider this case in Section
11
.

Lemma 8.10
.

If
e=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A&B&C\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A&B&C\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus f\in E(n,r)
, then
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e_{1}&e_{2}\\
e_{3}&e\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
is an NT-reducing singular square with base
e
, where

e_{1}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A\cup C&B\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A\cup B&C\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus f,\hskip 10.00002pte_{2}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A\cup C&\lx@intercol\hfil B\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A&B&C\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus f\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pte_{3}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A&B&C\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A\cup B&\lx@intercol\hfil C\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus f.

Proof.

The square is RL-singularised by
u=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A&B&C\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A\cup B&&C\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus f
, and is clearly NT-reducing; see Figure
10
.
∎
