Figure 10:
The NT-reducing singular square featuring in Lemma
8.10
; in each partition, the ‘
{}\oplus f
’ part has been omitted.

We now define a very special set of idempotents, still assuming that
r\geq 1
. Specifically, let
F(n,r)
be the set of all idempotents from
E(n,r)
that are full-domain or full-codomain or projections with precisely one upper (equivalently, lower) block:

(8.11)

F(n,r):=\bigl\{e\in E(n,r)\colon\NTu(e)=0\textup{ or }\NTd(e)=0\bigr\}\cup P_{1}(n,r).

Remark 8.12
.

The elements of
F=F(n,r)
are contained in the coloured regions in Figure
8
(left). The coloured horizontal and vertical strips contain the idempotents

E^{0}=E^{0}(n,r):=\bigl\{e\in E(n,r)\colon\NTu(e)=0\bigr\}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptE_{0}=E_{0}(n,r):=\bigl\{e\in E(n,r)\colon\NTd(e)=0\bigr\},

respectively. Their intersection
E^{0}\cap E_{0}
is the set
P_{0}
of all full-(co)domain projections; this is contained in the blue region,
D_{0}^{0}
. The set
P_{1}
is contained in the green region,
D_{1}^{1}
, which typically contains non-projection idempotents as well.
In fact, all
D_{i}^{i}
contain non-projection idempotents except when
i=0
or
i=n-r
.

The next proof refers to the components of an idempotent, as in Proposition
8.1
.

Lemma 8.13
.

If
1\leq r\leq n-2
, then every element of
E(n,r)\setminus F(n,r)

is the base of an NT-reducing singular square.

Proof.

Let
e\in E(n,r)\setminus F(n,r)
.
If
e
is a projection, then it must have at least two upper (and lower) blocks, and Lemma
8.10
applies.
So now suppose
e
is not a projection, i.e.
{\ker(e)\neq\coker(e)}
.
Without loss of generality assume that
\ker(e)\nsubseteq\coker(e)
.
By Lemma
8.8
it suffices to show that
there exists an idempotent
f\in E(n,r)
with
e\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}f
and
\ker(e)\subsetneq\ker(f)
.
As
\NTu(e)\neq 0
, there must exist an upper block
A
in
e
.
Let
B\cup C^{\prime}
be a transversal of
e
, chosen so that it belongs to the same component as
A
if possible (and is arbitrary otherwise).
We can now take
f
to be obtained from
e
, by replacing the blocks
A
and
B\cup C^{\prime}
by a single block
A\cup B\cup C^{\prime}
.
Proposition
8.1
can be used to show that
f
is indeed an idempotent. We have
e\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}f
(cf. Lemma
2.10
) and
\ker(e)\subsetneq\ker(f)
by construction.
∎

Lemma 8.14
.

Let
1\leq r\leq n-2
, and let
G
be a group defined by a presentation with generators
A(n,r)
, and defining relations that include all the square relations
\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}(n,r)
.
Then
G
is in fact generated by the set
A_{F(n,r)}
.

Proof.

Throughout the proof we write
F=F(n,r)
, and we must show that
a_{e}\in\langle A_{F}\rangle
for all
e\in E(n,r)
.
We prove this by induction on
\NT(e)
.
If
\NTu(e)=0
or
\NTd(e)=0
then
e\in F
, and there is nothing to prove.
This in particular includes the induction anchor
\NT(e)=0
.

Now suppose
\NTu(e)>0
and
\NTd(e)>0
, and assume without loss of generality that
e\not\in F
.
By Lemma
8.13
there exists an NT-reducing square
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e_{1}&e_{2}\\
e_{3}&e\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
with base
e
.
In particular, (
8.6
) gives
\NT(e_{i})<\NT(e)
for each
i
, so by induction each
a_{e_{i}}\in\langle A_{F}\rangle
.
By assumption, the singular square relation
a_{e_{1}}^{-1}a_{e_{2}}=a_{e_{3}}^{-1}a_{e}
holds in
G
. But then
a_{e}=a_{e_{3}}a_{e_{1}}^{-1}a_{e_{2}}\in\langle A_{F}\rangle
, and we are done.
∎

8.5
Labels

Now we introduce an auxiliary device of
labelling
the idempotents from
E(n,r)

by permutations from
\mathcal{S}_{r}
. It actually turns out that the label of
e\in E(n,r)
is the image of the generator
a_{e}
under the natural homomorphism from
G^{\textsf{P}}
or
G^{\sfE}
to
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, provided a suitable spanning tree is chosen, and the corresponding maximal subgroup in
\mathcal{P}_{n}
is appropriately identified with
\mathcal{S}_{r}
.
However, at this stage the reader need not concern themselves with this, and can regard the labelling as formalising a certain permutational aspect of each
e\in E(n,r)
.

All we say here is vacuous for
r=0
, so we assume that
r\geq 1
for the rest of this section.
Consider an arbitrary
e\in E(n,r)
. Suppose the transversals of
e
are
A_{1}\cup B_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,A_{r}\cup B_{r}^{\prime}
, and let
a_{i}:=\min(A_{i})
and
b_{i}:=\min(B_{i})
for each
i\in[r]
. Let
\lambda^{\prime}(e)
be the partial bijection
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a_{1}&\cdots&a_{r}\\
b_{1}&\cdots&b_{r}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. And then let
\lambda(e)
, the
label
of
e
, be the result of ‘scaling down’
\lambda^{\prime}(e)
to a permutation in
\mathcal{S}_{r}
, i.e. mapping each of the sets
\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{r}\}
and
\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{r}\}
in an order-preserving fashion onto
[r]
.

Diagrammatically, one obtains
\lambda^{\prime}(e)
by connecting the minimum elements in the top and bottom parts of each transversal of
e
. The label
\lambda(e)
is then obtained by deleting points that do not participate in transversals of
\lambda^{\prime}(e)
, and compressing the remaining diagram to the left. Here is an example for an idempotent
e\in E(5,3)
:

e=
\lambda^{\prime}(e)=
=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&2&5\\
4&2&3\end{smallmatrix}\right)
\lambda(e)=
=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&2&3\\
3&1&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

The idempotents
a,b\in\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{6})
in Figure
2
both have trivial label:
\lambda(a)=1\in\mathcal{S}_{1}
and
{\lambda(b)=1\in\mathcal{S}_{2}}
.

The main purpose of the labels for us is that they will enable us to ‘import’ certain technical results concerning the full transformation monoid
\mathcal{T}_{n}
from
[
41
]
.
Throughout we will identify
\mathcal{T}_{n}
with its natural copy inside
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, i.e. with the set of all partitions
with full domain and trivial cokernel; see Subsection
2.10
.
In
[
41
, Subsection 2.5]
a labelling function was defined for idempotents in
\mathcal{T}_{n}
, and it is easy to check that this is the restriction to
E(\mathcal{T}_{n})
of our labelling of
E(\mathcal{P}_{n})
.
The following facts are also clear.

Lemma 8.15
.

(i)

For every projection
p\in\sfP(\mathcal{P}_{n})
we have
\lambda(p)=1
.

(ii)

For every idempotent
e\in\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n})
we have
\lambda(e^{*})=\lambda(e)^{-1}
.∎

Next we list the results from
[
41
]
that will be used subsequently. We do so with slightly changed notation and terminology, to make them suitable for our purposes. Specifically, we will write:

•

D^{\mathcal{T}}(n,r):=\mathcal{T}_{n}\cap D(n,r)
for the
\mathscr{D}
-class of
\mathcal{T}_{n}
consisting of mappings of rank
r
,

•

E^{\mathcal{T}}(n,r):=\mathcal{T}_{n}\cap E(n,r)
for the set of idempotents in
D^{\mathcal{T}}(n,r)
,

•

A^{\mathcal{T}}(n,r):={\{a_{e}\colon e\in E^{\mathcal{T}}(n,r)\}}
for the set of generators from
A(n,r)
corresponding to idempotents in
\mathcal{T}_{n}
,

•

\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}^{\mathcal{T}}(n,r)
for the set of singular square relations involving only idempotents from
E^{\mathcal{T}}(n,r)
.

We also continue to write
T_{\textup{lex}}
for the spanning tree for the Graham–Houghton graph
\GH(D^{\mathcal{T}}(n,r))
, which was constructed in Section
5
.
We call an idempotent
e\in E(n,r)
a
Coxeter idempotent
if
\lambda(e)=(i,i+1)
for some
1\leq i\leq r-1
, i.e. if
\lambda(e)
is a Coxeter transposition.

Lemma 8.16
.

Let
G
be the group defined by the presentation
{\langle A^{\mathcal{T}}(n,r)\mid\mathcal{R}_{1}(T_{\textup{lex}}),\ \mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}^{\mathcal{T}}(n,r)\rangle}
, where
1\leq r\leq n-2
, and let
e,f\in E^{\mathcal{T}}(n,r)
.

(i)

If
\lambda(e)=1
then
a_{e}=1
in
G
.

(ii)

If
\lambda(e)=\lambda(f)
then
a_{e}=a_{f}
in
G
.

(iii)

If
e
is a Coxeter idempotent, then
a_{e}^{2}=1
in
G
.

(iv)

If
r\geq 2
, then
{a_{e}=a_{e_{1}}\cdots a_{e_{k}}}
in
G
for some Coxeter idempotents
e_{1},\ldots,e_{k}\in E^{\mathcal{T}}(n,r)
.

Proof.

Bearing Proposition
5.4
in mind, parts
(i)
and
(iii)
are precisely
[
41
, Lemma 4.3]
and
[
41
, Lemma 8.1]
, respectively.
Part
(ii)
is proved in
[
41
, Lemma 6.2]
for consecutive cycles, and the general statement follows by an easy induction based on
[
41
, Lemma 7.1]
.
Likewise, part

(iv)
can be obtained by induction based on

[
41
, Lemmas 6.5 and 7.1]
.
∎

In what follows we often need to check that
\lambda(e)=\lambda(f)
for idempotents
e,f\in E(n,r)
. One obvious way for this to happen is for
\lambda^{\prime}(e)
and
\lambda^{\prime}(f)
to be equal. It also happens when
\lambda^{\prime}(e)
and
\lambda^{\prime}(f)
differ in a small number of entries, by a small amount, so that the relative positions of entries in relation to each other do not change when passing from
\lambda^{\prime}
to
\lambda
. Here is one instance of this:

Lemma 8.17
.

If
e,f\in E(n,r)
are such that

\lambda^{\prime}(e)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}i_{1}&\cdots&i_{k-1}&i_{k}&i_{k+1}&\cdots&i_{r}\\
j_{1}&\cdots&j_{k-1}&j_{k}&j_{k+1}&\cdots&j_{r}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\lambda^{\prime}(f)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}i_{1}&\cdots&i_{k-1}&i&i_{k+1}&\cdots&i_{r}\\
j_{1}&\cdots&j_{k-1}&j&j_{k+1}&\cdots&j_{r}\end{smallmatrix}\right),

where
i\in\{i_{k},i_{k}\pm 1\}
and
j\in\{j_{k},j_{k}\pm 1\}
, then
\lambda(e)=\lambda(f)
.
∎

8.6
The tree
T_{\textup{fd}}

Our ability to link up with the results from
[
41
]
cited in Lemma
8.16
will depend on a ‘good’ choice of a spanning tree
T
of the Graham–Houghton graph
\GH(D(n,r))

for
r\geq 1
.
Different spanning trees will be used in Sections
9
and
10
below, but they will have a certain subset in common, which we specify now.
When we come to deal with the case
r=0
in Section
11
we will make use of an entirely different spanning tree.

In what follows, we will take the vertices of
\GH(D(n,r))
to be
I\sqcup J
, where

I:=\{i_{p}\colon p\in P(n,r)\}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptJ:=\{j_{p}\colon p\in P(n,r)\}

are two disjoint copies of
P(n,r)
, respectively indexing the
\mathscr{R}
- and
\mathscr{L}
-classes in
D(n,r)
. This is simply to avoid the notational difficulties arising from the fact that normally we use the same set for both, namely
P(n,r)
itself. Furthermore, we partition these sets as

I=I_{0}\cup\cdots\cup I_{n-r}\and J=J_{0}\cup\cdots\cup J_{n-r},

where

I_{k}:=\big\{i_{p}\colon p\in P_{k}(n,r)\big\}\and J_{k}:=\big\{j_{p}\colon p\in P_{k}(n,r)\big\}.

(Recall that
P_{k}=P_{k}(n,r)=\{p\in P(n,r)\colon\NTu(p)=k\}
.)

Let us start with the spanning tree
T_{\textup{lex}}
for the Graham–Houghton graph
\GH(D^{\mathcal{T}}(n,r))
, introduced in Section
5
; see Proposition
5.4
and Figure
3
. According to our interpretation of
\mathcal{T}_{n}
as partitions of full domain and trivial cokernel,
T_{\textup{lex}}
is a spanning tree for the subgraph of
\GH(D(n,r))
induced on
I_{0}\cup J_{n-r}
.

We now add some edges to
T_{\textup{lex}}
.
For a projection
{p=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&B_{1}&\cdots&B_{k}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&B_{1}&\cdots&B_{k}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\in P(n,r)}
with
\min(A_{1})<\cdots<\min(A_{r})
, define

(8.18)

e_{p}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}\cup B_{1}\cup\cdots\cup B_{k}&A_{2}&\cdots&A_{r}&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&A_{2}&\cdots&A_{r}&B_{1}&\cdots&B_{k}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr).

Note that
e_{p}\in E(n,r)
has full-domain; considered as an edge in
\GH(D(n,r))
, it connects some
i\in I_{0}
to

j_{p}\in J_{k}
.
Hence the set

(8.19)

T_{\textup{fd}}=T_{\textup{fd}}(n,r):=T_{\textup{lex}}\cup\{e_{p}\colon p\in P_{1}\cup\cdots\cup P_{n-r-1}\}

is a spanning tree for the subgraph of
\GH(D(n,r))
induced on
I_{0}\cup(J_{1}\cup J_{2}\cup\cdots\cup J_{n-r})
. A schematic diagram of the tree
T_{\textup{fd}}
is shown in Figure
11
.

I_{0}
I_{1}
I_{n-r-1}
I_{n-r}
J_{0}
J_{1}
J_{n-r-1}
J_{n-r}

Figure 11:
Schematic diagram of the tree
T_{\textup{fd}}
from (
8.19
), which is a spanning tree for the induced subgraph of the Graham–Houghton graph
\GH(D(n,r))
on vertices
I_{0}\cup(J_{1}\cup J_{2}\cup\cdots\cup J_{n-r})
, in the case
r\geq 1
. The edges from
T_{\textup{lex}}
are contained in the pink region (with vertices
I_{0}\cup J_{n-r}
), and the remaining edges are in the blue region.

The following result is key for our link between
\IG(\sfE(\mathcal{T}_{n}))
on the one hand, and
\PG(\sfP(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
and
\IG(\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
on the other:

Lemma 8.20
.

For every non-projection idempotent
e\in E(n,r)
with full domain, there exists an idempotent transformation
f\in E^{\mathcal{T}}(n,r)
such that

\lambda(e)=\lambda(f)
.
Furthermore, if
{T_{\textup{fd}}\subseteq T\subseteq E(n,r)}
, then the idempotent
f
can be chosen so that
a_{e}=a_{f}
is a consequence of
\presn(T)
.

Proof.

When
e\in\mathcal{T}_{n}
there is nothing to prove, so suppose instead that
e\not\in\mathcal{T}_{n}
, and write
e=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{k}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
. Since
e
is an idempotent, we have
B_{i}\subseteq A_{i}
for each
i\in[r]
. Let
b_{i}=\min(B_{i})
for each
i
, assuming that
b_{1}<\cdots<b_{r}
, and also pick arbitrary
c_{i}\in C_{i}
for
i\in[k]
. Define another three idempotents:

f:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil b_{1}&\cdots&b_{r}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr),\hskip 10.00002ptg:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}\cup C_{1}\cup\cdots\cup C_{k}&B_{2}&\cdots&B_{r}&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&B_{2}&\cdots&B_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{k}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pth:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}\cup C_{1}\cup\cdots\cup C_{k}&B_{2}&\cdots&B_{r}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil b_{1}&b_{2}&\cdots&b_{r}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr).

Note that we are using the convention of omitting lower singletons in
f
and
h
. Note also that
g=e_{q}
for the projection
q=e^{*}e=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{k}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{k}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
.
We claim that
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
is a singular square, RL-singularised by

u:=R(e)=\id_{\coker(e)}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{k}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{k}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr).

For an illustration see Figure
12
.
Indeed, it is straightforward to verify that
f
,
g
and
h
are idempotents, and that the appropriate
\mathscr{R}
- and
\mathscr{L}
-relationships hold. The equality
uh=h
is easily checked, while
uf=f
follows from the observation that the typical component of
e
(see Proposition
8.1
) has the form
\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{i}&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{i}&C_{j_{1}}&\cdots&C_{j_{l}}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
for some
l\geq 0
and
j_{1},\ldots,j_{l}\in[k]
. The equalities
fu=e
and
hu=g
hold because
b_{i}\in B_{i}
for each
i\in[r]
.

Since
e
is neither a projection nor a transformation, it follows that
1\leq k\leq n-r-1
, and so
q
(as above) belongs to
P_{1}\cup\cdots\cup P_{n-r-1}
, and hence
g=e_{q}\in T_{\textup{fd}}\subseteq T
,
so the relation
a_{g}=1
is in
\presn(T)
.
Also notice that
f,h\in\mathcal{T}_{n}
, with
\lambda(f)=\lambda(e)
, and
\lambda(h)=1
. (For the latter, note that
\min(B_{1}\cup C_{1}\cup\cdots\cup C_{k})=1
.)
Recalling that
T_{\textup{fd}}
, and hence
T
, contains the tree
T_{\textup{lex}}
, Lemma
8.16
(i)

implies that

a_{h}=1
is a consequence of
\presn(T)
. The square
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
yields the relation

a_{e}^{-1}a_{f}=a_{g}^{-1}a_{h}
from
\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}
, which is present in
\presn(T)
,
and hence
a_{e}=a_{f}
as required.
∎

e
f
E
g
h
E
u=R(e)

Figure 12:
The singular square featuring in the proof of Lemma
8.20
.

9
Maximal subgroups of
\PG(\sfP(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
, the case of rank
\geq 1

We have now reached the point in our treatment of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
where we can identify the maximal subgroups of
\PG(\sfP(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
corresponding to projections of rank
1\leq r\leq n-2
, and thus prove the main part of Theorem
1.1
(the case
r=0
was Proposition
6.47
):

Theorem 9.1
.

Let
n\geq 3
, and let
p_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
be a projection of rank
1\leq r\leq n-2
.
Then the maximal subgroup of
\PG(\sfP(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
containing
x_{p_{0}}
is isomorphic to the symmetric group
\mathcal{S}_{r}
.

Proof.

We will work with the presentation given in Corollary
7.22
.
For this we need to specify a spanning tree for the Graham–Houghton graph
\GH(D(n,r))
.
We start with the set
T_{\textup{fd}}
, which was constructed in Subsection
8.6
as an extension of
T_{\textup{lex}}
to
I_{0}\cup(J_{1}\cup\cdots\cup J_{n-r})
; see (
8.19
).
We expand
T_{\textup{fd}}
to

T:=T_{\textup{fd}}\cup P(n,r)
.
This is easily seen to be a spanning tree for
\GH(D(n,r))
; see Figure
13
for a schematic diagram.
By
Corollary
7.22
, the maximal subgroup
G^{\textsf{P}}
of
\PG(P(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
containing
x_{p_{0}}
is defined by the presentation

\langle A(n,r)\mid\mathcal{R}_{1}(T),\ \mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}(n,r),\ \mathcal{R}_{\textsf{inv}}(n,r)\rangle.

By Lemma
8.14
,
G^{\textsf{P}}
is generated by the set
A_{F}=\{a_{e}\colon e\in F\}
, where
F=F(n,r)
is as in (
8.11
). Note that
F
decomposes as
F=F_{1}\cup F_{2}\cup P_{0}\cup P_{1}
, where

(9.2)

F_{1}:=\{e\in E(n,r)\colon\NTu(e)=0<\NTd(e)\}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptF_{2}:=\{e\in E(n,r)\colon\NTd(e)=0<\NTu(e)\}.

For
e\in P_{0}\cup P_{1}
, the relation
a_{e}=1
is contained in
\mathcal{R}_{1}(T)
,
while for
e\in F_{2}
the relation
a_{e}=a_{e^{*}}^{-1}
is in
\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{inv}}
, and we have
e^{*}\in F_{1}
.
Thus all generators
a_{e}
with
e\in E(n,r)\setminus F_{1}

can be eliminated in terms of generators
a_{f}
with
f\in F_{1}
.
Furthermore, since
T_{\textup{fd}}\subseteq T
,
for every such
f
we have
a_{f}=a_{f^{\prime}}
for some
f^{\prime}\in\sfE(\mathcal{T}_{n})
by Lemma
8.20
.
Denoting by
A^{\prime}
the alphabet
\big\{a_{h}\colon h\in\mathcal{T}_{n}\cap E(n,r)\big\}
,
we see that for every
a_{e}\in A(n,r)\setminus A^{\prime}
there exists a word
w_{e}\in(A^{\prime})^{+}

such that
a_{e}=w_{e}
. We also define
w_{e}=a_{e}
for
e\in A^{\prime}
.
Performing the generator elimination then yields
the presentation
\langle A^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{R}\rangle
for
G^{\textsf{P}}
, where
\mathcal{R}
is obtained by substituting every instance of every
a_{e}
by
w_{e}
in all relations from

\mathcal{R}_{1}(T)\cup\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}(n,r)\cup\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{inv}}(n,r)
.
This set
\mathcal{R}
of defining relations contains
\mathcal{R}_{1}(T_{\textup{lex}})
, as well as the
set
\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}^{\prime}(n,r)
consisting of all square relations from
\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}(n,r)
involving solely idempotents from
\mathcal{T}_{n}
.
In other words,
G^{\textsf{P}}
is defined by
\langle A^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{R}_{1}(T_{\textup{lex}}),\ \mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}^{\prime}(n,r),\ \mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime}\rangle
, for some set of relations
\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime}
.
By Proposition
5.4
, the presentation
\langle A^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{R}_{1}(T_{\textup{lex}}),\ \mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}^{\prime}(n,r)\rangle
defines the symmetric group
\mathcal{S}_{r}
.
Thus
G^{\textsf{P}}
is a homomorphic image of
\mathcal{S}_{r}
.
On the other hand,
G^{\textsf{P}}
has the maximal subgroup of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
containing
p_{0}
as a homomorphic image, and this latter group is also isomorphic to
\mathcal{S}_{r}
(cf. Lemma
2.10
). Therefore
G^{\textsf{P}}\cong\mathcal{S}_{r}
, and the theorem is proved.
∎

Remark 9.3
.

The sets
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
from (
9.2
) are contained in the horizontal and vertical pink regions in Figure
8
, respectively. The set
\mathcal{T}_{n}\cap E(n,r)
– corresponding to the final generating set
A^{\prime}
– consists of the idempotents in the region
D^{0}_{n-r}(n,r)
, outlined in red in Figure
8
.

I_{0}
I_{1}
I_{n-r-1}
I_{n-r}
J_{0}
J_{1}
J_{n-r-1}
J_{n-r}

Figure 13:
Schematic diagram of the tree
T=T_{\textup{fd}}\cup P(n,r)
from the proof of Theorem
9.1
, which is a spanning tree for the Graham–Houghton graph
\GH(D(n,r))
, in the case
r\geq 1
. The edges from
T_{\textup{fd}}
are indicated in grey, and are contained in the blue region (with vertices
I_{0}\cup(J_{1}\cup J_{2}\cup\cdots\cup J_{n-r})
), while the edges from
P(n,r)
are indicated in black.

10
Maximal subgroups in
\IG(\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
, the case of rank
\geq 1

This section comprises the most difficult argument in the paper, namely the computation of the maximal subgroups of
\IG(E)
, where
E:=\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n})
.
Specifically, we prove the following, with the
r=0
case being deferred to Section
11
:

Theorem 10.1
.

Let
n\geq 3
, and let
e_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
be an idempotent of rank
1\leq r\leq n-2
. Then the maximal subgroup of
\IG(\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
containing
x_{e_{0}}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathcal{S}_{r}
.

The basic idea of the proof is the same as in the previous section, i.e. a reduction to
\IG(\sfE(\mathcal{T}_{n}))
.
However, a major difference here is that the end result is
not

\mathcal{S}_{r}
, the corresponding maximal subgroup of
\IG(\sfE(\mathcal{T}_{n}))
, but rather
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathcal{S}_{r}
. Consequently, we also need to account for the direct factor
\mathbb{Z}
. Since the argument to follow is rather involved, we provide the following ‘road map’ for the convenience of the reader.

•

We begin in Subsection
10.1
by identifying a suitable spanning tree
T(s)
, from which to build a presentation
\presn(T(s))
for our group
G^{\sfE}\cong\sfG(T(s))
. This tree contains certain non-projection full-domain or -codomain idempotents with label
1
, plus an arbitrary single projection
{s\in P_{0}}
.

•

In Subsection
10.2
we identify a large set
O=O(n,r)
of idempotents
e
of label
1
for which the relation
a_{e}=1
holds in our group
\sfG(T(s))
. It follows that
G^{\sfE}
is isomorphic to
\sfG(O\cup\{s\})
, the group defined by the larger presentation
\presn(O\cup\{s\})
. Removing the relation
a_{s}=1
leaves us with a group
\widehat{G}(n,r)=\sfG(O)
, which (when
r\leq n-3
) contains many natural copies of
\widehat{G}(n-1,r)
. This is crucial, as it enables inductive proofs of the technical results of the next two subsections.

•

By Lemma
8.14
, the group
\sfG(O)
is generated by the set
A_{F}
, where
F=P_{0}\cup P_{1}\cup F_{1}\cup F_{2}
is as in (
8.11
); cf. (
9.2
). In Subsection
10.3
we show that all generators corresponding to projections from
P_{1}
are equal in
\sfG(O)
. Subsection
10.4
does the same for
P_{0}
. Bringing the relation
a_{s}=1
back in, it follows that our group is generated by
A_{F_{1}\cup F_{2}\cup\{t\}}
for any fixed
t\in P_{1}
.

•

In Subsection
10.5
we show that certain generators from
A_{F_{2}}
are equal to corresponding generators from
A_{F_{1}}
. Combining this with earlier results will ultimately allow us to eliminate
all
generators from
A_{F_{2}}
, so that our group
\sfG(O\cup\{s\})
is generated by
A_{F_{1}\cup\{t\}}
. Bringing in additional earlier results allows us to replace
F_{1}\cup\{t\}
with the set
E^{\prime}=E^{\mathcal{T}}(n,r)\cup\{t\}
consisting of
t
and all idempotent transformations of rank
r
. By results of
[
41
]
, the generators corresponding to
E^{\mathcal{T}}(n,r)
satisfy the defining relations of the symmetric group
\mathcal{S}_{r}
.

•

In Subsection
10.6
we show that every generator
a_{e}
with
e\in E^{\mathcal{T}}(n,r)
commutes with
a_{t}
(for our fixed
t\in P_{1}
). Ultimately this will allow us to deduce that our group
G^{\sfE}
is a homomorphic image of
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathcal{S}_{r}
: the subgroup
\langle A_{E^{\mathcal{T}}(n,r)}\rangle
is a quotient of
\mathcal{S}_{r}
(as per the previous point), while the subgroup
\langle a_{t}\rangle
is cyclic, i.e. a quotient of
\mathbb{Z}
, and the generators of these two subgroups commute.

•

In Subsection
10.7
we connect with the (
\mathbb{Z}
-)twisted partition monoid
\mathcal{P}^{\Phi}_{n}
, noting that results of
[
23
]
– including a biordered set isomorphism
\sfE(\mathcal{P}^{\Phi}_{n})\cong\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n})
– imply that our group
G^{\sfE}
is also a homomorphic
pre
-image of
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathcal{S}_{r}
.

•

The proof is rounded off in Subsection
10.8
, where we note that Hopficity of
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathcal{S}_{r}

[
47
]
implies that indeed
G^{\sfE}\cong\mathbb{Z}\times\mathcal{S}_{r}
.

10.1
A spanning tree

In what follows we will work with different presentations, involving generators that are subsets of
E(n,r)
and relations involving the square relations and some relations of the form
a_{e}=1
. As before, for any subset
F\subseteq E(n,r)
, we write

•

\presn(F)
for the presentation
\langle A(n,r)\mid\mathcal{R}_{1}(F),\ \mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}(n,r)\rangle
, and

•

\sfG(F)
for the group defined by
\presn(F)
.

By Theorem
4.13
, the maximal subgroup
G^{\sfE}
of
\IG(\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
containing

x_{e_{0}}
is isomorphic to the group
\sfG(T)
for any spanning tree
T
of the Graham–Houghton graph
\GH(D(n,r))
.
We are going to pick a specific spanning tree below, but we will also work with sets of idempotents
F
that do not correspond to spanning trees.

As in the previous section, we start from the set
T_{\textup{fd}}
constructed in Subsection
8.6
, which is a spanning tree for the subgraph of
\GH(D(n,r))
induced on
{I_{0}\cup(J_{1}\cup J_{2}\cup\cdots\cup J_{n-r})}
; see (
8.19
). Recall that
T_{\textup{fd}}
contains the spanning tree
T_{\textup{lex}}
for the Graham–Houghton graph
\GH(D^{\mathcal{T}}(n,r))
. Applying the involution
∗
, the set

(10.2)

T_{\textup{fc}}=T_{\textup{fc}}(n,r):=T_{\textup{fd}}^{*}=T_{\textup{lex}}^{*}\cup\{e_{p}^{*}\colon p\in P_{1}\cup\cdots\cup P_{n-r-1}\}

is a spanning tree for the subgraph of
\GH(D(n,r))
induced on
(I_{1}\cup\cdots\cup I_{n-r})\cup J_{0}
. Consequently,
T_{\textup{fd}}\cup T_{\textup{fc}}
has two connected components, so adding
any
single edge between the two components will yield a spanning tree.
We will specifically choose this additional edge
s
to be a full-(co)domain projection, and denote the resulting tree by:

(10.3)

T(s):=T_{\textup{fd}}\cup T_{\textup{fc}}\cup\{s\}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $s\in P_{0}=P_{0}(n,r)$.}

A schematic diagram of this tree is shown in Figure
14
.

Lemma 10.4
.

For any full-domain projection
s\in P_{0}(n,r)
, the group
G^{\sfE}
is defined by the presentation

\presn(T(s))=\bigl\langle A(n,r)\mid\mathcal{R}_{1}(T(s)),\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}(n,r)\bigr\rangle.

In other words,
G^{\sfE}\cong\sfG(T(s))
.

Proof.

This is just the presentation given by Theorem
4.13
stated in terms of the specific spanning tree
T(s)
we have just built.
∎

I_{0}
I_{1}
I_{n-r-1}
I_{n-r}
J_{0}
J_{1}
J_{n-r-1}
J_{n-r}
s
i_{s}
j_{s}

Figure 14:
Schematic diagram of the tree
T(s)=T_{\textup{fd}}\cup T_{\textup{fc}}\cup\{s\}
from (
10.3
), which is a spanning tree for the Graham–Houghton graph
\GH(D(n,r))
in the case
r\geq 1
. The edges from
T_{\textup{fd}}
and
T_{\textup{fc}}
are indicated in grey, and are contained in the blue and pink regions, respectively, and the edge
s
is indicated in red.

10.2
Generators with label
1

We now prove a sequence of results involving the labels introduced in Subsection
8.5
. We start with Lemma
10.5
, which observes that all elements of the spanning tree
T(s)
in (
10.3
) have label
1
, and then build to Lemma
10.8
, which says that we can add the relations
a_{e}=1
to the presentation
\presn(T(s))
for
many
more idempotents
e
with label
\lambda(e)=1
.

Lemma 10.5
.

All the idempotents in the spanning tree
T(s)
have label
1
.

Proof.

Consider an arbitrary
e\in T(s)
. We split the proof into several cases, depending on the form of
e
,
as indicated in (
10.3
).

Case 1:
e=e_{V,\textup{{C}}(V)}\in T_{\textup{lex}}
, where
V=\{V_{1},\ldots,V_{r}\}
is a partition of
[n]
with
r
blocks.
Letting
v_{k}=\min(V_{k})
for
k\in[r]
, we have

e=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil V_{1}&\cdots&V_{r}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil v_{1}&\cdots&v_{r}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)

(omitting the trivial blocks)
so that

\lambda^{\prime}(e)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}v_{1}&\cdots&v_{r}\\
v_{1}&\cdots&v_{r}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, and hence

\lambda(e)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&\cdots&r\\
1&\cdots&r\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

Case 2:
e=e_{\textup{{V}}(C),C}\in T_{\textup{lex}}
, where
C=\{c_{1}<\cdots<c_{r}\}\subseteq[n]
.

In this case we have

e=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil[1,c_{1}]&(c_{1},c_{2}]&\cdots&(c_{r-2},c_{r-1}]&(c_{r-1},n]\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil c_{1}&c_{2}&\cdots&c_{r-1}&c_{r}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr),

so that

\lambda^{\prime}(e)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&c_{1}+1&\cdots&c_{r-2}+1&c_{r-1}+1\\
c_{1}&c_{2}&\cdots&c_{r-1}&c_{r}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, and hence

\lambda(e)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&\cdots&r\\
1&\cdots&r\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

Case 3:
e=e_{p}
, where
p\in P_{k}
for some
1\leq k\leq n-r-1
.
With notation as in (
8.18
), let
a_{k}:=\min(A_{k})
for
1\leq k\leq r
, and note that
\min(A_{1}\cup B_{1}\cup\cdots\cup B_{k})=1
. It follows that
\lambda^{\prime}(e)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&a_{2}&\cdots&a_{r}\\
a_{1}&a_{2}&\cdots&a_{r}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, and hence
\lambda(e)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&\cdots&r\\
1&\cdots&r\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

Case 4:
e\in T_{\textup{fc}}
.

This case follows from Cases 1–3, using Lemma
8.15
(ii)
.

Case 5:
e=s
.

This follows from Lemma
8.15
(i)
.
∎

In what follows we will in fact want to work with a set of relations that is larger than
\mathcal{R}_{1}(T(s))
,
and we now work towards enabling that. We begin by identifying certain sets of idempotents:

•

O_{\textup{fd}}=O_{\textup{fd}}(n,r):=\bigl\{e\in E(n,r)\colon\lambda(e)=1\text{ and }\NTu(e)=0<\NTd(e)\bigr\}
– the set of full-domain, non-projection idempotents with label
1
;

•

O_{\textup{fc}}=O_{\textup{fc}}(n,r):=\bigl\{e\in E(n,r)\colon\lambda(e)=1\text{ and }\NTd(e)=0<\NTu(e)\bigr\}
– the set of full-codomain, non-projection idempotents with label
1
;

•

O=O(n,r):=O_{\textup{fd}}(n,r)\cup O_{\textup{fc}}(n,r)
.

Lemma
10.4
says that the maximal subgroup
G^{\sfE}
under consideration is defined by the presentation
\presn(T(s))
.
Shortly we are going to see that the presentation
\presn(T(s))
is equivalent to
\presn(O\cup\{s\})
, so that
G^{\sfE}
also has presentation
\presn(O\cup\{s\})
.
In fact, much of what we will prove does not use the defining relation
a_{s}=1
, and
we will prefer to work with the slightly smaller presentation
\presn(O)
. This is to facilitate the inductive proofs of Lemmas
10.25
and
10.37
below; see Remark
10.26
.
The group defined by
\presn(O)
is typically not isomorphic to
G^{\sfE}
, but only a homomorphic preimage of it.

Lemma 10.6
.

Let
e,f\in E(n,r)
be any non-projection idempotents with full domain.

(i)

If
\lambda(e)=\lambda(f)
then
a_{e}=a_{f}
is a consequence of
\presn(T_{\textup{fd}})
.

(ii)

If
\lambda(e)=1
then
a_{e}=1
is a consequence of
\presn(T_{\textup{fd}})
.

Proof.

(i)

By Lemma
8.20
, there exist idempotent transformations
e^{\prime},f^{\prime}\in E^{\mathcal{T}}(n,r)
such that
\lambda(e^{\prime})=\lambda(e)
and
\lambda(f^{\prime})=\lambda(f)
,
and such that the relations
a_{e}=a_{e^{\prime}}
and
a_{f}=a_{f^{\prime}}
are consequences of
\presn(T_{\textup{fd}})
.
The relation
a_{e^{\prime}}=a_{f^{\prime}}
is a consequence of
\presn(T_{\textup{fd}})
by Lemma
8.16
(ii)
.

(ii)

This follows from
(i)
, keeping in mind Lemma
10.5
.
∎

Lemma
10.6
has an obvious dual, which we state without proof:

Lemma 10.7
.

Let
e,f\in E(n,r)
be any non-projection idempotents with full codomain.

(i)

If
\lambda(e)=\lambda(f)
then
a_{e}=a_{f}
is a consequence of
\presn(T_{\textup{fc}})
.

(ii)

If
\lambda(e)=1
then
a_{e}=1
is a consequence of
\presn(T_{\textup{fc}})
. ∎

We can now achieve our above-mentioned goal:

Lemma 10.8
.

For any
1\leq r\leq n-2
, and any
s\in P_{0}(n,r)
, the group
G^{\sfE}
is defined by the presentation

\presn(O\cup\{s\})=\bigl\langle A(n,r)\mid\mathcal{R}_{1}(O\cup\{s\}),\ \mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}(n,r)\bigr\rangle.

In other words
G^{\sfE}\cong\sfG(O\cup\{s\})
.

Proof.

By Lemma
10.4
, the group
G^{\sfE}
is defined by the presentation
\presn(T(s))
.
By Lemmas
10.6
(ii)
and
10.7
(ii)
, all the relations
\mathcal{R}_{1}(O)=\mathcal{R}_{1}(O_{\textup{fd}}\cup O_{\textup{fc}})
can be added to
\presn(T(s))
.
Since
{T(s)\subseteq O\cup\{s\}}
by Lemma
10.5
, it follows that the resulting presentation
is precisely
{\presn(O\cup\{s\})}
.
∎

Remark 10.9
.

In the group
\sfG(O\cup\{s\})
, we of course have
a_{s}=1
, for the fixed projection
{s\in P_{0}}
. For the other projections
p\in P_{0}\setminus\{s\}
, it is not clear at this stage that we necessarily have
a_{p}=1
in
\sfG(O\cup\{s\})
, but this is indeed the case. It takes a lot more work to prove this, but we eventually will in Lemma
10.38
.

10.3
Generators corresponding to projections from
P_{1}(n,r)

Our next task is to show that all the generators corresponding to projections from
P_{1}(n,r)
are equal to each other as a consequence of our defining relations. This is achieved in Lemma
10.25
below. The proof of this lemma is inductive, based on the case
r=n-2
, which is dealt with in Lemma
10.24
. By the nature of the induction (see Remark
10.26
), we are forced to omit the relation
a_{s}=1
(for the fixed projection
s\in P_{0}
), and work with the group
\sfG(O)
, where
O=O(n,r)
was constructed in the previous subsection. In order that we can conveniently vary our parameters, we denote this group by
\widehat{G}(n,r)=\sfG(O)=\sfG(O(n,r))
. It is worth noting that
O
contains
T_{\textup{fd}}\cup T_{\textup{fc}}
; indeed, the elements of the latter set are non-projections by definition, and they have label
1
by Lemma
10.5
.

To aid our considerations we remark that, when
r=n-2
, Figure
8
(discussed in Remark
8.4
) simplifies to a
3\times 3
grid, as shown in Figure
15
.
The set of projections in
D(n,n-2)
decomposes as
P(n,n-2)=P_{0}\cup P_{1}\cup P_{2}
, with each subset
P_{k}
(
k=0,1,2
) contained in the region
D_{k}^{k}
. As we noted in Remark
8.12
,
P_{0}
(resp.
P_{2}
) consists of
all
idempotents from
D_{0}^{0}

(resp.
D_{2}^{2}
), but
D_{1}^{1}
contains non-projection idempotents.

\NTu
\NTd
0
1
2
0
1
2
P_{0}
P_{1}
P_{2}

Figure 15:
The simplification of Figure
8
for the case
r=n-2
.

We begin with some quite technical lemmas.

Lemma 10.10
.

Suppose
1\leq r=n-2
, let
i,j,k\in[n]
be distinct, let
Y:=[n]\setminus\{i,j,k\}
, and let

e:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j&k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j&\lx@intercol\hfil k\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptp:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j&k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j&k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y}.

If
i\leq j+1
then
a_{e}=a_{p}
in
\widehat{G}(n,r)
.

Proof.

Consider the three further idempotents:

e_{1}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j&k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j,k&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y},\hskip 10.00002pte_{2}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j&k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j,k&\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptu:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j&k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j,k&\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y}.

These idempotents are shown pictorially in Figure
16
.
Routine computations show that
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&e_{1}\\
p&e_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
is an LR square, singularised by
u
, i.e. that

e\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}e_{1},\hskip 20.00003ptp\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}e_{2},\hskip 20.00003pte\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}p\and e_{1}\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}e_{2},

and also

ue=e,\hskip 20.00003ptup=p,\hskip 20.00003pteu=e_{1}\and pu=e_{2}.

The
\mathscr{R}
- and
\mathscr{L}
-relationships can be inferred by checking that (co)domains and (co)kernels match as appropriate, and applying Lemma
2.10
. The required equalities are verified in Figure
16
.

Next we note that the idempotents
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
are full-codomain non-projections,
and we claim that
\lambda(e_{1})=\lambda(e_{2})
.
To see this, write
x\wedge y
for
\min(x,y)
, and note that

\lambda^{\prime}(e_{1})=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\phantom{j}i\phantom{j}\\
i\wedge j\wedge k\end{smallmatrix}\right)\oplus\id_{Y}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\lambda^{\prime}(e_{2})=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}i\wedge j\\
i\wedge j\wedge k\end{smallmatrix}\right)\oplus\id_{Y}.

Since
i\leq j+1
, the values
i
and
i\wedge j
are either equal or consecutive, and the claim follows by Lemma
8.17
.

Now Lemma
10.7
gives
a_{e_{1}}=a_{e_{2}}
in
\sfG(T_{\textup{fc}})
. Since
T_{\textup{fc}}\subseteq O
, it follows that the equality
a_{e_{1}}=a_{e_{2}}
also holds in
\sfG(O)=\widehat{G}(n,r)
.
Combining this with the square relation
a_{e}^{-1}a_{e_{1}}=a_{p}^{-1}a_{e_{2}}
yields
a_{e}=a_{p}
, as required.
∎

While in the above lemma and the accompanying Figure
16
we have shown all the calculations needed to establish the required singular squares, from now on we will simply present the participating idempotents, and leave the actual checks to the reader.

.

\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&e_{1}\\

\mathscr{D}

.

a_{p}=a_{e}

P_{1}\subseteq E_{1}^{1}
and
E_{1}^{2}
, but the square also features idempotents from
E_{0}^{1}
and
E_{0}^{2}
.
This is illustrated in Figure
18
(left).

Lemma 10.12
.

Suppose
1\leq r=n-2
, let
i,j,k\in[n]
be distinct, let
Y:=[n]\setminus\{i,j,k\}
, and let

p:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j,k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j,k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptq:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j&k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j&k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y}.

If
i\leq j+1
then
a_{p}=a_{q}
in
\widehat{G}(n,r)
.

Proof.

Consider the following further idempotents:

\displaystyle e

\displaystyle:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j&k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&\lx@intercol\hfil j,k\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y},

\displaystyle e_{1}

\displaystyle:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j&k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j&\lx@intercol\hfil k\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y},

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,k&j\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j,k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y},

\displaystyle f_{1}

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,k&j\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j&k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y},

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j,k&\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j,k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y},

\displaystyle g_{1}

\displaystyle:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j,k&\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j&k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y},

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j&k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j,k&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y},

\displaystyle h_{1}

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j,k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j,k&\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y}.

It is routine to verify that:

\displaystyle\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&e_{1}\\
f&f_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)

is LR-singularised by

\displaystyle u=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j&k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j&&k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y},

yielding

\displaystyle a_{e}^{-1}a_{e_{1}}=a_{f}^{-1}a_{f_{1}};

\displaystyle\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}f&f_{1}\\
g&g_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)

is LR-singularised by

\displaystyle v=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,k&j&\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j&k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y},

yielding

\displaystyle a_{f}^{-1}a_{f_{1}}=a_{g}^{-1}a_{g_{1}};

\displaystyle\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&h\\
p&h_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)

is UD-singularised by

\displaystyle w=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j,k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j,k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y},

yielding

\displaystyle a_{e}^{-1}a_{h}=a_{p}^{-1}a_{h_{1}}.

These squares are illustrated in Figure
17
. Using Lemma
8.17
and
i\leq j+1
, we see that the full-domain idempotents
g
and
g_{1}
have equal labels, and hence we have

(10.19)

a_{g}=a_{g_{1}}

by Lemma
10.6
. Analogously, Lemma
10.7
gives

(10.20)

a_{h}=a_{h_{1}}.

Finally, by Lemma
10.10
we have

(10.21)

a_{e_{1}}=a_{q}.

Putting all this together we obtain

a_{p}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\eqref{eq:P1pq13}}}}{{=}}a_{h_{1}}a_{h}^{-1}a_{e}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\eqref{eq:P1pq15}}}}{{=}}a_{e}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\eqref{eq:P1pq11}}}}{{=}}a_{e_{1}}a_{f_{1}}^{-1}a_{f}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\eqref{eq:P1pq16}}}}{{=}}a_{q}a_{f_{1}}^{-1}a_{f}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\eqref{eq:P1pq12}}}}{{=}}a_{q}a_{g_{1}}^{-1}a_{g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\eqref{eq:P1pq14}}}}{{=}}a_{q},

as required.
∎

.

) of the generators corresponding to these idempotents, which itself follows from Lemma

.

Figure 18:
Stratified egg-box diagrams of the idempotents in the singular square(s) featuring in the proofs of Lemmas

Our next lemma establishes equalities between generators corresponding to natural families of projections.
Still supposing
r=n-2
, for any
i,j\in[n]
with
i<j
denote by
\textup{{p}}(i,j)
the unique projection in
P_{1}=P_{1}(n,n-2)
with the upper block
\{i,j\}
.

Lemma 10.23
.

Suppose
1\leq r=n-2
, and let
i,j\in[n]
with
i<j
.

(i)

If
i+1<j
then
a_{\textup{{p}}(i,j)}=a_{\textup{{p}}(i+1,j)}
in
\widehat{G}(n,r)
.

(ii)

If
j<n
then
a_{\textup{{p}}(i,j)}=a_{\textup{{p}}(i,j+1)}
in
\widehat{G}(n,r)
.

Proof.

(i)

Let
q:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+1&j\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+1&j\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y}
, with
Y:=[n]\setminus\{i,i+1,j\}
.
Applying Lemma
10.12
, with the role of the triple
(i,j,k)
played by
(i+1,i,j)
, yields the relation
a_{\textup{{p}}(i,j)}=a_{q}
.
Likewise, the same lemma, but with the role of
(i,j,k)
played by
(i,i+1,j)
, yields

a_{\textup{{p}}(i+1,j)}=a_{q}
. Combining these two relations yields
a_{\textup{{p}}(i,j)}=a_{\textup{{p}}(i+1,j)}
.

(ii)

Analogous reasoning to part
(i)
, with
q:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil j,j+1&i\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil j,j+1&i\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y}
for
Y:=[n]\setminus\{i,j,j+1\}
, yields
a_{\textup{{p}}(i,j)}=a_{q}=a_{\textup{{p}}(i,j+1)}
.
∎

We can now complete our objective for
r=n-2
:

Lemma 10.24
.

Suppose
1\leq r=n-2
.
For arbitrary
p,q\in P_{1}(n,r)
,
we have
a_{p}=a_{q}
in
\widehat{G}(n,r)
.

Proof.

Any projection in
P_{1}
satisfies precisely one of the following:
either (1) all its transversals have size
2
; or else (2) there exists a single transversal of size
4
.
By Lemma
10.12
every generator corresponding to a projection satisfying (2) is equal to a generator corresponding to a projection satisfying (1). So we may assume without loss of generality that both
p
and
q
satisfy (1).
We can therefore write
p=\textup{{p}}(i,j)
for some
i<j
.
By Lemma
10.23
we have

a_{p}=a_{\textup{{p}}(i,j)}=a_{\textup{{p}}(i,j+1)}=\cdots=a_{\textup{{p}}(i,n)}=a_{\textup{{p}}(i+1,n)}=\cdots=a_{\textup{{p}}(n-1,n)}.

Analogously,
a_{q}=a_{\textup{{p}}(n-1,n)}
, and hence
a_{p}=a_{q}
, as required.
∎

Now that we have dealt with
r=n-2
, we move on to the general case:

Lemma 10.25
.

Suppose
1\leq r\leq n-2
.
For arbitrary
p,q\in P_{1}(n,r)
,
we have
a_{p}=a_{q}
in
\widehat{G}(n,r)
.

Proof.

We fix
r\geq 1
, and prove the lemma by induction on
n\geq r+2
. When
n=r+2
, the result is true by Lemma
10.24
, so for the rest of the proof we assume that
n\geq r+3
.

For
i,j\in[n]
with
i\neq j
denote by
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{i,j}
the set of all partitions
a\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
such that
(i,j)\in\ker(a)

and
(i,j)\in\coker(a)
.
This is a subsemigroup of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
isomorphic to
\mathcal{P}_{n-1}
.

Recall that the group
\widehat{G}(n,r)
is defined by the generators
A(n,r)
and the defining relations

\mathcal{R}_{1}(O(n,r))
and
\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}(n,r)
.
Furthermore, recall that
O(n,r)
consists of all non-projection idempotents in
D(n,r)
with full domain or full codomain, and with label
1
. In particular,
O(n,r)
contains all the idempotents belonging to
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{i,j}
with the above properties.
Likewise,
\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}(n,r)
contains all the relations arising from singular squares that lie entirely in
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{i,j}
.
In this way, we see that the presentation
\langle A(n,r)\mid\mathcal{R}_{1}(O(n,r)),\ \mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}(n,r)\rangle
contains as a sub-presentation a natural copy of the presentation

\langle A(n-1,r)\mid\mathcal{R}_{1}(O(n-1,r)),\ \mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}(n-1,r)\rangle
corresponding to
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{i,j}
.
Thus, if the projections
p
and
q
belong to some common
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{i,j}
we conclude that
a_{p}=a_{q}
in
\widehat{G}(n,r)
by induction.

Now consider arbitrary
p,q\in P_{1}
, and fix
(i,j)\in\ker(p)
and
(k,l)\in\ker(q)
with
i\neq j
and
k\neq l
.
Note that all elements of
P_{1}
have non-trivial kernel, and also that we do not require
\{i,j\}
and
\{k,l\}
to be disjoint.
Since
r\leq n-3
there exists
u\in P_{1}
such that
(i,j),(k,l)\in\ker(u)
.
But then
p,u\in\mathcal{P}_{n}^{i,j}
and
u,q\in\mathcal{P}_{n}^{k,l}
. Using the observation from the previous paragraph we have

a_{p}=a_{u}=a_{q}
, completing the proof.
∎

Remark 10.26
.

The fact that we were working with the groups
\widehat{G}(n,r)
, which do not depend on the choice of projection
s\in P_{0}
, is crucial for the validity of the inductive argument in our proof of Lemma
10.25
. Indeed, if we worked with the set
O\cup\{s\}
, we would not be able to perform the inductive step, since
s
certainly would not be contained in all
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{i,j}
. The same is true of the proof of Lemma
10.37
below.

10.4
Generators corresponding to projections from
P_{0}(n,r)

We now want to do the same for generators corresponding to projections from
P_{0}(n,r)
, i.e. show they are all equal to each other as a consequence of the defining relations. We will again need to work within the group
\widehat{G}=\widehat{G}(n,r)=\sfG(O)
; however, once the relation
a_{s}=1
is brought back in (for a fixed projection
s\in P_{0}(n,r)
), the conclusion will be that all these generators are in fact equal to
1
. As before, we begin with the case
r=n-2
.
If
n=3
(and
r=1=n-2
) then
|P_{0}|=1
, and Lemmas
10.27
,
10.35
and
10.36
are vacuously true in this case.

Lemma 10.27
.

Suppose
1\leq r=n-2
, let
i,j,k,l\in[n]
be distinct, let
Y:=[n]\setminus\{i,j,k,l\}
, and let

p:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j,k&l\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j,k&l\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptq:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j&k,l\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j&k,l\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y}.

If
i,l\leq k+1
then
a_{p}=a_{q}
in
\widehat{G}(n,r)
.

Proof.

Consider the following further idempotents:

\displaystyle e

\displaystyle:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j&k&l\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,k&\lx@intercol\hfil j\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol&l\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y},

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j&k&l\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&\lx@intercol\hfil j\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol&k,l\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y},

\displaystyle g_{1}

\displaystyle:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j&k&l\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j,k&&l\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y},

\displaystyle h_{1}

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j&k&l\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j&&k,l\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y},

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j&k&l\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,k&j&l\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y},

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j&k&l\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j&k,l\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y},

\displaystyle g_{2}

\displaystyle:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j,k&&l\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,k&j&l\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y},

\displaystyle h_{2}

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j&&k,l\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j&k,l\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y}.

Then:

\displaystyle\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)

is LR-singularised by

\displaystyle u=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j&k&l\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j&&k,l\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y},

yielding

\displaystyle a_{e}^{-1}a_{f}=a_{g}^{-1}a_{h};

\displaystyle\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}g&g_{1}\\
g_{2}&p\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)

is LR-singularised by

\displaystyle v=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j&k&l\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j&k&l\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y},

yielding

\displaystyle a_{g}^{-1}a_{g_{1}}=a_{g_{2}}^{-1}a_{p};

\displaystyle\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}h&h_{1}\\
h_{2}&q\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)

is LR-singularised by

\displaystyle w=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j&k&l\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j&k&l\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y},

yielding

\displaystyle a_{h}^{-1}a_{h_{1}}=a_{h_{2}}^{-1}a_{q}.

These squares are illustrated in Figure
19
.
By Lemma
10.10
, we have
a_{e}=a_{e^{\prime}}
, where

e^{\prime}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,k&j&l\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,k&j&l\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y}
,
and also

a_{f}=a_{f^{\prime}}
, where

f^{\prime}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j&k,l\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j&k,l\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y}
.
Note that
e^{\prime},f^{\prime}\in P_{1}
, so that
a_{e^{\prime}}=a_{f^{\prime}}
by Lemma
10.25
.
Hence
a_{e}=a_{f}
, and combining with (
10.4
) yields
a_{g}=a_{h}
.

Combining this last conclusion with (
10.4
) and (
10.4
) yields

a_{g_{1}}a_{p}^{-1}a_{g_{2}}=a_{g}=a_{h}=a_{h_{1}}a_{q}^{-1}a_{h_{2}}.

The desired relation
a_{p}=a_{q}
will follow if we manage to show that

a_{g_{1}}=a_{h_{1}}
and
a_{g_{2}}=a_{h_{2}}
.
In fact, by Lemmas
10.7
and
10.6
, it suffices to show that
\lambda(g_{i})=\lambda(h_{i})
for
i=1,2
.

Again writing
x\wedge y
for
\min(x,y)
, we have

\displaystyle\lambda^{\prime}(g_{1})

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}i\wedge j&l\\
i\wedge j\wedge k&l\end{smallmatrix}\right)\oplus\id_{Y},

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\lambda^{\prime}(g_{2})

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}i\wedge j\wedge k&l\\
i\wedge k&l\end{smallmatrix}\right)\oplus\id_{Y},

\displaystyle\lambda^{\prime}(h_{1})

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}i\wedge j&l\\
i\wedge j&k\wedge l\end{smallmatrix}\right)\oplus\id_{Y},

\displaystyle\lambda^{\prime}(h_{2})

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}i\wedge j&k\wedge l\\
i&k\wedge l\end{smallmatrix}\right)\oplus\id_{Y}.

Since
i\leq k+1
, the values
i\wedge j\wedge k
and
i\wedge j
are either equal or consecutive.
Likewise,
l
and
k\wedge l
are either equal or consecutive.
Furthermore, not both pairs can be consecutive, as this would imply that

i=k+1=l
.
It again follows from Lemma
8.17
that
\lambda(g_{1})=\lambda(h_{1})
.
We obtain
\lambda(g_{2})=\lambda(h_{2})
analogously, and this completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Still supposing
r=n-2
, for any
i,j,k\in[n]
with
i<j<k
denote by
\textup{{p}}(i,j,k)
the unique projection in
P_{0}
with transversal
\{i,j,k,i^{\prime},j^{\prime},k^{\prime}\}
.

Lemma 10.35
.

Suppose
1\leq r=n-2
, and let
i,j,k\in[n]
with
i<j<k
.

(i)

If
i+1<j
then
a_{\textup{{p}}(i,j,k)}=a_{\textup{{p}}(i+1,j,k)}
in
\widehat{G}(n,r)
.

(ii)

If
j+1<k
then
a_{\textup{{p}}(i,j,k)}=a_{\textup{{p}}(i,j+1,k)}
in
\widehat{G}(n,r)
.

(iii)

If
k<n
then
a_{\textup{{p}}(i,j,k)}=a_{\textup{{p}}(i,j,k+1)}
in
\widehat{G}(n,r)
.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to the proof of Lemma
10.23
, but using Lemma
10.27
instead of Lemma
10.12
.
Thus for
(i)
we let

q:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,k&i+1,j\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,k&i+1,j\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y}
,
where
Y:=[n]\setminus\{i,i+1,j,k\}
,
and then apply Lemma
10.27
twice to obtain
a_{\textup{{p}}(i,j,k)}=a_{q}=a_{\textup{{p}}(i+1,j,k)}
.
The intermediate partitions to use in proving
(ii)
and
(iii)

are

\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,k&j,j+1\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,k&j,j+1\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y}
and

\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j&k,k+1\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j&k,k+1\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus\id_{Y}

(for appropriate
Y
), respectively.
∎

Lemma 10.36
.

Suppose
1\leq r=n-2
. For arbitrary
p,q\in P_{0}(n,r)
, we have
a_{p}=a_{q}
in
\widehat{G}(n,r)
.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to the proof of Lemma
10.24
.
Lemma
10.27
enables us to assume without loss of generality that
p
and
q
have a transversal of size
6
.
We can therefore write
p=\textup{{p}}(i,j,k)
for some
i<j<k
.
By Lemma
10.35
we have

\displaystyle a_{p}=a_{\textup{{p}}(i,j,k)}=a_{\textup{{p}}(i,j,k+1)}=\cdots=a_{\textup{{p}}(i,j,n)}=a_{\textup{{p}}(i,j+1,n)}=\cdots

\displaystyle=a_{\textup{{p}}(i,n-1,n)}

\displaystyle=a_{\textup{{p}}(i+1,n-1,n)}=\cdots=a_{\textup{{p}}(n-2,n-1,n)}.

Analogously,
a_{q}=a_{\textup{{p}}(n-2,n-1,n)}
, and hence
a_{p}=a_{q}
.
∎

Lemma 10.37
.

Suppose
1\leq r\leq n-2
. For arbitrary
p,q\in P_{0}(n,r)
, we have
a_{p}=a_{q}
in
\widehat{G}(n,r)
.

Proof.

This is exactly the same as the proof of Lemma
10.25
, except that Lemma
10.36
is used in place of Lemma
10.24
when dealing with the case
r=n-2
.
∎

Thus, as soon as we add to
\widehat{G}(n,r)=\sfG(O)
a relation equating any generator corresponding to a projection from
P_{0}
to
1
, all such generators are equal to
1
:

Lemma 10.38
.

Suppose
1\leq r\leq n-2
, and let
s\in P_{0}(n,r)
be arbitrary. Then for every
p\in P_{0}(n,r)
we have
a_{p}=1
in
\sfG(O\cup\{s\})
. ∎

10.5
Coxeter idempotents

As our next step, we now move on to the generators
a_{e}
corresponding to full-domain/codomain Coxeter idempotents
e\in E(n,r)
, i.e. those for which
\lambda(e)=(i,i+1)
is a Coxeter transposition. The significance of these elements is that:

•

any generator of
G^{\sfE}
corresponding to a full-domain non-projection idempotent is a product of generators corresponding to full-domain Coxeter idempotents (see Lemmas
8.16
and
8.20
), and

•

the generators corresponding to full-domain Coxeter idempotents satisfy the defining (Coxeter) relations of
\mathcal{S}_{r}
(see
[
40
, Lemmas 8.1–8.3]
).

Together with the dual statements concerning full-
co
domain Coxeter idempotents (and additional arguments provided later in this section), it will follow that the full-domain and full-codomain idempotents lead to two copies of
\mathcal{S}_{r}
in our group
G^{\sfE}
. The next lemma is pivotal, and will be used to show that in fact these copies of
\mathcal{S}_{r}
coincide. We also bring the relation
a_{s}=1
back in, where
s
is our fixed projection from
P_{0}(n,r)
.

Lemma 10.39
.

Suppose
1\leq r\leq n-2
, and let
s\in P_{0}(n,r)
be arbitrary.
Let
e,f\in E(n,r)
be such that
e
is full-domain,
f
is full-codomain, and
\lambda(e)=\lambda(f)=(i,i+1)
for some
1\leq i\leq r-1
. Then
a_{e}=a_{f}
in
\sfG(O\cup\{s\})
.

Proof.

The result is vacuous for
r=1
, so we assume that
r\geq 2
, which also implies
n\geq 4
.
During the proof, for a partition
g\in\mathcal{P}_{[i,i+3]}
, we write
g^{+}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
for the following:

•

If
r\geq 3
, then
g^{+}=g\oplus\id_{\sigma}
, where
\sigma
is the equivalence on
[n]\setminus[i,i+3]
with classes

\{1\},\ldots,\{i-1\},\{i+4\},\ldots,\{r+1\},[r+2,n].

•

If
r=2
(and so
i=1
), then
g^{+}
is obtained by adding
i+4,\ldots,n
to the block of
g
containing
i+3
, and adding
(i+4)^{\prime},\ldots,n^{\prime}
to the block of
g
containing
(i+3)^{\prime}
.

With this notation in place, it follows from Lemmas
10.6
and
10.7
that we can prove the current lemma for the specific choices

e=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+2,i+3&&i+1\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i+2,i+3&i&i+1\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptf=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+2&i+3&i+1\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr&i,i+2,i+3&i+1\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+}.

We proceed via a series of claims as follows. Each involves certain idempotents
g_{i}
, whose meanings are fixed throughout. The elements constructed during the proofs are temporary, however, and we will re-use their names (
h_{i}
and
u
). Also,
t
will denote some arbitrary but fixed projection from
P_{1}=P_{1}(n,r)
.
The singular squares we use in the proofs of the claims are shown in Figure
21
. Some of these are instances of the square from Lemma
8.8
(or its dual), but for convenience we will provide a singularising element in each case.

g_{1}
