Claim 7

Figure 21:
The singular squares featuring in the proof of Lemma
10.39
.
In each partition, only the points
i,i+1,i+2,i+3
and their dashed counterparts are shown (left to right).

Claim 1
.

For

g_{1}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+2\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol&i+1&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&i+2&i+1&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+}

we have
a_{g_{1}}=a_{t}
.

Proof.

Consider the square
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}g_{1}&h_{1}\\
h_{2}&h_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
, where

h_{1}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+2&i+1&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+2&i+1&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+},\hskip 10.00002pth_{2}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+1,i+2&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i+1&i&i+2&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pth_{3}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+1,i+2&&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i+1&i,i+2&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+}.

It is easy to verify that it is LR-singularised by

u=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+2&i+1&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+2&i+1&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+}
.
As
h_{1}\in P_{1}
we have
a_{h_{1}}=a_{t}
by Lemma
10.25
.
The idempotents
h_{2}
and
h_{3}
have full domain and label
1
, and hence
a_{h_{2}}=a_{h_{3}}=1
by Lemma
10.6
.
The square relation
a_{g_{1}}^{-1}a_{h_{1}}=a_{h_{2}}^{-1}a_{h_{3}}
now implies

a_{g_{1}}=a_{h_{1}}=a_{t}
, as required.
∎

Claim 2
.

For

g_{2}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&i+1&i+2&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&i+1&&i+2,i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+}

we have
a_{g_{2}}=a_{t}
.

Proof.

The square
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}g_{2}&h_{1}\\
h_{2}&h_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
, where

h_{1}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&i+1&i+2&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr&i+1&&i,i+2,i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+},\hskip 10.00002pth_{2}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&i+1&i+2,i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&i+1&i+2,i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pth_{3}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&i+1&i+2,i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr&i+1&i,i+2,i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+}.

is LR-singularised by

u=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&i+1&i+2&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr&i+1&i,i+2&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+}
.
We have
a_{h_{2}}=a_{t}
by Lemma
10.25
since
h_{2}\in P_{1}
.
The idempotents
h_{1}
and
h_{3}
are full-codomain, and both have label
(i,i+1)
; hence
a_{h_{1}}=a_{h_{3}}
by Lemma
10.7
.
The square relation now implies

a_{g_{2}}=a_{h_{2}}=a_{t}
, as required.
∎

Claim 3
.

For

g_{3}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+1&\lx@intercol\hfil i+2\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i+1&i&i+2&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+}

we have
a_{g_{3}}=a_{t}
.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to the previous two claims.
The square is
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}g_{3}&h_{1}\\
h_{2}&h_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
, where

h_{1}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+1&i+2&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+1&i+2&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+},\hskip 10.00002pth_{2}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+1,i+2&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i+1&i&i+2&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pth_{3}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+1,i+2&&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+1&i+2&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+}.

The UD-singularising idempotent is

u=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&i+1,i+2&&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&i+1&i+2&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+}
.
This time
h_{1}
belongs to
P_{1}
, while
h_{2}
and
h_{3}
have full domain and label
1
.
∎

Claim 4
.

For

g_{4}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+1&i+2&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i+1&i&i+2,i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+}

we have
a_{g_{4}}=1
.

Proof.

This time the square is
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}g_{4}&h_{1}\\
h_{2}&h_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
, where

h_{1}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+1&i+2&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+1&&i+2,i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+},\hskip 10.00002pth_{2}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+1&&i+2,i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i+1&i&i+2,i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pth_{3}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+1&i+2,i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+1&i+2,i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+},

and the LR-singularising idempotent is

u=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+1&i+2&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+1&i+2&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+}
.
The idempotents
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
have full domain or codomain, and label
1
, so
a_{h_{1}}=a_{h_{2}}=1
.
The idempotent
h_{3}
is a projection from
P_{0}
, so
a_{h_{3}}=1
by Lemma
10.38
.
Thus, the square relation gives
a_{g_{4}}=1
.
∎

Claim 5
.

For

g_{5}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&i+1&i+2&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&i+1&i+2&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+}

we have
a_{g_{5}}=a_{t}^{2}
.

Proof.

This time we have the square
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}g_{5}&g_{2}\\
g_{3}&g_{4}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
,
where
g_{2}
,
g_{3}
and
g_{4}
were defined in Claims
2
–
4
.
The square is LR-singularised by

u=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&i+1&i+2&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&i+1&&i+2,i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+}
.
Substituting the relations obtained in Claims
2
–
4

into the square relation

a_{g_{5}}^{-1}a_{g_{2}}=a_{g_{3}}^{-1}a_{g_{4}}
yields
a_{g_{5}}=a_{t}^{2}
.
∎

Claim 6
.

For

g_{6}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+2&i+1&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&i+1&i+2,i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+}

we have
a_{g_{6}}=1
.

Proof.

This time we have the square
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}g_{6}&g_{1}\\
g_{2}&g_{5}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
,
where
g_{1}
,
g_{2}
and
g_{5}
were defined in Claims
1
,
2
and
5
.
The square is UD-singularised by

u=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&i+1&i+2&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&i+1&i+2&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+}
.
Substituting Claims
1
,
2
and
5

into the square relation

a_{g_{6}}^{-1}a_{g_{1}}=a_{g_{2}}^{-1}a_{g_{5}}
yields
a_{g_{6}}=1
.
∎

Claim 7
.

We have
a_{e}^{-1}=a_{f}
.

Proof.

This time we have the square
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e&h_{1}\\
g_{6}&f\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
,
where

h_{1}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+2,i+3&i+1\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+2,i+3&i+1\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+}
.
The square is LR-singularised by
u=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+2&i+1&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+2&i+1&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+}
.
Since
h_{1}\in P_{0}
we have
a_{h_{1}}=1
by Lemma
10.38
, and
also
a_{g_{6}}=1
by Claim
6
.
The desired relation follows from the square relation
a_{e}^{-1}a_{h_{1}}=a_{g_{6}}^{-1}a_{f}
.
∎

To complete the proof of the lemma,
let
e^{\prime}\in E(n,r)
be any idempotent full transformation with label
(i,i+1)
.
By Lemma
10.6
(i)
we have
a_{e}=a_{e^{\prime}}
. On the other hand, by Lemma
8.16
(iii)
we have
a_{e^{\prime}}^{2}=1
, and so
a_{e}^{2}=1
. Combining this with Claim
7
we deduce
a_{e}=a_{e}^{-1}=a_{f}
, as required.
∎

10.6
Commuting relations

We now start moving towards identifying the group
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathcal{S}_{r}
.
It will turn out that the generators
a_{e}
corresponding to full-domain or full-codomain idempotents
e
will generate the symmetric group
\mathcal{S}_{r}
, whereas those corresponding to
e\in P_{1}(n,r)
– which are mutually equal by Lemma
10.25
– will generate
\mathbb{Z}
. The key point in concluding that we indeed have a direct product is to establish commutation relations between the two families of generators.

Lemma 10.40
.

Suppose
1\leq r\leq n-2
, and let
s\in P_{0}(n,r)
be arbitrary. Let
e\in E(n,r)
be any idempotent with full domain and label
(i,i+1)
for some
1\leq i\leq r-1
, and let
t\in P_{1}(n,r)
. Then
a_{e}a_{t}=a_{t}a_{e}
in
\sfG(O\cup\{s\})
.

Proof.

Again the result is vacuous for
r=1
, so we assume that
r\geq 2
. We also use the
g^{+}
notation from the proof of Lemma
10.39
for idempotents
g
of
\mathcal{P}_{[i,i+3]}
.
By Lemma
10.6
it is sufficient to show that
a_{e}a_{t}=a_{t}a_{f}
holds for

e:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+3&&i+1,i+2\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i+3&i&i+1,i+2\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptf:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+2,i+3&&i+1&\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i+3&i&i+1&i+2\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+}.

Define some further idempotents:

\displaystyle e_{1}

\displaystyle:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+3&i+2&i+1\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i+3&i&i+1,i+2\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+},\hskip 10.00002pt

\displaystyle f_{1}

\displaystyle:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+3&&i+1&i+2\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i+3&i&i+1&i+2\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+},\hskip 10.00002pt

\displaystyle g_{1}

\displaystyle:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&i+1&i+2&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&i+1,i+2&&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+},

\displaystyle e_{2}

\displaystyle:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+3&i+1&i+2\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+3&i+1,i+2&\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+},\hskip 10.00002pt

\displaystyle f_{2}

\displaystyle:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+3&i+1&i+2\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+3&i+1&i+2\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+},\hskip 10.00002pt

\displaystyle g_{2}

\displaystyle:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&i+1&i+2&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&i+1&i+2&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+}.

\displaystyle e_{3}

\displaystyle:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+3&i+1,i+2\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+3&i+1,i+2\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+},\hskip 10.00002pt

\displaystyle f_{3}

\displaystyle:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+2,i+3&i+1&\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,i+3&i+1&i+2\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+},\hskip 10.00002pt

These idempotents participate in several singular squares, which will be exhibited below, and are illustrated in Figure
22
.

The square
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e_{1}&e_{2}\\
e&e_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
is UD-singularised by

u=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&i+1,i+2&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&i+1,i+2&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+}
.
In it,
e_{3}
is a projection from
P_{0}
, so
a_{e_{3}}=1
by Lemma
10.38
;
and
e_{2}
has full codomain and label
1
, so
a_{e_{2}}=1
by Lemma
10.7
.
Therefore, the square relation
a_{e_{1}}^{-1}a_{e_{2}}=a_{e}^{-1}a_{e_{3}}
implies

(10.41)

a_{e}=a_{e_{1}}.

The square
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}f_{1}&f_{2}\\
f&f_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
is UD-singularised
by

v=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&i+1&i+2,i+3&\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&i+1&i+3&i+2\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+}
.
Note that
f_{2}\in P_{1}
, so by Lemma
10.25
we have
a_{f_{2}}=a_{t}
;
and
f_{3}
has full domain and label
1
, so
a_{f_{3}}=1
by Lemma
10.6
.
Combining this with the square relation
a_{f_{1}}^{-1}a_{f_{2}}=a_{f}^{-1}a_{f_{3}}
yields

(10.42)

a_{t}a_{f}=a_{f_{1}}.

Now consider
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e_{1}&f_{1}\\
g_{1}&g_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
, which is LR-singularised by

w=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&i+1&i+2&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&i+1&i+2&i+3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)^{\!\!+}
.
In Claim
5
from the proof of Lemma
10.39
we showed that
a_{g_{2}}=a_{t}^{2}
.
Also, we have
a_{g_{1}}=a_{t}
, which is shown analogously to Claim
3
in the proof of Lemma
10.39
. Consequently,
a_{g_{1}}^{-1}a_{g_{2}}=a_{t}
.
Combining this with the square relation
a_{e_{1}}^{-1}a_{f_{1}}=a_{g_{1}}^{-1}a_{g_{2}}
,
and with (
10.41
) and (
10.42
), we have

a_{e}a_{t}=a_{e}a_{g_{1}}^{-1}a_{g_{2}}=a_{e}a_{e_{1}}^{-1}a_{f_{1}}=a_{t}a_{f},

completing the proof.
∎

Figure 22:
The singular squares featuring in the proof of Lemma
10.40
.
In each partition, only the points

and their dashed counterparts are shown (left to right).

Corollary 10.43
.

Let
e\in E(n,r)
be any non-projection idempotent with full domain, and let
t\in P_{1}
. Then
a_{t}a_{e}=a_{e}a_{t}
in
\sfG(O\cup\{s\})
.

Proof.

By Lemma
8.20
, we may assume without loss of generality that
e\in\mathcal{T}_{n}
.
Since
O\cup\{s\}
contains
T_{\textup{lex}}
,
it follows from Lemma
8.16
(iv)
that
a_{e}=a_{e_{1}}\cdots a_{e_{k}}
, with each
e_{i}
a Coxeter idempotent.
The result now follows from Lemma
10.40
.
∎

10.7
The twisted partition monoid
\mathcal{P}^{\Phi}_{n}

As we will soon see, the results obtained so far will allow us to conclude that our group
G^{\sfE}
is a homomorphic image of
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathcal{S}_{r}
. We now provide a means to show that, conversely,
G^{\sfE}
is also a homomorphic
preimage
of
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathcal{S}_{r}
.

Consider partitions
a,b\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, and the product graph
\Gamma(a,b)
. A component of this graph is called
floating
if it is contained entirely within
[n]^{\prime\prime}
, the middle row of vertices. We let
\Phi(a,b)
denote the number of such floating components. For example, with
a,b\in\mathcal{P}_{6}
from Figure
2
, we have
\Phi(a,b)=1
, with the unique floating component of
\Gamma(a,b)
being
\{1^{\prime\prime},2^{\prime\prime},6^{\prime\prime}\}
.

The (
\mathbb{Z}
-)
twisted partition monoid

\mathcal{P}^{\Phi}_{n}
has underlying set
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathcal{P}_{n}
, and product

(i,a)(j,b)=(i+j+\Phi(a,b),ab)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $i,j\in\mathbb{Z}$ and $a,b\in\mathcal{P}_{n}$.}

The monoid
\mathcal{P}^{\Phi}_{n}
is a special case of a
tight twisted product
, as studied in
[
23
]
. The analogous twisted product with underlying set
\mathbb{N}\times\mathcal{P}_{n}
is more commonly studied; see
[
28
,
27
,
31
,
62
,
4
,
58
,
2
]
. The following is a list of facts about
\mathcal{P}^{\Phi}_{n}
, which are consequences of the stated results from
[
23
]
:

•

\mathcal{P}^{\Phi}_{n}
is regular
[
23
, Theorem 7.4]
.

•

The
\mathscr{D}
-classes of
\mathcal{P}^{\Phi}_{n}
are the sets
\mathbb{Z}\times D(n,r)
, for
0\leq r\leq n
, and every maximal subgroup contained in
\mathbb{Z}\times D(n,r)
is isomorphic to the direct product
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathcal{S}_{r}

[
23
, Corollary 5.2 and Theorem 6.2]
.

•

Writing
\overline{e}=(-\Phi(e,e),e)
for
e\in\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n})
, we have
\sfE(\mathcal{P}^{\Phi}_{n})=\{\overline{e}\colon e\in\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n})\}

[
23
, Proposition 6.4 and Remark 6.5]
.

•

The map
e\mapsto\overline{e}
determines an isomorphism of biordered sets
\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n})\to\sfE(\mathcal{P}^{\Phi}_{n})

[
23
, Corollary 6.15 and Remark 6.16]
.

•

The idempotent-generated submonoid
\langle\sfE(\mathcal{P}^{\Phi}_{n})\rangle
consists of the identity element
(0,1)
, and all
\mathscr{D}
-classes
\mathbb{Z}\times D(n,r)
for
0\leq r\leq n-1

[
23
, Theorem 8.7]
.

Consequently, we have a surmorphism
\IG(\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n}))\cong\IG(\sfE(\mathcal{P}^{\Phi}_{n}))\to\langle\sfE(\mathcal{P}^{\Phi}_{n})\rangle
given by
{x_{e}\mapsto\overline{e}}
for
e\in\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n})
.
Moreover, for any fixed
e_{0}\in\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n})
of rank
0\leq r\leq n-1
, this surmorphism restricts to a surmorphism from
G^{\sfE}
, the maximal subgroup of
\IG(\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
containing
x_{e_{0}}
, to the maximal subgroup of
\mathcal{P}^{\Phi}_{n}
containing
\overline{e}_{0}
, which is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathcal{S}_{r}
. We record this final conclusion here for future reference, and we note that this includes the case
r=0
.

Proposition 10.44
.

For any
0\leq r\leq n-1
, the group
G^{\sfE}
is a homomorphic preimage of
{\mathbb{Z}\times\mathcal{S}_{r}}
.
∎

10.8
Completing the proof

Recall from Lemma
10.8
that our maximal subgroup
G^{\sfE}\cong\sfG(O\cup\{s\})
is defined by the presentation

\presn(O\cup\{s\})=\bigl\langle A(n,r)\mid\mathcal{R}_{1}(O\cup\{s\}),\ \mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}(n,r)\bigr\rangle,

where
s
is a fixed projection from
P_{0}(n,r)
.

Fix any
t\in P_{1}
, and define

E^{\prime}:=E(n,r)\cap\mathcal{T}_{n}=\{e\in E(n,r)\colon\NTu(e)=0,\ \NTd(e)=n-r\}.

Let
\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}^{\prime}(n,r)
be the set of square relations in

\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}(n,r)
that
involve only idempotents from
E^{\prime}
, and let
\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}^{\prime\prime}(n,r):=\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}(n,r)\setminus\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}^{\prime}(n,r)
.
Adding the commuting relations established in Corollary
10.43
for
e\in E^{\prime}
to the presentation
\presn(O\cup\{s\})
yields the equivalent presentation:

(10.45)

\bigl\langle A(n,r)\mid\mathcal{R}_{1}(O\cup\{s\}),\ \mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}^{\prime}(n,r),\ \mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}^{\prime\prime}(n,r),\ a_{e}a_{t}=a_{t}a_{e}\ (e\in E^{\prime})\bigr\rangle.

Now let
A^{\prime}(n,r):=\big\{a_{e}\colon e\in E^{\prime}\cup\{t\}\big\}
. By Lemma
8.14
, we have
G^{\sfE}=\langle A_{F}\rangle
, where
F=F(n,r)
is defined in (
8.11
), and we again decompose
F=P_{0}\cup P_{1}\cup F_{1}\cup F_{2}
, where
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are as in (
9.2
). By Lemmas
10.25
and
10.38
, we have
G^{\sfE}=\langle A_{F_{1}\cup F_{2}\cup\{t\}}\rangle
. Next, by Lemma
8.20
and its dual, we obtain
G^{\sfE}=\langle A_{E^{\prime}\cup E^{\prime\prime}\cup\{t\}}\rangle
, where
E^{\prime\prime}:=\{e^{*}\colon e\in E^{\prime}\}
. Combining the dual of Lemma
8.16
(iv)
with Lemma
10.39
, each letter from
A_{E^{\prime\prime}}
is a product of letters from
A_{E^{\prime}}
. So we conclude that
G^{\sfE}=\langle A_{E^{\prime}\cup\{t\}}\rangle=\langle A^{\prime}(n,r)\rangle
.
Thus, we can eliminate all the generators from
A(n,r)\setminus A^{\prime}(n,r)
in the presentation (
10.45
).
Notice that the relations
\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}^{\prime}(n,r)
are unaffected by this elimination, since they involve only generators from
A_{E^{\prime}}\subseteq A^{\prime}(n,r)
.

Recall that
O
contains the tree
T_{\textup{lex}}
, and so

\mathcal{R}_{1}(O\cup\{s\})
contains
\mathcal{R}_{1}(T_{\textup{lex}})
.
Hence we now see that
G^{\sfE}
is defined by the presentation

\langle A^{\prime}(n,r)\mid\mathcal{R}_{1}(T_{\textup{lex}}),\ \mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}^{\prime}(n,r),\ a_{e}a_{t}=a_{t}a_{e}\ (e\in E^{\prime}),\ \mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime\prime}\rangle

for some set of relations
\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime\prime}
.
By Proposition
5.4
, the presentation

\langle A^{\prime}(n,r)\setminus\{t\}\mid\mathcal{R}_{1}(T_{\textup{lex}}),\ \mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}^{\prime}(n,r)\rangle

defines the symmetric group
\mathcal{S}_{r}
.
Therefore
G^{\sfE}
is a homomorphic image of
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathcal{S}_{r}
.
But by Proposition
10.44
,
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathcal{S}_{r}
is also a homomorphic image of
G^{\sfE}
. Since
\mathbb{Z}
is abelian and Hopfian, and
\mathcal{S}_{r}
is finite, it follows by
[
47
, Corollary, p233]
that
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathcal{S}_{r}
is Hopfian. Therefore
G^{\sfE}\cong\mathbb{Z}\times\mathcal{S}_{r}
, as required.

11
Maximal subgroups in
\IG(\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
, the case of rank
0

Having completed the proof of Theorem
1.2
for
1\leq r\leq n-2
, we now deal with the final case, where
r=0
. Here we cannot ‘reduce to
\mathcal{T}_{n}
’, as
D(n,0)
consists entirely of partitions of rank
0
, and hence contains no transformations at all.

Proposition 11.1
.

Let
n\geq 2
, and let
e_{0}
be an idempotent of rank
0
in
\mathcal{P}_{n}
.
Then the maximal subgroup of
\IG(\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
containing
x_{e_{0}}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

The proof is in many ways similar to, yet much easier than, the proof for the
r\geq 1
case, but with a couple of variations. We cover it rapidly, but deal with the differences in full detail.

Note that
all
the elements of
D(n,0)
are idempotents, so we can pick a very straightforward spanning tree for
\GH(D(n,0))
:

T=T(n,0):=\big\{e\in E(n,0)\colon\ker(e)=\nabla_{[n]}\text{ or }\coker(e)=\nabla_{[n]}\big\}.

We remark that every element of
E(n,0)
must have at least one upper block and at least one lower block. Those that attain at least one of those lower bounds are precisely the elements of
T
.

By Theorem
4.13
, the maximal subgroup
G^{\sfE}
is isomorphic to
\sfG(T)
,
i.e. it is defined by the presentation

\presn(T)=\big\langle A(n,0)\colon\mathcal{R}_{1}(T),\ \mathcal{R}_{\textsf{sq}}(n,0)\big\rangle.

We can eliminate the majority of the generators in
A(n,r)
by using Lemma
8.8
, and the following analogue of Lemma
8.10
concerning NT-reducing squares (cf. Definition
8.5
).

Claim 1
.

If
p=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A&B&C\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A&B&C\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus q\in P(n,0)
, then
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e_{1}&e_{2}\\
e_{3}&p\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
is an NT-reducing square with base
p
, where

e_{1}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A\cup C&B\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A\cup B&C\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus q,\hskip 10.00002pte_{2}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }\lx@intercol\hfil A\cup C\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol&B\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A&B&C\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus q\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pte_{3}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A&B&C\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr\lx@intercol\hfil A\cup B\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol&C\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus q.

Proof.

An RL-singularising element is
u=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A&B&C\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A\cup B&&C\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\oplus q
; see Figure
23
.
∎

A\cup C

Figure 23:
The NT-reducing singular square featuring in Claim
1
from the proof of Proposition
11.1
; in each partition, the ‘
{}\oplus q
’ part has been omitted.

We now define
F=F(n,0)=T\cup P_{2}
, but we note that this is different from the set denoted
F
in Section
10
.

Claim 2
.

Every element of
E(n,0)\setminus F(n,0)
is the base of an NT-reducing singular square.

Proof.

Let
e\in E(n,0)\setminus F(n,0)
. If
e
is a projection, then Claim
1
applies. So now suppose
e
is not a projection, and assume without loss of generality that
\ker(e)\not\subseteq\coker(e)
. Since
e
does not belong to
T
, it has at least two upper blocks, say
A
and
B
. Now let
f\in E(n,0)
be obtained from
e
by merging these into the single block
A\cup B
. Then
e\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}f
(cf. Lemma
2.10
) and
\ker(e)\subsetneq\ker(f)
. Thus, Lemma
8.8
applies.
∎

An inductive argument as in Lemma
8.14
now gives
G^{\sfE}=\langle A_{F}\rangle
. Recalling that
a_{e}=1
for
e\in T
, it follows that all the generators from
A(n,0)
can be eliminated except those in
A_{P_{2}}=\{a_{e}\colon e\in P_{2}\}
.

Claim 3
.

If
p,q\in P_{2}(n,0)
then
a_{p}=a_{q}
in
\sfG(T)
.

Proof.

Analogously to Lemma
10.25
, we prove this by induction on
n\geq 2(=r+2)
.
The first case is
n=2
, when
|P_{2}|=1
, and there is nothing to prove.
However, unlike the proof of Lemma
10.25
, here we must also check the case
n=3
, which we do below. The singular squares used in the proof are illustrated in Figure
24
. Although each square is singular by Lemma
8.8
or Claim
1
, we still picture a singularising element for convenience.

For distinct
i,j\in[3]
, define

\textup{{p}}(i,j):=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j&k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j&k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
,
where
\{i,j,k\}=[3]
.
Then
\textup{{p}}(i,j)=\textup{{p}}(j,i)
and
P_{2}=\{\textup{{p}}(1,2),\textup{{p}}(1,3),\textup{{p}}(2,3)\}
.

By Lemma
8.8
we have a singular square
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}f_{1}&f_{2}\\
\textup{{p}}(i,j)&e_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
, where

f_{1}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }\lx@intercol\hfil i,j,k\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j&k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr),\hskip 10.00002ptf_{2}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }\lx@intercol\hfil i,j,k\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j&k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pte_{1}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }\lx@intercol\hfil i,j\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol&k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j&k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr).

Furthermore
f_{1},f_{2}\in T
, so
a_{f_{1}}=a_{f_{2}}=1
. The square relation now yields

(11.2)

a_{\textup{{p}}(i,j)}=a_{e_{1}}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ for }e_{1}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }\lx@intercol\hfil i,j\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol&k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j&k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr).

A dual argument shows that

(11.3)

a_{\textup{{p}}(j,k)}=a_{e_{2}}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ for }e_{2}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j&k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&\lx@intercol\hfil j,k\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr).

Lemma
8.8
again gives us a singular square
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}g_{1}&g_{2}\\
g_{3}&e_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
, where

g_{1}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j,k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j,k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr),\hskip 10.00002ptg_{2}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }\lx@intercol\hfil i,j,k\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j,k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr),\hskip 10.00002ptg_{3}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j&k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr\lx@intercol\hfil i,j,k\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pte_{3}:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j&k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j,k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr).

Since
g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}\in T
, we have
a_{g_{1}}=a_{g_{2}}=a_{g_{3}}=1
, and the square relation yields

(11.4)

a_{e_{3}}=1\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ for }e_{3}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i,j&k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j,k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr).

Finally, let
z:=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j&k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil i&j&k\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&2&3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&2&3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
.
The square
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e_{3}&e_{1}\\
e_{2}&z\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
is singular by Claim
1
.
Substituting (
11.2
)–(
11.4
) into the resulting square relation
a_{e_{3}}^{-1}a_{e_{1}}=a_{e_{2}}^{-1}a_{z}
yields

a_{\textup{{p}}(j,k)}a_{\textup{{p}}(i,j)}=a_{z}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ for }\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}.

Using this twice we have

a_{\textup{{p}}(j,k)}a_{\textup{{p}}(i,j)}=a_{z}=a_{\textup{{p}}(k,j)}a_{\textup{{p}}(i,k)}=a_{\textup{{p}}(j,k)}a_{\textup{{p}}(i,k)},

from which it follows that
a_{\textup{{p}}(i,j)}=a_{\textup{{p}}(i,k)}
if
\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}
.
And now

a_{\textup{{p}}(1,2)}=a_{\textup{{p}}(1,3)}=a_{\textup{{p}}(3,1)}=a_{\textup{{p}}(3,2)}=a_{\textup{{p}}(2,3)}.

This completes the proof of Claim
3
when
n=3
.

For
n\geq 4
the inductive step now proceeds as in the proof of Lemma
10.25
.
Let
p,q\in P_{2}
be arbitrary.
Recall the copies
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{i,j}
of
\mathcal{P}_{n-1}
in
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, for arbitrary distinct
i,j\in[n]
.
Note that our spanning tree
T(n,0)
contains a natural copy of the tree
T(n-1,0)
corresponding to each
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{i,j}
.
Thus, if
p
and
q
belong to the same
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{i,j}
we obtain
a_{p}=a_{q}
by induction.
Otherwise, we let
(i,j)\in\ker(p)
and
(k,l)\in\ker(q)
be arbitrary with
i\neq j
and
k\neq l
.
Since
n\geq 4
, there exists
{u\in P_{2}}
such that
(i,j),(k,l)\in\ker(u)
.
But then
a_{p}=a_{u}=a_{q}
, and Claim
3
is proved.
∎

.

.

We can now complete the proof of the proposition.
We have seen that we can eliminate all the generators, except for a single

, for

.
Thus
G^{\sfE}\cong\sfG(T)
is cyclic. But
G^{\sfE}
is infinite by Proposition

, so it follows that indeed
G^{\sfE}\cong\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

Remark 11.5
.

Figure
25
shows the location in
D(3,0)
of the idempotents from the singular squares
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}f_{1}&f_{2}\\
\textup{{p}}(i,j)&e_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
,
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}g_{1}&g_{2}\\
g_{3}&e_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
and
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}e_{3}&e_{1}\\
e_{2}&z\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
used in the proof of Claim
3
in Proposition
11.1
. Note that since
r=0
, the
\NTu
and
\NTd
parameters range from
1
to
3
. Note also that the strip
D^{1}(3,0)
is a single
\mathscr{R}
-class, and
D_{1}(3,0)
is a single
\mathscr{L}
-class; the unions of these strips is the spanning tree
T(3,0)
.

Stratified egg-box diagram of the idempotents in the singular squares featuring in the proof of Claim

. See Remark

12
Further applications, concluding remarks and open problems

Now that we have completed the proofs of our results, we once again take a broader view, and discuss some implications. In particular we suggest some natural questions, the answers to which would advance our understanding of the free objects that we have been considering –
\PG(P)
,
\IG(E)
and
\RIG(E)
– and their mutual relationships.

For a projection-generated regular
*
-semigroup
S
with
P=\sfP(S)
,
we saw in Subsection
2.8
that the Green’s
\mathscr{R}
-,
\mathscr{L}
- and
\mathscr{D}
-structure of
\PG(P)
is the same as the corresponding structure for
S
.
So, once we also understand the maximal subgroups of
\PG(P)
, its structure is completely determined.
Theorem
1.1
and Corollary
6.45
complete this programme when
S
is the partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}
.
These results can be interpreted as saying that
\PG(P)
is ‘almost equal’ to
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, in the
sense that the former
is obtained from the latter by only changing groups in the top two
\mathscr{D}
-classes
(to trivial or free as appropriate), and leaving the rest of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
intact.
From this
it follows quickly that
\PG(P)
has a solvable word problem (see
[
81
, Remark 4.5]
),
and in fact even admits a presentation by a finite complete rewriting system (using
[
39
, Theorem 1]
), and also that it is residually finite (using
[
35
]
).

We hope that the results of this paper will initiate a systematic study of maximal subgroups of the semigroups
\PG(P)
where
P
is the projection algebra of other natural regular
*
-semigroups. We recall that for the Temperley–Lieb monoid we have

\PG(\sfP(\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}))\cong\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}

[
26
, Theorem 9.1]
, but the following are open:

Problem 12.1
.

Determine the maximal subgroups of the semigroups
\PG(\sfP(S))
, where
S
is a diagram monoid such as the Brauer monoid
\mathcal{B}_{n}
or Motzkin monoid
\mathcal{M}_{n}
.

One difficulty that immediately presents itself in the case of
\mathcal{B}_{n}
is that there is no natural copy of
\mathcal{T}_{n}
to work towards, as we did in this paper.
As for
\mathcal{M}_{n}
, it is known that all of its maximal subgroups are trivial.
However in
[
26
, Example 10.12]
it is shown that the maximal subgroups of

\PG(\sfP(\mathcal{M}_{4}))
corresponding to idempotents of rank
1
are infinite.

Like
\PG(\sfP(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
, the free idempotent-generated regular semigroup
\RIG(\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n}))

is entirely determined by its maximal subgroups.
The latter are identical to those of
\IG(\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
by
(RIG3)
, and hence determined by Theorem
1.2
and Remark
4.15
.
Thus, as above for
\PG(\sfP(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
, we quickly deduce that

\RIG(\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
has a solvable word problem, a finite complete rewriting system and is residually finite.
So, in the same way as
\PG(\sfP(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
was ‘almost equal’ to
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, here
\RIG(\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
is ‘almost equal’ to
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\Phi}
.
This seems quite remarkable, in that it implies that the twisting map
\Phi
, which counts
the number of floating components in the product graphs of pairs of partitions, is somehow intrinsically ‘encoded’ in the idempotent structure of the partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, whose multiplication expressly ‘forgets’ this information.
We believe that it would be enlightening to find an intuitive explanation/interpretation of this observation.

For the free idempotent-generated semigroup

\IG(\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
the knowledge of its maximal subgroups only entails precise understanding of
the structure of its regular
\mathscr{D}
-classes.
As already pointed out,
\IG(\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
does have non-regular
\mathscr{D}
-classes, and therefore its overall structure remains opaque.
In particular, it does not readily follow that it possesses any of the above nice properties.

Problem 12.2
.

Does
\IG(\sfE(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
have a solvable word problem?
Does it have a finite complete rewriting system? Is it residually finite?

The word problem in free idempotent-generated semigroups in general is the subject of
[
17
,
10
,
12
]
, but none of the results there give an immediate answer to the above questions.

On a more general level, we may ask which groups arise as maximal subgroups of different free objects.
For example, it is known that every group arises as a maximal subgroup of some
\IG(E)

[
40
,
18
,
87
]

and hence also of some
\RIG(E)
.

Problem 12.3
.

Is it true that every group is the maximal subgroup of some free projection-generated regular
*
-semigroup
\PG(P)
?

None of the constructions deployed in
[
40
,
18
,
87
]
yield regular
*
-semigroups, so the following seems of interest:

Problem 12.4
.

Which groups arise as maximal subgroups of
\IG(E)
, where
E
is the biordered set of a regular
*
-semigroup?

Finally, one could consider maximal subgroups of
\PG(P)
and
\IG(E)
simultaneously:

Problem 12.5
.

Which pairs of groups arise as maximal subgroups of
\PG(P)
and
\IG(E)
arising from the same regular
*
-semigroup, and corresponding to the same idempotent?

From general theory, we know that the maximal subgroups of
\PG(P)
are quotients of their

\IG(E)
counterparts, but at this stage it is not clear to us whether there may be further constraints.
